Bloch waves, band edges and effective mass

Condensed matter and band theory

Derive an electronic band from both weak and strong binding, then use its slope and curvature to predict motion.

From first principles

Why translating a lattice state produces an energy band

In a perfect periodic chain the Hamiltonian is unchanged when the site labels are shifted by one. A wave may therefore have site amplitudes of the form \(c_j=C e^{ikja}\), with \(k\) defined up to \(2\pi/a\). To give that phase a physical energy, choose one localized orbital per cell of spacing \(a\). Assume an orthonormal basis, on-site energy \(\epsilon_0\), and a real hopping matrix element \(-t\) to either nearest neighbour. Projecting the stationary Schrödinger equation on orbital \(j\) gives

\[E c_j=\epsilon_0 c_j-t(c_{j-1}+c_{j+1}).\]

Successive amplitudes differ only by \(e^{\pm ika}\). Cancel \(c_j\), then combine these phases into a cosine:

\[E(k)=\epsilon_0-t(e^{ika}+e^{-ika})=\epsilon_0-2t\cos(ka).\]

For \(t>0\) the in-phase combination has the lowest energy. Comparing \(k=0\) and \(k=\pi/a\) gives bandwidth \(4t\). Form a narrow wave packet from neighbouring \(k\)'s. Its envelope speed is

\[d(E/\hbar)/dk=(2ta/\hbar)\sin(ka).\]

Translation symmetry determines the allowed phase labels, while the coupling strength determines the numerical dispersion. A real crystal can require several orbitals and longer-range couplings.

Background · Band velocity
\[\mathbf v_n(\mathbf k)=\hbar^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf k} E_n\]

This is the group velocity of a band wave packet in the ordinary semiclassical approximation. The Fermi surface determines which states can respond at low temperature.

Derivation and applications

Use band curvature to predict acceleration off the force axis

Near a smooth, isolated band minimum the first derivative of energy vanishes. In a low-symmetry crystal its second derivatives can depend on direction. Choose principal axes \(x,y\) in the plane near the minimum \(\mathbf k_0\), and write \(\mathbf q=\mathbf k-\mathbf k_0\). Taylor expansion defines two positive effective masses through the curvatures,

\[E(\mathbf k_0+\mathbf q)\simeq E_0+\frac{\hbar^2 q_x^2}{2m_x}+\frac{\hbar^2 q_y^2}{2m_y}.\]

Treat a narrow wave packet semiclassically in a uniform force \(\mathbf F\), over a time short enough for scattering to be negligible. Neglect interband transitions and any anomalous velocity. Differentiating energy component by component gives group velocity. Combining this with the crystal-momentum equation gives its acceleration,

\[v_i=\frac{1}{\hbar}\frac{\partial E}{\partial q_i}=\frac{\hbar q_i}{m_i},\]
\[\hbar\dot q_i=F_i,\]
\[a_i=\frac{F_i}{m_i}.\]

Thus a force rotated through the principal axes traces an ellipse of accelerations. To describe a longitudinal measurement, project the full vector onto the applied force. If the force makes angle \(\theta\) to the \(x\) axis and \(\phi\) is the direction of the resulting acceleration in the same quadrant, then

\[\frac{a_\parallel}{F}=\frac{\cos^2\theta}{m_x}+\frac{\sin^2\theta}{m_y},\]
\[\tan\phi=\frac{m_x}{m_y}\tan\theta .\]

The longitudinal inverse mass is a weighted average of inverse principal masses. The difference \(\phi-\theta\) vanishes along principal axes and for an isotropic band. The expansion requires the whole accelerated packet to stay inside the region of roughly constant curvature.

Worked example

Follow an oblique force pulse

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Let \(m_x=0.20m_e\), \(m_y=0.80m_e\), where \(m_e\) is the free electron mass. A force pulse of magnitude \(1.00\times10^{-17}\,\mathrm N\) acts at \(45^\circ\) for \(50.0\,\mathrm{fs}\) on a packet initially at rest. Its velocity gains are \(F\Delta t/(\sqrt2m_i)\), or \(1.94\,\mathrm{m/s}\) along \(x\) and \(0.485\,\mathrm{m/s}\) along \(y\). The velocity points only \(14.0^\circ\) above the light-mass axis.

Second application

Recover an unseen principal curvature

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A second band has longitudinal mass \(0.300m_e\) for a force at \(45^\circ\), and a measurement along its \(x\) axis gives

\[m_x=0.200m_e.\]

In units of \(1/m_e\), inversion yields \(m_e/m_y=2/0.300-1/0.200=1.667\), so \(m_y=0.600m_e\). A pulse along \(y\) should produce one third of the acceleration measured along \(x\) for the same force, providing an independent check of the principal-axis assignment.

Try it · Mass tensor

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Why label a state by crystal momentum?

A lattice translation by \(a\) commutes with the Hamiltonian when \(V(x+a)=V(x)\). Translation eigenvalues have modulus one because translation preserves norm. Write them \(e^{ika}\); then \(\psi_k(x)=e^{ikx}u_k(x)\) with \(u_k(x+a)=u_k(x)\). Since \(e^{i(k+G)a}=e^{ika}\) for \(G=2\pi h/a\), a single Brillouin zone suffices. The index distinguishing states at the same \(k\) is the band index. Crystal momentum labels translation symmetry and can change by a reciprocal vector in scattering.

A weak potential couples a degenerate pair

Near \(k=G/2\), free states \(|k\rangle\) and \(|k-G\rangle\) have nearly equal kinetic energies. The periodic potential has Fourier coefficients \(V_G\) that couple these waves. Absorbing the mean potential in both energies, the two-state characteristic equation is

\[(E-E_k)(E-E_{k-G})=|V_G|^2,\]

with solutions

\[E_\pm=\tfrac12(E_k+E_{k-G})\pm\sqrt{\tfrac14(E_k-E_{k-G})^2+|V_G|^2}.\]

At equality the splitting is \(2|V_G|\). For a real coefficient the states are standing waves whose densities peak at different locations relative to the potential. Far from the degeneracy, the square root is dominated by the kinetic splitting and one obtains two nearly free waves again. A Fourier component that vanishes by symmetry cannot open a first-order gap at that crossing. Check other bands before interpreting a local splitting as a gap for the entire solid.

Build a band from a localised orbital

Start instead with one localised orbital \(|n\rangle\) per cell, all of energy \(\epsilon_0\), with overlap neglected in the basis and nearest-neighbour hopping matrix element \(-t\). A translation eigenstate has site amplitudes \(c_n=e^{ikna}/\sqrt N\). Acting with \(H\) on one site amplitude gives \(E c_n=\epsilon_0 c_n-t(c_{n-1}+c_{n+1})\), and hence \(E(k)=\epsilon_0-2t\cos ka\). The bandwidth \(4|t|\) measures the energy spread produced by hopping. In the limit of vanishing hopping the local energy repeats for every \(k\). More than one orbital per cell generates a matrix eigenproblem even in this simple construction.

The group velocity is \(v=\hbar^{-1}dE/dk\). Under a weak force with \(\hbar\dot k=F\), acceleration is \(F E''(k)/\hbar^2\). Define \(m^*=\hbar^2/E''(k)\), a local measure of curvature. Near the bottom of the cosine band with \(t>0\),

\[E\simeq\epsilon_0-2t+t(ka)^2,\]
\[m^*=\hbar^2/(2ta^2).\]

Near the top the curvature changes sign. Describing the missing electrons in a nearly full band as positively charged holes with positive mass simplifies transport calculations.

Use filling to decide whether current can flow

For a complete time-reversal-symmetric band, the contributions of \(k\) and \(-k\) to current cancel. More generally the integral of the derivative of a periodic band over a whole zone is zero. A weak field can redistribute carriers near a partially filled band edge, whereas a fully filled isolated band has no vacant neighbouring states. With one spin-degenerate orbital per primitive cell there are two states per cell. One electron per cell half fills this noninteracting band; two fill it. Symmetry breaking, interactions and additional bands can change the conclusion. A single measured zone-edge splitting therefore gives incomplete information about conduction.

For further work on three-dimensional bands, Fermi surfaces and electron motion, see David Tong’s Solid State Physics lectures at Cambridge.

Open a gap by doubling a chain’s period

A uniform one-dimensional chain with one spin-degenerate orbital and one electron per site has a half-filled band. A distortion can change both the spectrum and the periodicity used to count states. Let the sites be separated by \(a\) before the distortion. Group successive sites into \(A,B\) pairs and allow alternating hopping amplitudes \(t_1,t_2>0\). The distorted unit cell has length \(2a\). For equal on-site energies, chosen as zero, the amplitude on \(A\) couples to \(B\) inside its own cell and to \(B\) one cell away. Bloch translation supplies a phase \(e^{-2ika}\) for the latter coupling. The sublattice equations give a two-by-two matrix with zero diagonal and off-diagonal elements \(-(t_1+t_2 e^{\mp 2ika})\). Its characteristic equation yields

\[E_\pm(k)=\pm\sqrt{t_1^2+t_2^2+2t_1t_2\cos(2ka)},\qquad |k|\leq\pi/(2a).\]

At the zone centre the hoppings add. At the new zone edge they subtract, opening a gap \(2|t_1-t_2|\). In the uniform limit the folded branches simply touch. Merely drawing a smaller zone did not change the crystal. It takes a physical alternation to split the states. With two electrons per doubled cell, the lower band is exactly filled when spin is counted. Its electronic energy falls on dimerization at fixed mean hopping: writing \(t_{1,2}=t\pm\delta\) gives

\[E_-=-2\sqrt{t^2\cos^2(ka)+\delta^2\sin^2(ka)},\]

which is at most the undistorted lower energy. The lattice also pays an elastic cost. Quantitative distortion amplitudes require that competing energy.

For \(t_1=1.20\,\mathrm{eV}\) and \(t_2=0.80\,\mathrm{eV}\) the zone-centre energies remain \(\pm2.00\,\mathrm{eV}\), while the zone-edge energies become \(\pm0.40\,\mathrm{eV}\). The minimum direct excitation costs \(0.80\,\mathrm{eV}\), corresponding to a vacuum wavelength of about \(1.55\,\mathrm{\mu m}\) if the optical transition is allowed. The cause of this insulating filling is the pairing of bonds. Away from the commensurate filling the chemical potential can lie inside a band even if the same structural splitting remains visible.

Follow a wave packet through the whole Brillouin zone

In a weak static field a band electron's crystal momentum changes even though its group velocity need not keep rising. Take one isolated tight-binding band \(E(k)=E_0-2t\cos(ka)\), positive hopping energy \(t\), lattice spacing \(a\) and electron charge \(-e\). Let the wave packet be narrow in \(k\) compared with the zone and neglect scattering and transitions to other bands. The semiclassical force equation is \(\hbar\dot k=-e\mathcal E\), while velocity follows from the band gradient:

\[v=(2ta/\hbar)\sin(ka).\]

Starting at \(k=0\) yields \(ka=-\omega_B t_{\rm lab}\) with Bloch angular frequency \(\omega_B=e\mathcal E a/\hbar\). Integrating velocity over laboratory time gives

\[x(t_{\rm lab})-x(0)=\frac{2t}{e\mathcal E}[\cos(\omega_Bt_{\rm lab})-1],\]
\[T_B=\frac{h}{e\mathcal E a}.\]

Although the field never changes sign, velocity reverses when the packet samples regions of opposite band slope. Over a complete zone traversal there is no net displacement because the band energy is periodic. Energy conservation offers another derivation: the electrostatic potential-energy change of a negatively charged electron in a uniform field is \(+e\mathcal E\Delta x\), offsetting the change in band energy. The maximal excursion equals the band width \(4t\) divided by the force magnitude.

Estimate an observable oscillation in a superlattice

Use a one-dimensional superlattice with \(a=8.00\) nm, hopping \(t=5.00\) meV and field \(\mathcal E=2.00\times10^5\) V/m. The potential-energy change per lattice period is \(e\mathcal E a=1.60\) meV. Thus \(T_B=h/(1.60\,{\rm meV})=2.58\) ps and the peak-to-peak excursion is \(20.0\,{\rm meV}/(e\mathcal E)=100\) nm, about 12.5 lattice periods. The maximum speed from the band derivative is

\[2ta/\hbar=1.22\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

At one quarter cycle velocity reaches its largest negative magnitude, while at half a cycle the electron reaches its furthest position and momentarily stops.

A momentum-relaxation time of 0.20 ps would interrupt most trajectories before a cycle completes, obscuring a Bloch oscillation despite the periodic dispersion. Conversely a scattering time several times longer than 2.58 ps would make coherent oscillatory transport plausible if the field does not tunnel carriers into other bands. In the weak-field limit the period and excursion both grow, so the mathematical limit of arbitrarily large excursions cannot be taken in a finite imperfect specimen. Recording the oscillation frequency as field is varied tests the predicted linear scaling separately from the much more sample-dependent amplitude.

The motion also clarifies the role of negative effective mass near a band top. The electron's crystal momentum continues to change in the direction of the applied force throughout the period. Its acceleration is proportional to the local band curvature, however, and can point in the opposite direction once that curvature changes sign. This reverses packet acceleration without a sudden elastic collision with a particular lattice plane. A spectrum measured along the direction of motion can predict the velocity through its local slope, giving a direct cross-check of the acceleration inferred from a time-domain trace.

The probe must resolve the oscillations before relaxation for that comparison to be meaningful.

Predicting a gap and testing it in a periodic medium

Condensed matter and band theory

Combine the translation argument, a measurable band edge and an independent numerical calculation to distinguish a gap from a folded crossing.

From first principles

Infer pass bands and stop bands from a repeated cell

For a normally incident monochromatic plane wave in a lossless layered dielectric, record its tangential electric and magnetic fields in a two-component state. Interface conditions transmit these same tangential fields between adjacent layers. Propagation through one complete cell of period \(a\) is a linear map represented by a matrix \(M(\omega)\). Forward and backward propagation in each lossless layer have reciprocal phase factors, so the cell transfer matrix has determinant one. A Bloch state reproduces its field after one cell up to a multiplier:

\[Mv=\lambda v,\]
\[\lambda=e^{ika},\]
\[\lambda^2-(\operatorname{tr}M)\lambda+1=0 .\]

The second root is \(1/\lambda\). Adding the two roots and using the phase relation gives the band condition

\[\cos(ka)=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr} M(\omega).\]

For a real lossless layered system the trace is real. Where its half lies between −1 and 1 there are real wave numbers. Outside this interval the two multipliers have different magnitudes and one solution decays with each successive cell. In a uniform material the trace simply reproduces homogeneous propagation: folding the dispersion into the first zone can make branches touch without opening a gap. Index contrast allows repeated reflections and can create finite stop bands.

Derivation and applications

Find the long-wavelength optical index of a layered cell

A finite wavelength can resolve two different dielectrics in a repeating stack. Far below the first band edge, what uniform medium would reproduce the phase accumulated per cell? Consider lossless, nonmagnetic layers of thickness \(d_1,d_2\), real refractive indices \(n_1,n_2\), and cell length \(a=d_1+d_2\). At normal incidence the field is tangential to the interfaces. Define \(k_0=\omega/c\) and the phase thickness \(\delta_j=k_0n_jd_j\). For Bloch wavevector \(K\), the exact trace relation is

\[\cos(Ka)=\cos\delta_1\cos\delta_2-\frac12\left(\frac{n_1}{n_2}+\frac{n_2}{n_1}\right)\sin\delta_1\sin\delta_2.\]

On the acoustic-like branch through zero frequency, all three phase angles are small. Keep the quadratic terms in each cosine and the product of the linear terms in the sines. Constant terms on both sides cancel. Substitution of the optical phases gives

\[(Ka)^2\simeq k_0^2\left[n_1^2d_1^2+n_2^2d_2^2+(n_1^2+n_2^2)d_1d_2\right].\]

The mixed term records the interfaces even at long wavelengths. Factor the bracket as \(a(n_1^2d_1+n_2^2d_2)\) and take the forward-propagating root. Writing the first layer’s thickness fraction as \(f=d_1/a\) yields

\[n_{\rm eff}=\lim_{\omega\to0}\frac{K}{k_0}=\sqrt{fn_1^2+(1-f)n_2^2}.\]

This is the thickness-weighted average of the relative permittivities under a square root. It agrees with a tangential electric field that is nearly uniform across a small cell. As a thickness fraction tends to unity the remaining material’s index is recovered. Near a band edge the omitted fourth-order terms in the trace matter, so a constant effective index cannot predict the gap.

Worked example

Measure the phase of a deeply subwavelength stack

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A cell contains \(10.0\,\mathrm{nm}\) of index 2.00 and \(30.0\,\mathrm{nm}\) of index 1.50. Its effective index is

\[\sqrt{0.25(4)+0.75(2.25)}=1.64.\]

At vacuum wavelength \(4.00\,\mathrm{\mu m}\), ten cells accumulate Bloch phase \(2\pi n_{\rm eff}(0.400/4.00)=1.03\) radians. The larger layer phase per cell is just \(0.071\) radians, consistent with the expansion.

Second application

Infer a layer fraction from a low-frequency slope

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Another stack uses indices 3.00 and 1.50. Its long-wavelength measured phase velocity is \(c/2.10\). Solving the squared-index average gives

\[f=(2.10^2-1.50^2)/(3.00^2-1.50^2)=0.320.\]

A \(50.0\,\mathrm{nm}\) cell should contain \(16.0\,\mathrm{nm}\) of the high-index material. A microscope can check that inference independently of the optical phase measurement.

Try it · Homogenised stack

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Transfer a field by one period

Consider a lossless stack of two nonmagnetic dielectrics at normal incidence. Layer \(j\) has real refractive index \(n_j\), thickness \(d_j\) and optical phase \(\delta_j=2\pi f n_j d_j/c\). Within a layer the electric field is a sum of forward and backward waves; the corresponding magnetic field has the opposite sign for the backward wave. Eliminating the wave amplitudes between the two faces gives the layer transfer matrix for electric field and magnetic field scaled by the vacuum impedance (one time-phase convention):

\[M_j=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\delta_j&i\sin\delta_j/n_j\\i n_j\sin\delta_j&\cos\delta_j\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det M_j=1 .\]

The tangential fields are continuous at a simple dielectric interface. Thus the matrix of one complete cell of size \(a=d_1+d_2\) is the product of the two layer matrices. Its eigenvalues multiply to one. For propagation in an infinite periodic stack the Bloch multipliers must be \(e^{\pm ika}\). Their sum fixes the half-trace:

\[\cos(ka)=\cos\delta_1\cos\delta_2-\tfrac12(n_1/n_2+n_2/n_1)\sin\delta_1\sin\delta_2 .\]

If the right side lies outside \([-1,1]\), no real \(k\) exists. One solution decays and the reciprocal one grows along the chosen direction. A semi-infinite passive medium selects the decaying solution inside a stop band. A finite sample can still transmit an evanescent field, especially when it contains few cells.

Derive a check that the numerical solver could fail

Set \(n_1=n_2=n\). The right side reduces to \(\cos(\delta_1+\delta_2)\) by the addition formula. The first two folded branches meet where the combined phase is \(\pi\), at \(fa/c=1/(2n)\). There is no interface reflection and no forbidden interval. With \(n=\sqrt{13}\) both branches should meet at about 0.138675. Two nearly equal eigenvalues on a finite mesh must be compared with the numerical resolution before reporting a narrow gap.

As another analytic limit, take each layer a quarter wavelength thick at the same frequency, so

\[\delta_1=\delta_2=\pi/2.\]

The half-trace is \(-\tfrac12(r+r^{-1})\), \(r=n_1/n_2\). Its magnitude exceeds one whenever \(r\ne1\). For \(r=2\) the decaying multiplier has magnitude \(1/2\) per cell. The attenuation here arises from coherent reflections in a lossless model. Small transmission need not mean that electromagnetic energy was dissipated.

Read the finite-element eigenvalues against the calculation

In the OneSimulation band experiment, keep the layer fractions fixed and change only the second permittivity. For the pair (13,12) the two reported zone-edge frequencies are 0.1396 and 0.1433 in reduced units; the gap is about 2.6% of their mean. Compare this separation with the zero-contrast result and with the two roots of the half-trace equation at \(-1\). For the much larger contrast (13,1), using a weak-perturbation approximation would be hard to justify. The transfer matrix still includes repeated scattering exactly for these ideal layers.

When investigating an unfamiliar contrast, halve the spatial mesh size and repeat the edge calculation. Track modes by their fields as well as by sorted eigenvalue if two branches nearly meet. For the scalar one-dimensional normal-incidence problem the observed stop band has a precise meaning. It is an interval of optical frequencies for that propagation geometry. Inferring a complete three-dimensional gap would require other directions and polarisations. The link to electronic bands is the shared translation eigenvalue and coherent coupling at a crossing; electronic filling and the optical transfer-matrix test answer different physical questions.

Turn an infinite stop band into finite-stack reflectance

The forbidden Bloch interval of an infinite periodic medium does not imply perfect reflection from a finite sample. Its termination and number of periods matter. Consider normal incidence on alternating lossless dielectric layers with refractive indices \(n_H,n_L\), each of optical thickness \(\lambda_0/4\) at the selected vacuum wavelength. Treat all permeabilities as \(\mu_0\) and use normalized optical admittance \(Y=n\). Across a quarter-wave layer the characteristic matrix mapping the fields on its far face to its entrance has zero diagonal and off-diagonal entries \(i/Y\) and \(iY\), with an overall phase convention fixed consistently.

For a terminating medium of admittance \(Y_{\rm load}\) the resulting input admittance is \(Y_{\rm layer}^2/Y_{\rm load}\). Applying this inversion first to the rear low-index layer and then to the front high-index layer multiplies load admittance by \((n_H/n_L)^2\). After \(N\) identical pairs, with the high-index layer first as seen by incident light,

\[Y_{\rm in}=n_s(n_H/n_L)^{2N},\]
\[R=\left|\frac{n_0-Y_{\rm in}}{n_0+Y_{\rm in}}\right|^2 .\]

Here \(n_s\) is the substrate index and \(n_0\) the incident medium index. This design-frequency result follows from phase accumulation in each layer. For a general wavelength the matrices are no longer simple inversions and both diagonal entries must be retained. Interchanging the order of high and low layers in a finite stack also changes the input admittance and reflection phase.

Select a period count for a required mirror

Let \(n_0=n_s=1.00\), \(n_H=2.00\) and \(n_L=1.50\) at \(\lambda_0=600\) nm. Quarter-wave physical thicknesses are

\[600/(4\times2.00)=75.0\,\mathrm{nm},\]
\[600/(4\times1.50)=100\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

Four pairs give

\[Y_{\rm in}=(4/3)^8=9.99,\]
\[R=[(9.99-1)/(9.99+1)]^2=0.669.\]

Eight pairs give \(Y_{\rm in}\simeq99.8\) and \(R=0.961\). Thus a fairly clear infinite-medium stop band can coexist with substantial transmission through a short specimen.

For at least 99.0 percent reflectance at the design wavelength the required admittance ratio is \((1+\sqrt{0.990})/(1-\sqrt{0.990})\simeq398\). Solving \((4/3)^{2N}\ge398\) gives \(N\ge10.4\), hence eleven complete pairs. If the indices become equal and match both external media, \(Y_{\rm in}=n_0\) for any period count and the reflection vanishes. This finite-stack result tests a different observable from an infinite cell eigenfrequency. It connects the gap calculation to a transmission or reflectance measurement while exposing the additional data, particularly termination and thickness, needed for that prediction.

At the chosen wavelength the reflection amplitude for high-index-first ordering is negative because the effective admittance exceeds the incident admittance. Measuring reflected intensity alone loses this phase sign. Interference with a calibrated reference reflector can recover the sign and identify termination differences even when two samples have similar reflectances. This matters when the periodic mirror forms one end of a resonant cavity: the reflection phase contributes to the total round-trip phase and can move the resonance frequency. A pair of finite mirrors can therefore share a stop-band range while giving different cavity phase conditions.

Derive the quarter-wave stop-band width

The finite mirror is easy to evaluate at the design wavelength. The same stack admits an analytic expression for the width of its infinite first stop band. In both layers the optical phase thickness is \(\delta=(\pi/2)(\omega/\omega_0)\) when refractive indices are treated as nondispersive. The one-period transfer matrix has unit determinant and half-trace \(\cos(Ka)\), where \(K\) is the Bloch wavenumber and \(a\) the physical period. Multiplication of the two layer matrices gives

\[\cos(Ka)=\cos^2\delta-\frac12\left(\frac{n_H}{n_L}+\frac{n_L}{n_H}\right)\sin^2\delta .\]

At band centre the half-trace is less than minus one for unequal indices, so \(K\) has a nonzero imaginary component. At either neighbouring edge set the half-trace equal to minus one and solve for \(\delta\). The two edge phases are \(\pi/2\pm\arcsin[(n_H-n_L)/(n_H+n_L)]\) for \(n_H>n_L\). Their separation gives fractional angular-frequency width

\[\frac{\Delta\omega}{\omega_0}=\frac4\pi\arcsin\left(\frac{n_H-n_L}{n_H+n_L}\right).\]

With indices 2.00 and 1.50 this is \(0.183\). A central frequency of 500 THz thus has edges near 454 and 546 THz. The frequency interval is symmetric about the centre in this ideal quarter-wave construction; the corresponding wavelength interval is asymmetric because wavelength is reciprocal to frequency. As the two indices merge, the edge separation vanishes even though an artificially repeated unit cell still has a Brillouin-zone boundary.

At centre the decaying Bloch amplitude falls by \(n_L/n_H=0.750\) per period. A physical period of 175 nm therefore gives a field-amplitude decay length \(175/\ln(2/1.5)=608\) nm. This infinite-medium length explains the strong dependence of the finite mirror on period count. The external-medium matching factors are still needed to turn that internal decay into the actual reflected intensity.

How alternating bonds split a one-dimensional band

Condensed matter and band theory

Two periodic chains contain precisely the same number of atoms at the same spacing. One has equal bonds and the other alternates strong and weak bonds. The difference changes which electron energies can propagate, even without changing the on-site energy of any atom.

Choose a primitive cell that repeats the bonds

Let a cell of length \(a\) contain orbitals A and B. An electron hops between A and B in the same cell with real amplitude \(t_1>0\), and between B and A in the next cell with amplitude \(t_2>0\). Use one orbital per site, neglect interactions and set both on-site energies to zero. We can absorb an overall hopping sign into the B basis state. Translation by one cell multiplies each component by \(e^{ika}\), although the relative amplitudes on A and B remain unknown. Writing those amplitudes as \(A,B\) turns the real-space nearest-neighbour equations into

\[EA=(t_1+t_2e^{-ika})B,\]
\[EB=(t_1+t_2e^{ika})A.\]

The cell boundary crossed by the second hopping accounts for its phase. The two coefficients are complex conjugates as required by Hermiticity. Substitute the second relation into the first without dividing by energy. A nonzero component then requires

\[E^2=(t_1+t_2e^{ika})(t_1+t_2e^{-ika}).\]

The two energies at each wavevector are

\[E_\pm(k)=\pm\sqrt{t_1^2+t_2^2+2t_1t_2\cos ka}.\]

For positive hoppings their magnitude is largest at \(k=0\), namely \(t_1+t_2\), and smallest at \(ka=\pi\), namely \(|t_1-t_2|\). The gap is \(2|t_1-t_2|\). At one spinless electron per cell the lower band is full, so this is also the independent-particle excitation gap in a large periodic chain.

Read the band formation from two limits

Take \(t_1=1.0\,\mathrm{eV}\), \(t_2=0.6\,\mathrm{eV}\). The lower band runs from \(-1.6\) to \(-0.4\,\mathrm{eV}\), the upper from \(0.4\) to \(1.6\,\mathrm{eV}\). The gap is \(0.8\,\mathrm{eV}\), while each bandwidth is \(1.2\,\mathrm{eV}\). If \(t_2\) tends to zero, the cell becomes an isolated pair with two discrete bonding energies \(\pm t_1\). Bands flatten because cells can no longer exchange probability. At \(t_1=t_2=t\), the spectrum becomes \(\pm2t|\cos(ka/2)|\) in the reduced zone. The two pieces meet at its edge. The two-site construction then folds the simple uniform chain into a smaller wavevector interval.

One can also read off a velocity. Away from a crossing, differentiate \(E_\pm\) to obtain

\[v_\pm=\mp a t_1t_2\sin(ka)/[\hbar|E_\pm|].\]

At extrema of a gapped band it vanishes. At the equal-hopping crossing the derivative written as a ratio has a removable directional ambiguity, because the ordered upper and lower bands meet with opposite slopes.

Exchange the two bonds

Keep \(t_1\) fixed and move \(t_2\) through it. Compare both the gap and the bandwidth. Interchanging the numerical values of the two hoppings exactly preserves the bulk energies. The experiment in the next chapter distinguishes these patterns by opening the chain at a specified bond. A termination supplies physical information that the unordered hopping pair and the energy graph alone cannot encode.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on inferring the hopping strengths

A measured dimer band has gap \(0.50\,\mathrm{eV}\) and reaches \(1.45\,\mathrm{eV}\) above the symmetry centre at \(k=0\). Find the two hopping amplitudes and predict the upper-band width.

Work through the solution

The sum is 1.45 eV and the absolute difference is 0.25 eV. Adding and subtracting gives the unordered pair 0.85 and 0.60 eV. The upper bandwidth is

\[1.45-0.25=1.20\,\mathrm{eV}.\]

Bulk energies do not determine which hopping is interrupted by a particular surface cut.

Expand the squared band energy near the Brillouin-zone boundary for unequal hoppings. Identify the small-\(q\) dispersion and its range.

Work through the solution

Set \(ka=\pi+qa\). Since \(\cos(\pi+qa)\simeq-1+(qa)^2/2\),

\[E^2\simeq(t_1-t_2)^2+t_1t_2(qa)^2.\]

For sufficiently small \(q\) the upper edge has quadratic curvature \(E_+\simeq|t_1-t_2|+t_1t_2(qa)^2/[2|t_1-t_2|]\). The last expansion fails when its second term in the squared energy competes with the gap term.

If the same constant on-site energy \(U\) is added to every site, which inferred quantities would move in an experiment measuring energies against a fixed reference?

Work through the solution

The Hamiltonian gains \(U I\), moving both bands and their centre by \(U\). Their gap, widths, group velocities, and the hopping estimates from differences remain unchanged. The half-filling chemical potential can still lie inside the shifted gap.

Further reading · Asbóth, Oroszlány and Pályi, SSH model and bulk bands.

Finding an end state one bond at a time

Condensed matter and band theory

The bulk spectrum of an alternating chain is unchanged when strong and weak bonds exchange places. A boundary with a named first site breaks this equivalence. The zero-energy equations provide its wavefunction without diagonalising a large matrix.

Start at an exposed A site

Use the alternating chain with intracell link \(t_1\) and intercell link \(t_2\), both positive. End the left edge at \(A_1\). The first B site connects \(A_1\) and \(A_2\). Seek an eigenstate at \(E=0\) supported only on A sites. Each A-site equation then holds because all neighbouring B amplitudes vanish. Each B-site equation requires cancellation between its two neighbouring A amplitudes. Hence

\[0=t_1 A_j+t_2 A_{j+1},\]
\[A_j=(-t_1/t_2)^{j-1}A_1.\]

Relative signs matter in the cancellation, even though a local probability measurement sees only squared magnitudes. On a semi-infinite chain the geometric series for the norm converges only for \(r=t_1/t_2<1\). Normalising gives

\[|A_1|^2=1-r^2.\]

The amplitude decay length satisfies \(e^{-a/\ell}=r\), so \(\ell=a/\ln(t_2/t_1)\). The probability decay length is half that. As the bulk gap closes, the decay length diverges and the bound state spreads into the interior.

Impose the far boundary too

A finite chain can have \(M\) A sites and \(M-1\) B sites, ending on A at both edges. Every B site still imposes exactly the recurrence above, leaving one overall free amplitude. The resulting state has exactly zero energy for any positive hoppings. Its finite norm is the sum of \(M\) successive powers of \(r^2\). At \(r=1\) the apparent zero over zero in the closed formula is resolved by summing \(M\) unit terms. The state then has probability \(1/M\) at every A site.

When \(r>1\) its norm remains finite in this finite sample and the weight accumulates at the right edge. An equal-sublattice finite chain ending on B instead has an additional boundary equation. Two exponentially localised end solutions can then hybridise, shifting their energies slightly above and below zero. Equal sublattice counts alone provide no exact zero eigenvalue. A change of boundary conditions can therefore alter the interpretation of a measured near-zero peak.

Connect the recurrence to the bulk phase

The off-diagonal matrix element \(h=t_1+t_2e^{ika}\) traces a circle centred at \(t_1\) with radius \(t_2\) as \(k\) crosses the Brillouin zone. If \(t_2>t_1\) it winds once around zero. If \(t_2<t_1\) it misses zero. Passing between the two with fixed cell choice requires the gap \(2|h|\) to close. A diagonal potential on just one sublattice would break the off-diagonal symmetry used to pin energies to zero. The winding statement and the boundary argument require a consistent assignment of the unit cell and termination.

For \(r=0.5\) the first three semi-infinite A probabilities are 0.75, 0.1875 and 0.046875. For \(r=0.90\), the first site has only 0.19 and the amplitude decay length increases from \(1.44a\) to \(9.49a\). Use the finite odd chain below to move the density from one end to the other. At reciprocal hopping ratios the two distributions must be mirror images. Check this using the endpoint readouts.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on inspecting a local measurement

Four successive A sites have relative intensities \(1,0.36,0.1296,0.0467\) near a left edge. Infer the hopping ratio and amplitude decay length. Would a local intensity probe detect the alternating signs of the state?

Work through the solution

Ratios of neighbouring probabilities equal \(r^2\), hence \(r=0.60\). The amplitude length is

\[-a/\ln(0.60)=1.96a.\]

A measurement proportional to the absolute square loses the sign. Interference with a phase reference or a measurement sensitive to the amplitude across a bond can access the phase relation.

A finite odd chain has five A sites and hopping ratio \(r=0.8\). What fraction of zero-mode probability lies on its first two A sites?

Work through the solution

Sum the finite geometric sequence. The requested fraction is

\[(1+r^2)/\sum_{j=0}^4r^{2j}=(1-r^4)/(1-r^{10})=0.661.\]

Taking the semi-infinite formula would give

\[1-r^4=0.590.\]

The far boundary changes the normalisation substantially for this short array.

Can bond disorder that preserves positive nonzero nearest-neighbour hoppings destroy the exact zero solution of a finite chain ending on A at both ends?

Work through the solution

At each B site retain the local recursion \(A_{j+1}=-(t_{1j}/t_{2j})A_j\). It produces a finite nonzero solution after choosing \(A_1\), with all B amplitudes zero. The profile is a product of local ratios and may cease to look like a single exponential. A generic A-site potential would invalidate the zero-energy equation.

Further reading · Asbóth, Oroszlány and Pályi, edge states and bulk-boundary correspondence.

Why a honeycomb lattice has Dirac cones

Condensed matter and band theory

In an alternating chain the two paths from A to B can cancel at a special wavevector. The honeycomb lattice adds a third path. Their cancellation now occurs at isolated points in a two-dimensional Brillouin zone and produces a linear low-energy dispersion.

Add the three Bloch phases

Each honeycomb A site has three nearest B neighbours at displacements \(d(0,1)\), \(d(\sqrt3/2,-1/2)\), and \(d(-\sqrt3/2,-1/2)\), where \(d\) is the bond length. For equal real hopping magnitude \(t\), sum the translation phase for each displacement. The common off-diagonal factor is

\[f(\boldsymbol k)=e^{ik_yd}+2e^{-ik_yd/2}\cos(\sqrt3 k_xd/2),\]
\[E_\pm=\pm t|f|.\]

This uses a basis phase convention that includes the orbitals' intra-cell positions. A rephasing can change the phase of \(f\) but leaves its magnitude invariant. Along \(k_y=0\), cancellation occurs where \(1+2\cos(\sqrt3 k_xd/2)=0\). Choose \(K=(4\pi/(3\sqrt3d),0)\). The counterpart at \(-K\) is inequivalent up to primitive reciprocal vectors. Near either valley many wavevectors represent distinct states even when their energies almost coincide.

Linearise at a zero, then test the approximation

Write \(\boldsymbol k=K+\boldsymbol q\) with \(qd\ll1\). At \(K\) the cosine equals \(-1/2\) and the sine \(\sqrt3/2\). Differentiating the cosine term with respect to \(k_x\) consequently gives \(-3d/2\). Differentiating the two exponential factors with respect to \(k_y\) gives

\[id+id/2=3id/2.\]

The constant terms cancel. Therefore

\[f(K+\boldsymbol q)\simeq \tfrac{3d}{2}(-q_x+iq_y),\]
\[E_\pm\simeq\pm\hbar v_F|\boldsymbol q|,\]
\[v_F=\frac{3td}{2\hbar}.\]

With \(t=2.7\,\mathrm{eV}\), \(d=0.142\,\mathrm{nm}\) and \(\hbar=0.6582\,\mathrm{eV\,fs}\), the speed is

\[0.874\,\mathrm{nm/fs}=8.74\times10^5\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

At \(qd=0.05\) the leading energy magnitude is \(0.2025\,\mathrm{eV}\). The energy varies linearly with momentum displacement even though the microscopic Hamiltonian contains only hopping between neighbours. The two components describe sublattice amplitude, distinct from the real electron spin.

Break the equivalence of the sublattices

Assign on-site energies \(+\Delta\) to A and \(-\Delta\) to B. The characteristic equation of the two-by-two Hamiltonian becomes

\[E^2=\Delta^2+t^2|f|^2.\]

A gap \(2|\Delta|\) opens at K. For \(\Delta=0.10\,\mathrm{eV}\), the edge sits at \(0.10\,\mathrm{eV}\) and the predicted conduction energy at \(qd=0.05\) becomes about \(0.226\,\mathrm{eV}\). Far enough from the origin the slopes recover their nearly linear values. Close to it, expansion of the square root gives

\[E_+\simeq|\Delta|+\hbar^2v_F^2q^2/(2|\Delta|).\]

A band-edge mass \(|\Delta|/v_F^2\) has emerged. The expansion requires \(\hbar v_Fq\ll|\Delta|\).

Compare the exact positive-energy cut along \(q_x\) with the local cone in the graph. Unequal deviations to the right and left of K are allowed by the lattice and cannot be captured by the lowest-order isotropic cone. The reflected cut at the other valley restores the time-reversal relation.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a measured slope

A near-valley spectrum has slope \(0.55\,\mathrm{eV\,nm}\). Infer \(v_F\) and the nearest-neighbour hopping for a bond length \(d=0.142\,\mathrm{nm}\). A gap of \(80\,\mathrm{meV}\) then appears with the same asymptotic slope. What is \(|\Delta|\)?

Work through the solution

The slope equals \(\hbar v_F\). Dividing by \(0.6582\,\mathrm{eV\,fs}\) gives \(0.836\,\mathrm{nm/fs}\). Since the slope also equals \(3td/2\),

\[t=2(0.55)/(3\times0.142)=2.58\,\mathrm{eV}.\]

The two edges shift symmetrically by half the full gap, giving \(|\Delta|=40\,\mathrm{meV}\).

Consider a gapless honeycomb cone exactly at the valley centre. What does the tight-binding Hamiltonian predict for the energy and eigenvectors, and what information comes from approaching along a direction?

Work through the solution

At \(f=0\) the matrix vanishes, so every two-component vector in the degenerate subspace has energy zero. For a nearby nonzero \(f\), a chosen eigenbranch fixes a relative phase between its A and B components according to the argument of \(f\). The limiting eigenvector therefore depends on the direction of approach even though the limiting energy is always zero.

At \(q=0\), repeat the eigenvector argument with \(\Delta>0\). Predict a consequence for sublattice-resolved spectroscopy at the two band edges.

Work through the solution

At the valley centre the Hamiltonian is diagonal with entries \(+\Delta,-\Delta\). The upper state lives on A and the lower on B in this basis. A probe selective to A should see more weight at the conduction edge than at the valence edge. Farther from the valley centre the hopping mixes the sublattices again.

Further reading · Delft Topology in Condensed Matter, honeycomb Hamiltonian and Dirac points.

When a periodic dielectric has no propagating Bloch wave

Fields and the response of matter

Derive a band gap using two lossless transparent materials and distinguish evanescent Bloch propagation from absorption of optical power.

Construct a unit cell whose waves interfere

Stack two transparent, lossless, nonmagnetic dielectrics periodically in one dimension. Their refractive indices are \(n_1,n_2\), their physical thicknesses \(d_1,d_2\), and the period \(a=d_1+d_2\). Work at normal incidence, so polarisation directions are equivalent for isotropic layers. Each separate material admits a propagating wave at the chosen optical frequency, yet the periodic stack may not. The effect comes from coherent interface reflections, not from a negative refractive index or an absorptive constituent.

Describe the local field by its tangential electric and magnetic components. Propagation through one homogeneous layer connects them by a \(2\times2\) transfer matrix with diagonal \(\cos\phi_j\), off-diagonal terms proportional to \(iZ_j\sin\phi_j\) and \(i\sin\phi_j/Z_j\), where \(\phi_j=\omega n_jd_j/c\) and \(Z_j=Z_0/n_j\). Changing the chosen propagation direction or time convention may reverse the two imaginary signs together. The real trace and unit determinant of the cell matrix below are unaffected.

Read the dispersion relation from the trace

At an interface both tangential components are continuous in the absence of imposed sheet currents. Multiply the two propagation matrices to form one cell. Its determinant is one because each layer's determinant is

\[\cos^2\phi+\sin^2\phi=1.\]

Thus the two eigenvalues are reciprocal. For a Bloch mode they are \(e^{\pm i k_Ba}\) on a pass band. Taking half the trace gives

\[\cos(k_Ba)=\cos\phi_1\cos\phi_2-\frac12\left(\frac{n_1}{n_2}+\frac{n_2}{n_1}\right)\sin\phi_1\sin\phi_2 .\]

When the right side lies between −1 and 1 a real Bloch phase exists. If its magnitude exceeds one, a decaying and a growing Bloch factor replace a pair of unit-modulus factors. For a bounded excitation of a semi-infinite stack choose the decaying direction. The attenuation per cell is \(\operatorname{arcosh}|\operatorname{trace}(M)/2|\). A lossless semi-infinite crystal in a stop band reflects incident power. Spatial attenuation of a Bloch amplitude in this region is compatible with zero volumetric heat production.

Obtain a quantitative stop-band width

Choose \(n_1d_1=n_2d_2=\lambda_0/4\). Writing \(s=\omega/\omega_0\), both layer phases are \(\phi=\pi s/2\). At \(s=1\) the half trace is \(-\tfrac12(n_2/n_1+n_1/n_2)\). Recognising \(\cosh(\log r)=(r+r^{-1})/2\) gives centre attenuation \(|\log(n_2/n_1)|\) per cell. For \(n_1=1.5,n_2=2.0\) this is 0.2877, so a decaying eigencomponent has amplitude factor 0.75 per cell. This statement about an eigencomponent should not be confused with the exact transmittance of a specific finite stack, which also depends on entrance and exit impedances.

The first gap edges obey half trace −1. With \(\cos^2\phi=1-\sin^2\phi\), solve for the edge sine or cosine. The two edge phases are symmetric about \(\pi/2\) and satisfy

\[|\cos\phi|=|n_2-n_1|/(n_1+n_2).\]

Hence the fractional angular-frequency width is \((4/\pi)\arcsin(|n_2-n_1|/(n_1+n_2))\), about 0.183 for the example. The lowest gap width tends continuously to zero as index contrast vanishes. A gap in a plotted numerical spectrum at \(n_1=n_2\) would indicate an implementation or plotting problem.

Compare a frequency scan with a finite mirror

Open the live experiment

The graph plots half the transfer-matrix trace against normalised frequency and marks the admissible interval for a real Bloch phase. Change the index contrast first, keeping the layers quarter wave at the same reference frequency. The physical layer thickness adjusts in this family; it is a different experiment from changing a material while manufacturing all thicknesses fixed. Use the frequency control to read an attenuation exponent at the centre and just outside the gap.

At low frequency a wavelength spans many cells and a homogenised description can predict the long-wave speed. At the first gap the field changes phase by order one across a cell, exactly where the homogenisation scale separation fails. Conversely, an electromagnetic calculation showing multiple bands at very low frequency should be checked against a uniform-medium or long-wavelength effective-index limit. Compare a finite stack's reflection with the Bragg-mirror experiment. The bulk trace determines whether a propagating state can exist. A finite sample adds interference between boundaries and possible surface or defect effects.

Tutorial

If the two refractive indices become equal, simplify the trace formula for arbitrary thicknesses.

Work through the solution

The impedance factor becomes one, so the half trace is \(\cos\phi_1\cos\phi_2-\sin\phi_1\sin\phi_2=\cos(\phi_1+\phi_2)\). It never exceeds unit magnitude. Labelling a uniform medium by an artificial period folds the dispersion into zones but does not create a physical gap.

At the central quarter-wave frequency for index ratio 2, how many periods reduce a decaying Bloch component's amplitude by a factor of 100?

Work through the solution

The amplitude factor per cell is

\[e^{-\log2}=1/2.\]

Require \(2^{-N}\le0.01\), giving \(N\ge7\). A prediction for transmitted optical power also needs termination and impedance matching.

A measured stack exhibits both a transmission minimum and measurable heating. Can the minimum be attributed entirely to a band gap?

Work through the solution

A lossless band gap can reflect power without heating. Measure reflected and transmitted flux under consistent normalisation, then infer absorption from \(1-R-T\) after accounting for diffuse scattering and other exit channels. The bulk stop-band model and material absorption can coexist.

Compare with band calculations

MIT's mathematical nanophotonics course computes full periodic Maxwell modes. The one-dimensional trace above is a small benchmark with known pass and stop boundaries that a numerical eigenproblem should reproduce.

Action density, stress waves and the boundary that reflects them

Analytical mechanics from constraints to invariants

A chain of coupled masses becomes an elastic field when the wavelength is long compared with the spacing. Derive the field equation and its boundary traction from the same variation, follow energy and stress across a junction, and decide whether a measured pulse is limited by material waves or by numerical dispersion.

Pass from a discrete rod to an action density

Let equal masses of spacing \(a\) move longitudinally by \(u_j(t)\), with nearest-neighbour spring constant \(k_s\) and mass \(m_s\) per site. Their Lagrangian is \(\sum_j[m_s\dot u_j^2/2-k_s(u_{j+1}-u_j)^2/2]\). For variations smooth on the spacing, \((u_{j+1}-u_j)/a\to u_x\) and \(\sum_j a\,f_j\to\int f(x)\,dx\). Hold the line density \(\rho A=m_s/a\) and axial rigidity \(EA=k_s a\) fixed. The continuum Lagrangian density per unit reference length is

\[{\cal L}=\rho A\,u_t^2/2-EA\,u_x^2/2,\]

with \(A\) area, \(\rho\) volume density and \(E\) Young modulus. Both terms have units of joules per metre. This is a slender-rod model with small strain, primarily uniaxial stress and wavelengths long compared with the cross-sectional dimensions.

Vary the field in the action over \([0,L]\times[t_1,t_2]\). Integration of \(\rho A\,u_t\delta u_t\) by parts in time, with fixed endpoint fields, produces \(-\rho A\,u_{tt}\delta u\). Integration of \(-EAu_x\delta u_x\) by parts in space produces \(+\partial_x(EAu_x)\delta u\) and a remaining term \(-[EAu_x\delta u]_0^L\). Thus the interior equation is \(\rho A u_{tt}=\partial_x(EAu_x)\), and the end force conjugate to displacement is axial force \(N=EAu_x\). Adding external work \(F_L(t)\delta u(L)\) at the right face yields

\[N(L)=F_L.\]

A prescribed displacement instead imposes \(\delta u(L)=0\), leaving its reaction as an output. Keeping this boundary term prevents prescribing displacement and independent traction simultaneously at the same end of a second-order rod.

Determine the energy flux and characteristic impedance

Assume time-independent coefficients. Multiply the field equation by \(u_t\) and rearrange to obtain

\[\partial_t[\rho A u_t^2/2+EAu_x^2/2]+\partial_x[-EAu_xu_t]=0.\]

The energy flux is \(-Nu_t\), with positive sign denoting energy moving towards increasing \(x\). The minus sign follows the convention of positive tensile \(N\): positive mechanical power entering through a right face is \(Nu_t\), which is inward with respect to the rod on its left. This identity controls energy transport across both smooth material changes and sharp joints.

For a homogeneous right-moving wave \(u=F(x-ct)\), \(c=\sqrt{E/\rho}\), particle velocity \(v=u_t=-cF'\) and axial force \(N=EAF'=-Zv\), where \(Z=\rho A c=EA/c\). A left-moving wave has \(N=+Zv\). The impedance is force divided by particle velocity and has units \({\rm kg/s}\). If one instead uses stress, impedance is \(\rho c\) per unit area. Doubling the area of the same material keeps wave speed unchanged but doubles the force–velocity impedance. A transit-time measurement and a reflection measurement therefore provide independent material or geometry information.

For a rod with \(E=70\,{\rm GPa}\), \(\rho=2700\,{\rm kg/m^3}\) and \(A=1.0\times10^{-4}\,{\rm m^2}\), \(c\approx5092\,{\rm m/s}\) and \(Z\approx1375\,{\rm kg/s}\). A right-going compressive pulse with particle speed \(0.020\,{\rm m/s}\) carries axial force \(-27.5\,{\rm N}\), compressive stress \(0.275\,{\rm MPa}\), and instantaneous power \(Zv^2=0.550\,{\rm W}\) across a section. A rectangular approximation lasting \(100\,\mu{\rm s}\) carries about \(55\,\mu{\rm J}\) and occupies \(0.509\,{\rm m}\). The pulse speed is kilometres per second; the material particles move only millimetres per second. Treating these as the same velocity would spoil force, impulse and energy estimates.

Match a bonded interface and distinguish amplitude from power

At a perfect massless bond displacement and axial force are continuous. For an incident right-going velocity amplitude \(v_i\) in rod 1, let \(v_r\) be reflected left-going velocity and \(v_t\) transmitted right-going velocity. Velocity continuity gives

\[v_i+v_r=v_t.\]

Force continuity gives

\[-Z_1v_i+Z_1v_r=-Z_2v_t.\]

Thus

\[r_v=\frac{Z_1-Z_2}{Z_1+Z_2},\]
\[t_v=\frac{2Z_1}{Z_1+Z_2},\]
\[{\cal R}=r_v^2,\]
\[{\cal T}=\frac{Z_2}{Z_1}t_v^2=\frac{4Z_1Z_2}{(Z_1+Z_2)^2}.\]

The power fractions add to one for real positive impedances. The reflected force coefficient has opposite sign to the reflected velocity coefficient because the wave direction reverses. At a free end \(Z_2\to0\), reflected velocity equals incident velocity, so end velocity doubles while total force vanishes. At a rigid end \(Z_2\to\infty\), velocity reverses and force doubles. The sign a strain gauge reports can therefore differ from that of a velocity-sensitive optical measurement at the same reflection.

A step from the worked rod into one of the same material with twice the cross-sectional area gives \(Z_2=2Z_1\), \(r_v=-1/3\), \(t_v=2/3\), and power fractions \(1/9\) and \(8/9\). A reduced transmitted velocity amplitude does not imply that most energy is reflected. If the interface includes a concentrated mass \(M_b\), force jumps instead obey \(N_2-N_1=M_b\dot v_b\) with signs determined by forces exerted on that mass from either side. At high frequency the reaction of that mass can mimic a much stiffer termination. Frequency-dependent reflection is then an expected boundary effect, rather than evidence that the bulk modulus varies with frequency.

Connect continuum modes to their lattice predecessor

For a periodic chain the exact discrete equation is

\[m_s\ddot u_j=k_s(u_{j+1}-2u_j+u_{j-1}).\]

Substitute \(u_j=U e^{i(kja-\omega t)}\) to obtain

\[\omega=2\sqrt{k_s/m_s}\,|\sin(ka/2)|.\]

In the first positive Brillouin zone, \(\omega/(ck)=\sin(ka/2)/(ka/2)\) and group velocity \(d\omega/dk=c\cos(ka/2)\), where \(c=a\sqrt{k_s/m_s}\). The continuum rod retains only the leading low-\(ka\) term. A wave with eight sites per wavelength has phase speed about \(0.9745c\) and group speed \(0.9239c\). A narrowband packet's peak can arrive later than the continuum prediction even when a single crest is timed fairly accurately.

A finite-difference code approximating the continuum rod can produce the same spatial dispersion with its mesh spacing in place of a physical lattice spacing. Distinguish them experimentally or numerically by changing the computational mesh while holding the physical structure fixed. Lattice effects persist as the numerical grid is refined; artificial spatial dispersion diminishes. A separate time-step study is needed if a time integrator also disperses the wave. Comparing both peak arrival and phase at two frequencies tests more of the dynamics than fitting a single transit time.

Derive transverse beam waves from curvature energy

A slender beam bending laterally is governed by a different elastic energy from the axial rod. Let \(w(x,t)\) denote small transverse deflection of its neutral axis. Under the Euler–Bernoulli assumption that cross sections remain plane and normal to that axis, the axial strain a distance \(z\) from the neutral axis is approximately \(-z w_{xx}\). Integrating the local elastic energy \(E\epsilon_{xx}^2/2\) across the section gives \(EI_Aw_{xx}^2/2\) per unit length, where \(I_A=\int z^2\,dA\) is a geometrical second moment of area, with units \({\rm m^4}\). It is distinct from a mass moment of inertia. The kinetic contribution uses line density \(\rho A\) as before.

Varying \({\cal L}=\rho A w_t^2/2-EI_Aw_{xx}^2/2\) requires integrating twice by parts in space. With constant coefficients the interior variation gives

\[\rho A w_{tt}+EI_A w_{xxxx}=0.\]

The boundary terms are \(-[EI_Aw_{xx}\delta w_x]+[(EI_Aw_{xx})_x\delta w]\). Thus slope variations pair with bending moments and displacement variations with shear forces, with signs set by the chosen face orientation. A clamped end prescribes both deflection and slope; a free end sets moment and shear to zero. A simply supported end prescribes deflection and zero bending moment. Using only one boundary condition per end, as for the axial rod, leaves the fourth-order beam problem underdetermined.

A sinusoid \(e^{i(kx-\omega t)}\) gives

\[\omega=\sqrt{EI_A/(\rho A)}\,k^2.\]

Phase speed grows proportionally to wavenumber and group speed is twice phase speed in this model. A narrow transverse pulse in a long beam consequently spreads even in a perfectly homogeneous non-dissipative continuum. By contrast the axial rod was nondispersive in its long-wavelength continuum approximation. At high enough frequency the simple bending model omits rotary inertia, shear deformation and eventually three-dimensional elastic behaviour. Extrapolating its unbounded speed to arbitrary \(k\) would use the slender, low-frequency approximation far beyond its derivation.

For a simply supported beam with length \(L\), the sine modes satisfy both end conditions and give

\[\omega_n=(n\pi/L)^2\sqrt{EI_A/(\rho A)}.\]

For a rectangular section of width \(w_b\) and thickness \(d\) in the bending direction, \(I_A=w_bd^3/12\) and \(A=w_bd\). Doubling thickness doubles all the natural angular frequencies at fixed \(L,E,\rho\), whereas doubling width alone leaves the ideal frequencies unchanged. The stiffness increases and so does the inertia. A beam with \(L=0.50\,{\rm m}\), \(d=5.0\,{\rm mm}\) and the earlier aluminium properties has \(\sqrt{EI_A/(\rho A)}\approx7.35\,{\rm m^2/s}\), giving fundamental \(46.2\,{\rm Hz}\). The second bending mode is near four times that frequency, a clear difference from the approximately harmonic axial mode sequence.

Match a force pulse to a support spring and dashpot

A right termination exerts force on the rod through a dashpot with mechanical impedance \(Z_L\). For a real positive dashpot impedance the reflected velocity fraction is \(r=(Z-Z_L)/(Z+Z_L)\). Taking \(Z_L=Z\) gives zero reflection at the termination even though energy leaves a finite rod: the dashpot dissipates the arriving power. With \(Z_L=3Z\) one obtains a reflected velocity of \(-1/2\) of the incident value, a reflected energy fraction one quarter and absorbed power fraction three quarters. A free or rigid end also reflects energy, but it absorbs no net power in the ideal limit. A measured weak reflection alone is therefore not proof of weak damping inside the rod.

For a pure end spring of stiffness \(k_L\) attached to a rigid support, the velocity impedance is \(Z_L=k_L/(i\omega)\) under \(e^{i\omega t}\). It is imaginary, so the reflection coefficient has unit magnitude at every frequency and a frequency-dependent phase. At low frequency the spring impedance is large and the end looks nearly fixed. At high frequency its impedance is small and the end looks nearly free within the lumped approximation. An impulsive signal contains many frequencies and can return with a changed waveform even without any transmitted or dissipated energy. A support combining a resistor-like dashpot and a spring gives both amplitude loss and phase variation.

A measurement with strain gauges at two positions can separate forward and backward waves if their path delays are resolved. In a narrow frequency band the two records are linear combinations of advancing and returning phase factors. If the gauge spacing corresponds to an integer number of half wavelengths, these combinations become linearly dependent and the separation is ill-conditioned. Change the spacing, use a broadband pulse, or combine a velocity measurement with a strain measurement at one station so that the two characteristic directions enter with opposite signs. The relation \(v=(v_++v_-)\), \(N=Z(-v_++v_-)\) then gives a direct local decomposition.

Derive sound propagation from mass and momentum conservation

The displacement-action argument has a fluid analogue. Take an initially uniform fluid of density \(\rho_0\) and pressure \(p_0\), moving little from equilibrium in one spatial dimension. Write small perturbations \(\rho',p',v\). Linearised mass conservation is

\[\partial_t\rho'+\rho_0\partial_x v=0.\]

Momentum balance for a short fluid parcel is \(\rho_0\partial_tv=-\partial_xp'\). If compression over an acoustic cycle is approximately isentropic, \(p'=c_s^2\rho'\) with \(c_s^2=(\partial p/\partial\rho)_s\). Differentiate the first equation in time, eliminate the velocity derivative with momentum balance, and obtain \(\partial_t^2p'=c_s^2\partial_x^2p'\).

The thermodynamic derivative matters because pressure can respond differently when compression is slow enough for heat exchange. For a perfect gas with ratio of specific heats \(\gamma\), isentropic perturbations give

\[c_s^2=\gamma p_0/\rho_0.\]

Taking an isothermal derivative would give \(p_0/\rho_0\) instead. For \(p_0=101\,{\rm kPa}\), \(\rho_0=1.20\,{\rm kg/m^3}\), and \(\gamma=1.40\), the isentropic speed is about \(343\,{\rm m/s}\), while the isothermal calculation gives \(290\,{\rm m/s}\). A measured transit time can discriminate between these constitutive conditions. Wall heat exchange in a very narrow tube can alter which approximation is appropriate over a chosen frequency band.

For a right-moving sound wave \(p'=\rho_0c_s v\), with the pressure positive in compression. This differs in sign from the tensile-positive axial force used for the rod, so match physical force conventions before borrowing formulas across the two examples. The acoustic power per area is \(p'v\). For sinusoidal pressure with rms amplitude \(1.0\,{\rm Pa}\) in the stated gas, the mean intensity is

\[p_{\rm rms}^2/(\rho_0 c_s)=2.43\times10^{-3}\,{\rm W/m^2}.\]

A peak amplitude of \(1.0\,{\rm Pa}\) would yield half this number. Writing rms or peak explicitly prevents a routine factor-of-two error when comparing a microphone measurement with an energy flux.

Determine whether a measured transit reveals a modulus or a geometry

Suppose two axially loaded slender rods have measured wave speeds agreeing within their uncertainties. That alone determines only the ratio \(E/\rho\) under the slender-rod assumptions. Measuring force–velocity impedance \(Z=\rho A c\) and cross section as well gives \(\rho=Z/(Ac)\) and \(E=Zc/A\). With \(Z=1375\,{\rm kg/s}\), \(c=5092\,{\rm m/s}\) and \(A=1.0\times10^{-4}\,{\rm m^2}\), the inversion gives

\[\rho=2700\,{\rm kg/m^3},\]
\[E=70.0\,{\rm GPa}.\]

If area were overestimated by five percent, both inferred quantities would be underestimated by almost five percent while their ratio remained correct.

Now examine a thick elastic solid under a narrow compressional disturbance with wavelengths short compared with its transverse dimensions. Lateral deformation can be constrained by surrounding material. In an isotropic three-dimensional continuum the longitudinal plane-wave modulus is \(\lambda+2\mu_s\), where \(\lambda,\mu_s\) are Lamé coefficients, rather than the uniaxial-stress Young modulus of a slender rod. The shear-wave speed satisfies \(c_T^2=\mu_s/\rho\) and longitudinal speed \(c_L^2=(\lambda+2\mu_s)/\rho\). Measuring both, together with density, can recover the two independent elastic moduli. Using only one longitudinal speed to infer Young modulus without specifying the deformation geometry can give a physically incorrect value.

For \(\rho=2500\,{\rm kg/m^3}\), \(c_L=5000\,{\rm m/s}\) and \(c_T=3000\,{\rm m/s}\), these relations give

\[\mu_s=22.5\,{\rm GPa},\]
\[\lambda=17.5\,{\rm GPa}.\]

The implied Young modulus \(E=\mu_s(3\lambda+2\mu_s)/(\lambda+\mu_s)=54.8\,{\rm GPa}\), while \(\rho c_L^2=62.5\,{\rm GPa}\). Both numbers represent material stiffness, but they enter different mechanical boundary conditions. A long thin specimen of the same material should have an axial speed closer to \(\sqrt{E/\rho}=4680\,{\rm m/s}\). Comparing the bulk and rod experiments tests the constitutive and geometrical assumptions behind the measured speed.

Derive orthogonality when a rod changes material along its length

For a rod with positive varying density \(\rho(x)\), area \(A(x)\) and modulus \(E(x)\), a separated free oscillation \(u(x,t)=\phi(x)\cos\omega t\) satisfies

\[-[EA\phi']'=\omega^2\rho A\phi.\]

Suppose two modes \(\phi_n,\phi_m\) satisfy the same homogeneous fixed or free boundary conditions and the correct continuity of displacement and axial force across every perfectly bonded interface. Multiply the first equation by \(\phi_m\), the second by \(\phi_n\), subtract and integrate. Interior terms produced by integration by parts cancel pairwise, including the interface flux terms, and the endpoint terms vanish. Thus

\[(\omega_n^2-\omega_m^2)\int\rho A\phi_n\phi_m\,dx=0.\]

Mode shapes with distinct frequencies are orthogonal under the mass-weighted inner product, not generally under a simple average of displacements measured at uniformly spaced stations. An initial velocity distribution \(v_0(x)\) projects onto mode \(n\) with coefficient \(\int\rho A v_0\phi_n\,dx/\int\rho A\phi_n^2\,dx\). In a rod with a heavy section, the same imposed velocity there stores more energy and carries more modal weight. An experiment interpreting raw camera amplitudes without line-density weighting can misassign energy among modes. Normalising each measured mode shape to unit maximum does not fix the mass weighting needed for energies.

Multiplying one eigenvalue equation by its own shape gives

\[\omega_n^2=\int EA(\phi_n')^2dx/\int\rho A\phi_n^2dx.\]

For an admissible trial function satisfying prescribed displacement conditions, the same quotient bounds the fundamental squared frequency from above when rigid translations are excluded. Consider a uniform fixed–free rod and trial \(\phi=x/L\). The numerator is \(EA/L\) and denominator \(\rho A L/3\), giving \(\omega_{\rm trial}=\sqrt3\,c/L\). The exact fundamental is \(\pi c/(2L)\), so this one-parameter shape overestimates frequency by about \(10.3\%\). It need not satisfy the free-end traction condition itself because that is a natural condition of the variational minimiser; it does need to satisfy the essential fixed-end displacement.

For a free–free rod the constant displacement mode has zero frequency. Using an arbitrary positive trial function without removing its mass-weighted mean can drive a quotient towards zero by adding a large constant. To estimate the first deforming mode impose orthogonality to the rigid translation, \(\int\rho A\phi\,dx=0\). This has an immediate mechanical interpretation: the deformation mode should not translate the centre of mass of an isolated rod. The null mode and its mass-weighted constraint reappear in numerical eigenproblems built from discretised stiffness and mass matrices.

Find where a discrete elastic chain ceases to look continuous

Shorten the wavelength relative to the lattice spacing. Compare the phase and group velocities with those inferred from a continuum action.

Open this experiment online

Track work and impulse in a one-dimensional pulse experiment

A finite displacement pulse provides a useful warning about integrating strain and velocity records. A right-going strain pulse of one sign can carry net momentum \(\int\rho A v\,dx\). Because \(v=-cu_x\), its integrated material momentum is \(-\rho Ac[u(+\infty)-u(-\infty)]\) at a fixed time. A localised displacement bump that returns to the same displacement on both sides has zero net ordinary material momentum, even though its energy is positive and travels to the right. Its strain and particle-velocity records contain positive and negative parts. A unipolar velocity pulse leaves behind a displacement step, produced by a source that has supplied net impulse.

For a right-moving rectangular velocity pulse of amplitude \(0.020\,{\rm m/s}\), duration \(100\,\mu{\rm s}\) in the worked rod, the local displacement jump measured in time is \(2.0\,\mu{\rm m}\). Its impulse magnitude across a section is \(Z v\Delta t=2.75\times10^{-3}\,{\rm N\,s}\), while its transmitted energy is

\[Zv^2\Delta t=55.0\,\mu{\rm J}.\]

A second equal pulse of opposite velocity sign launched later cancels the net displacement and material momentum but doubles the transmitted energy. Thus integrating a signed velocity signal and integrating its square distinguish different properties of the same experimental record.

A displacement sensor has a small linear drift during a burst. Which inferred observables are particularly sensitive?

Work through the solution

Differentiating adds a constant offset to particle velocity. Integrating that apparent velocity over a long window produces a growing spurious displacement jump and momentum estimate. Squaring it adds both a cross term with the physical velocity and a positive baseline energy. Measure a pre-pulse baseline, choose an integration interval covering the physical pulse, and test that the result is stable when the window is moderately extended. An independent strain gauge gives another characteristic combination; comparison with \(N=-Zv\) can expose drift or an overlapping reflection.

Can a finite-element rod model with a plausible first eigenfrequency still fail an impulse test?

Work through the solution

The first frequency mostly probes a long-wavelength weighted stiffness-to-mass ratio. A brief pulse contains higher modes and is sensitive to temporal resolution, dispersion, boundary tractions and the way discrete source work enters. Verify modal frequencies at several wavelengths, check the pulse's positive energy and signed impulse separately, and compare the measured incoming and reflected characteristic fluxes. A single standing-wave eigenvalue cannot validate all of those physically independent balances.

Recover a jump condition at an interface with a point mass

For a concentrated mass \(M_b\) joining two rods include \(M_bu_t(x_b)^2/2\) as a separate contribution to the Lagrangian. Its time variation contributes \(-M_bu_{tt}\delta u\) at the joint. The left and right rod boundary terms contribute respectively \(-N_1\delta u\) and \(+N_2\delta u\). Requiring stationarity for an independently varying shared displacement gives exactly \(N_2-N_1=M_bu_{tt}\). This derivation identifies both the sign and the constitutive assumption of a perfectly bonded, nonslipping joint.

For identical rods and a sinusoidal drive at angular frequency \(\omega\), the reflected velocity coefficient becomes \(r=-i\omega M_b/(2Z+i\omega M_b)\) under the stated phasor convention. At \(\omega M_b=2Z\) it reflects half the power and transmits half. The mass itself absorbs no cycle-averaged energy, though it stores kinetic energy during a cycle. For \(Z=100\,{\rm kg/s}\) and \(M_b=0.10\,{\rm kg}\), the equal-power frequency is about \(318\,{\rm Hz}\). A real joint that loses appreciable power at that frequency needs a damping or radiation term in addition to its measured mass.

Tutorial on stress waves

A gauge \(0.80\,{\rm m}\) from a free end records the echo of a sharp disturbance in the worked rod. What echo delay and strain sign should it expect?

Work through the solution

The extra path from gauge to end and back is \(1.60\,{\rm m}\), giving \(1.60/5092=0.314\,{\rm ms}\). A free end has zero net axial force, so the reflected force and strain have opposite signs to the incident ones. The velocity reflection keeps its sign. Finite pulse width and any change of material between gauge and end can obscure that simple result.

A bonded step has measured reflected velocity coefficient \(-0.20\) and no detectable dissipation. Infer its impedance ratio and transmitted energy fraction.

Work through the solution

Rearranging \(r_v=(1-r)/(1+r)\) gives

\[r=Z_2/Z_1=(1-r_v)/(1+r_v)=1.50.\]

The energy reflection is \(0.04\) and transmission \(0.96\). The velocity transmission is \(0.80\), whose square alone is only \(0.64\). Multiplying by the transmitted-to-incident impedance ratio recovers the correct energy fraction.

A rod model with a prescribed displacement at its right end reports nonzero work there, even though the variational boundary term vanished in deriving its equation. Is there a contradiction?

Work through the solution

Vanishing of \(N\delta u\) follows because the allowed virtual displacement at a prescribed boundary is zero. Actual boundary velocity can be nonzero when the prescribed displacement varies in time. The actuator supplies power \(Nu_t\) at the right boundary. Compare the time-integrated input with the change of stored energy plus any losses or outgoing energy rather than identifying an admissible variation with actual displacement.

Compare a finite chain with its long-wavelength continuum.

Fließbach includes string vibrations, beam bending and sound in his continuum mechanics treatment.

Derive quantum occupations from particle counting. Use them to predict responses of electrons, acoustic modes and photons. Look for the boundary between a one-particle and a collective description.

Partition functions and thermal fluctuations

Thermodynamics and statistical physics

Calculate the occupation, heat capacity and energy fluctuations of a two-level system from a single partition function.

From first principles

Obtain Boltzmann weights by counting the bath’s available states

Put a small system in weak thermal contact with a much larger bath. Treat the combined pair as isolated with energy \(E_{\rm tot}\). If the small system occupies a particular microstate \(i\) of energy \(E_i\), the bath must have energy \(E_{\rm tot}-E_i\). The probability of that microstate is proportional to the number of bath states compatible with the remaining energy,

\[P_i\propto\Omega_{\rm bath}(E_{\rm tot}-E_i)=\exp[S_{\rm bath}(E_{\rm tot}-E_i)/k_B].\]

A large bath changes temperature negligibly over the small system’s range of exchanged energies. Expand its entropy to first order, using \(\partial S_{\rm bath}/\partial E=1/T\); an arbitrary energy reference contributes only a common factor. Normalizing the resulting probabilities gives

\[P_i=\frac{e^{-\beta E_i}}{Z},\]
\[Z=\sum_j e^{-\beta E_j},\]
\[\beta=\frac1{k_BT}.\]

The sum called the partition function counts every microstate, so equal-energy states contribute separately. Differentiating it yields the mean energy \(U=-\partial_\beta\ln Z\) when the energy levels are temperature independent. A constant shift of all levels changes \(Z\) and \(U\) but leaves the normalized probabilities unchanged.

Background · Inverse temperature
\[\beta=1/(k_BT)\]

It is conjugate to energy in a canonical weight and has units of reciprocal energy. Temperatures in Boltzmann factors are absolute.

Derivation and applications

Measure a static response from spontaneous fluctuations

Consider a coordinate \(x\) that can exchange energy with a heat bath at fixed temperature \(T\). A weak constant force \(f\) changes its energy from \(H_0\) to \(H_f=H_0-fx\). This sign gives lower energy to positive displacement under a positive force. Assume that the accessible states themselves do not depend on the force and that the instrument allows enough time for the system to sample equilibrium. Write \(d\Gamma\) for a sum or integral over states and \(\beta=1/(k_BT)\). Differentiation of the Boltzmann weight gives

\[Z(f)=\int e^{-\beta(H_0-fx)}d\Gamma,\]
\[\frac{\partial\ln Z}{\partial f}=\beta\langle x\rangle_f .\]

A second derivative acts on both the weighted numerator for the mean and its normalization. The quotient rule subtracts the square of the mean. Hence the static compliance, measured with temperature held fixed, obeys

\[\left(\frac{\partial\langle x\rangle_f}{\partial f}\right)_T=\beta\left(\langle x^2\rangle_f-\langle x\rangle_f^2\right).\]

For a harmonic confining potential \(H_0=kx^2/2\), with spring constant \(k>0\), completing the square makes the interpretation especially direct. The position distribution remains Gaussian and its centre shifts with the load,

\[\tfrac12kx^2-fx=\tfrac12 k(x-f/k)^2-\frac{f^2}{2k},\]
\[\langle x\rangle_f=\frac{f}{k},\]
\[\operatorname{var}x=\frac{k_BT}{k}.\]

The same stiffness controls response and equilibrium noise. For an anharmonic potential the general variance identity still gives the local response at the current force, although the compliance can change at larger loads. A finite exposure time can average away rapid motion, so measured positions must resolve the fluctuating coordinate.

Worked example

Calibrate a bead trap at thermal equilibrium

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A bead suspended in a harmonic optical trap at \(295\,\mathrm K\) has a measured one-coordinate standard deviation of \(40.0\,\mathrm{nm}\). Its stiffness is

\[k=k_BT/(40.0\times10^{-9})^2=2.55\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

A steady force of \(0.100\,\mathrm{pN}\) should shift the mean by \(39.3\,\mathrm{nm}\). Resolving such a shift requires averaging over many statistically independent positions.

Second application

Predict noise from a measured mechanical compliance

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A second device near a fixed bias force responds to an extra \(0.500\,\mathrm{pN}\) with a mean displacement of \(10.0\,\mathrm{nm}\). Its local compliance is \(2.00\times10^4\,\mathrm{m/N}\). At \(310\,\mathrm K\) the expected displacement standard deviation is

\[\sqrt{k_BT(2.00\times10^4)}=9.25\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

This inference uses only local linear response and thermal equilibrium, so it remains applicable even if the device is nonlinear over a larger displacement range.

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Put a small system in a heat bath

A spectroscopic probe may resolve two energy levels even when a bulk thermometer gives only one temperature. Consider many independent units with ground energy zero and excitation energy \(\Delta>0\). Each unit has two nondegenerate states and is weakly coupled to a much larger bath at temperature \(T\). Weak coupling lets us assign energies to the unit while still permitting energy exchange over the measurement time.

Under the canonical distribution the probability of a microstate \(j\) is \(p_j=e^{-\beta E_j}/Z\), with \(\beta=1/(k_BT)\). The factor \(Z=\sum_j e^{-\beta E_j}\) normalises probabilities. This form comes from expanding the bath entropy about its energy before the exchange: the number of bath states compatible with system energy \(E_j\) is proportional to \(\exp[-E_j/(k_BT)]\). A bath with appreciable temperature change after one exchange would call for a finite-reservoir correction.

Differentiate the normalisation

The mean energy is \(\sum_j p_j E_j\). Differentiating \(\ln Z\) with respect to \(\beta\) inserts a factor \(-E_j\) in the weighted sum, giving \(\langle E\rangle=-\partial_\beta\ln Z\). Differentiating a second time produces \(\langle E^2\rangle-\langle E\rangle^2\), which is the energy variance. Changing variables from \(\beta\) to \(T\) produces the fluctuation relation

\[C=\frac{\partial\langle E\rangle}{\partial T}=\frac{\langle E^2\rangle-\langle E\rangle^2}{k_BT^2}.\]

Hold fixed the external parameters that set the energy levels while taking this derivative. A change of magnetic field or volume can shift the levels and introduce an additional energy response. For independent identically prepared units, variances add. The standard deviation of total energy grows as the square root of the number of units, while its mean grows in proportion to that number. Relative fluctuations consequently shrink as a macroscopic sample is assembled.
