Magnetic moments and susceptibility

Condensed matter and band theory

Derive thermal magnetisation for a two-state moment and distinguish its signature from the response of a filled electronic sea.

From first principles

Obtain the Curie law by counting two magnetic orientations

Take many identical, independent localized moments per volume \(n\), each with component \(+\mu\) or \(-\mu\) along an applied induction \(B\). The magnetic energies are \(-\mu B\) and \(+\mu B\). At temperature \(T\) their probabilities are proportional to their Boltzmann factors. With \(x=\mu B/(k_BT)\), normalize the two weights and subtract their contributions to the mean:

\[Z=e^x+e^{-x},\]
\[\langle m_z\rangle=\mu\frac{e^x-e^{-x}}{e^x+e^{-x}}=\mu\tanh x .\]

Multiply by number density to get magnetization. In weak fields or at high temperatures \(x\ll1\), so the leading term in \(\tanh x\) gives

\[M\simeq\frac{n\mu^2 B}{k_BT},\]
\[\chi=\frac{\partial M}{\partial H}\simeq\frac{\mu_0 n\mu^2}{k_BT}.\]

The susceptibility expression uses a weakly magnetic sample with \(B\simeq\mu_0 H\) and negligible demagnetizing correction. At low temperature and fixed nonzero field both the expansion and the \(1/T\) extrapolation fail. The exact two-state average saturates at \(n\mu\) because no moment can contribute more than \(\mu\). Exchange between neighbours can also invalidate the independent weights.

Derivation and applications

Distinguish the saturation moment from the Curie moment

An ion with angular momentum quantum number \(J\) can have more than two projections on a magnetic field. Consider independent identical ions of number density \(n\) in thermal equilibrium, with isotropic Landé factor \(g\) and negligible splitting at zero field. Label the levels \(m=-J,\ldots,J\) so that their moments along the applied induction \(B\) are \(g\mu_Bm\). Adjacent Zeeman levels are then separated by \(g\mu_B B\). Introduce \(x=g\mu_BB/(k_BT)\). Weighing each level with its Boltzmann factor gives

\[Z=\sum_{m=-J}^{J}e^{mx},\]
\[M=ng\mu_B\,\frac{\partial\ln Z}{\partial x}.\]

In weak fields each zero-field state is almost equally likely. The zeroth-order sum of \(m\) vanishes by symmetry. The next term in the numerator is \(x\sum m^2\), while the denominator is \(2J+1\) to this order. Using the sum of squares of the allowed projections gives

\[\langle m\rangle\simeq x\frac{\sum_{m=-J}^J m^2}{2J+1}=\frac{J(J+1)}{3}x .\]

For a dilute specimen take \(B\simeq\mu_0H\). The dimensionless volume susceptibility is the derivative of \(M\) with respect to magnetic field strength \(H\). In strong fields the highest projection alone survives. These limits identify two different combinations of the microscopic parameters,

\[\chi=\frac{\mu_0 n(g\mu_B)^2J(J+1)}{3k_BT},\]
\[M_{\rm sat}=ng\mu_B J .\]

The Curie-law effective moment is \(g\mu_B\sqrt{J(J+1)}\), whereas the aligned projection is \(g\mu_BJ\). Their ratio approaches one for large \(J\). A susceptibility fit requires \(Jx\ll1\); a saturation measurement requires the opposite tendency and sufficiently weak interactions between moments.

Worked example

Predict the response of dilute spin-one ions

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Take \(J=1\), \(g=2.00\),

\[n=1.00\times10^{26}\,\mathrm{m^{-3}},\]
\[T=10.0\,\mathrm K.\]

The aligned magnetization is \(1.85\times10^3\,\mathrm{A/m}\). The Curie formula gives \(\chi=2.09\times10^{-4}\). At \(B=0.100\,\mathrm T\), \(Jx=0.0134\), and the predicted magnetization is only \(\chi B/\mu_0=16.6\,\mathrm{A/m}\), consistent with weak polarization.

Second application

Identify a moment from two calibrated measurements

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Suppose independent concentration measurements in another material yield a saturation moment of \(3.00\mu_B\) per ion and an effective Curie moment of \(3.87\mu_B\). The squared ratio is \(1+1/J=(3.87/3.00)^2=1.66\), giving \(J\simeq1.50\). The saturation value then gives \(g\simeq2.00\). Susceptibility alone fixes only a product of the factor \(g\) and the size of the angular momentum.

Try it · Spin magnetisation

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

A moment has an energy as well as a direction

Consider \(N\) independent identical magnetic moments in volume \(V\), each with allowed projection \(\pm\mu\) along a uniform applied induction \(B\). The energies are \(\mp\mu B\); the state aligned with the field has lower energy. For \(\beta=1/(k_BT)\) the one-moment partition function is \(Z_1=2\cosh(\beta\mu B)\). From \(\langle m_z\rangle=k_BT\,\partial_B\ln Z_1\) obtain

\[M=n\mu\tanh(\mu B/k_BT),\]
\[n=N/V .\]

The full curve approaches \(n\mu\) at high field or low temperature. In the weak-field regime \(\mu B\ll k_BT\), expansion of the hyperbolic tangent gives

\[M\simeq n\mu^2 B/(k_BT).\]

Under weak magnetisation and negligible demagnetising corrections use \(B\simeq\mu_0H\); the dimensionless SI susceptibility \(\partial M/\partial H\) is then

\[\chi=\mu_0n\mu^2/(k_BT).\]

Be explicit about differentiating with respect to \(B\) or \(H\), which carry different units.

Work a moment count from two measurements

Suppose a dilute two-state sample exhibits saturation magnetisation \(9.27\times10^3\) A/m and the moments are known to have \(\mu=\mu_B=9.27\times10^{-24}\) J/T. The implied density is \(1.00\times10^{27}\) m⁻³. At 10 K and 1 T the argument \(\mu B/k_BT\) is 0.0672, safely in the linear range for a rough estimate: \(M\simeq622\) A/m. At the same temperature a field of about 14.9 T gives argument one and \(M/M_\mathrm{sat}=0.762\). Fitting a straight line up to that field would underestimate the zero-field slope.

If the moment magnitude is unknown, saturation determines \(n\mu\) and the small-field slope supplies \(n\mu^2\). Taken together they can separate density from moment within this model. A mixture of moment species, orbital contributions or interactions can defeat this two-equation inference. Plotting curves at several temperatures against \(B/T\) tests the independent-moment scaling without needing to know the overall density beforehand.

Compare with itinerant spin response

For a weakly perturbed degenerate metal, the two spin species shift by \(\mp\mu B\). Almost all states below \(E_F\) remain doubly occupied, so only a thin shell at the Fermi surface changes its net spin. At low temperature and nearly constant total density of states \(D_V(E_F)\), the spin imbalance is approximately \(D_V(E_F)\mu B\), giving \(M\simeq\mu^2D_V(E_F)B\). The leading Pauli response is nearly temperature independent, in contrast to the \(1/T\) scaling of independent localised moments. This argument addresses spin susceptibility; orbital diamagnetism also contributes to a real measured susceptibility.

Diagnose a small impurity contribution

If bulk data show a roughly constant offset plus a low-temperature upturn, test a simple additive model \(\chi=\chi_0+C/T\) over a weak-field interval. Plot \(\chi\) against \(1/T\) rather than taking an inverse before subtracting the offset. An upturn can arise from a dilute impurity population even when the host electronic sea dominates heat capacity. If scaling fails systematically, examine interactions between moments; the next chapter shows how cooperative ordering changes the small-field susceptibility.

Cool local moments by adiabatic demagnetization

Independent moments have an entropy that depends on the ratio of magnetic field to temperature. For a two-state moment of magnitude \(\mu\), energies are \(\mp\mu B\). Define \(x=\mu B/(k_BT)\). The one-moment partition function is \(2\cosh x\), average energy is \(-\mu B\tanh x\), and the magnetic entropy follows from \(S=k_B\ln Z+U/T\),

\[\frac{S_{\rm spin}}{Nk_B}=\ln(2\cosh x)-x\tanh x .\]

At zero field for fixed positive temperature the moment has two equiprobable orientations and contributes \(k_B\ln2\). At large \(x\) nearly all moments favour the lower state and the spin entropy tends to zero. If field is increased at fixed temperature, this reduction of spin entropy expels heat to a bath. Now isolate the material and reduce the field quasistatically. In the ideal spin-dominated limit entropy remains fixed, so \(x\) remains fixed and \(T/B\) is constant.

Thermal isolation and sufficiently slow variation are both required. The first prevents external heat from entering, and the second allows internal degrees of freedom to follow equilibrium. Other stores of entropy, particularly lattice vibrations or mobile electrons, participate in an actual specimen. The pure spin scaling can then be modified even when the process as a whole is perfectly adiabatic.

Estimate temperature and cooling capacity

Take \(N=1.00\times10^{21}\) independent moments each of magnitude \(\mu_B\), initially at 2.00 K. Magnetize isothermally from negligible field to 3.00 T, using \(\mu_B/k_B=0.6717\) K/T. The final magnetic parameter is \(x=1.01\), giving spin entropy per particle about \(0.362k_B\) compared with the initial \(0.693k_B\). The heat passed to the bath in that reversible isothermal step is \(T N k_B(0.693-0.362)=9.14\) mJ. Work is supplied by the field source during preparation.

Isolate and reduce field from 3.00 T to 0.300 T. Holding \(x\) fixed predicts

\[T_f=2.00(0.300/3.00)=0.200\,\mathrm{K}.\]

Further field reduction in the ideal noninteracting equations would approach zero temperature. Real moments interact, however, and internal effective fields or ordering lift their near-zero-field degeneracy. Thermal leaks and finite relaxation rates also become increasingly important. A practical test records temperature against field on several ramp rates; dependence on the rate can distinguish irreversible heating from a reproducible correction to the equilibrium entropy model. The final cold sample can absorb only finite heat before its moments become disordered, even if the predicted endpoint temperature is very low.

A second check comes from magnetization along the ideal isolated sweep. Since \(M=N\mu\tanh x\) at fixed volume and \(x\) is conserved, the spin magnetization should stay almost constant while both the external field and the temperature fall. In contrast, an isothermal field decrease reduces \(x\) and magnetization. Measuring temperature alone cannot establish that the sample really followed an isentropic spin path. A simultaneous magnetic reading tests the fixed-population interpretation. Deviations concentrated at the weakest fields can signal interactions between moments rather than a general failure of the high-field entropy estimate.

Pairing, excitation gaps and what a spectrum measures

Condensed matter and band theory

Use a two-component excitation model to predict spectroscopic signatures, then distinguish three physically different gaps encountered across the course.

From first principles

See how pairing opens a spectroscopic gap

Begin with a single normal-state band and let \(\xi\) be its energy measured from the chemical potential. A minimal mean-field model for a uniform paired state mixes an electron amplitude with a hole amplitude through a complex pairing energy \(\Delta\). The two diagonal energies have opposite signs. To find its stationary excitations write the eigenvalue condition:

\[H=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&\Delta\\\Delta^*&-\xi\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det(H-EI)=(\xi-E)(-\xi-E)-|\Delta|^2=0 .\]

It follows that the two branches are \(E_\pm=\pm\sqrt{\xi^2+|\Delta|^2}\). At the former Fermi surface \(\xi=0\), even the cheapest positive excitation costs \(|\Delta|\). To predict a tunnelling spectrum for constant isotropic gap and nearly constant normal density of states, count the normal states mapping into an energy interval \(dE\). The Jacobian from \(\xi=\pm\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}\) gives the normalized density, with the electron and hole weights combined:

\[\frac{D_s(E)}{D_n(0)}=\frac{|E|}{\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}}\quad (|E|>|\Delta|).\]

There are no states within the ideal gap at this level of description. Finite temperature, lifetime broadening and instrumental resolution change the measured conductance near its edges. The model assumes a nonzero \(\Delta\); it predicts consequences of pairing without deriving the attractive interaction that supports it.

Derivation and applications

Predict the weights of a particle peak and its partner

A pairing gap determines excitation energies, but a momentum-resolved spectrum also measures how strongly an electron couples to each peak. Consider a clean, uniform superconductor with a real positive gap \(\Delta\), at temperature low enough to neglect thermal broadening. Measure the normal-state energy \(\xi\) at one wavevector from the chemical potential. In the electron and hole basis the positive-energy eigenvector has components \(u,v\). The first row of its eigenvalue equation requires

\[(\xi-E)u+\Delta v=0,\]
\[\frac{v}{u}=\frac{E-\xi}{\Delta},\]
\[E=\sqrt{\xi^2+\Delta^2}.\]

Normalize the vector to unit probability. Since \((E-\xi)(E+\xi)=\Delta^2\), the squared amplitude ratio can be written without subtracting two nearly equal energies. The resulting coherence factors are

\[|u|^2=\frac{1}{1+(E-\xi)/(E+\xi)}=\frac12\left(1+\frac{\xi}{E}\right),\]
\[|v|^2=\frac12\left(1-\frac{\xi}{E}\right).\]

The negative-energy eigenvector is orthogonal to the positive one and consequently has electron weight \(|v|^2\). In the ideal single-particle spectral function at this momentum the two sharp peaks have unit total weight,

\[A(\epsilon)=|u|^2\delta(\epsilon-E)+|v|^2\delta(\epsilon+E),\]
\[\int A(\epsilon)\,d\epsilon=1 .\]

Thus the weights exchange as the wavevector passes through the normal Fermi surface. At \(\xi=0\) the two weights are equal. Well above the surface the positive-energy peak carries almost all the weight. To compare both sides quantitatively an experiment must account for occupation and matrix elements; ordinary photoemission alone samples the occupied side most directly.

Worked example

Resolve a weak partner peak

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

At a selected momentum let \(\xi=3.00\,\mathrm{meV}\) and \(\Delta=4.00\,\mathrm{meV}\). The spectral energies are \(+5.00\) and \(-5.00\,\mathrm{meV}\), with respective electron weights \(0.800\) and \(0.200\). At a momentum with the opposite \(\xi\) and the same gap the two peak energies stay fixed while their weights reverse. Such a weight exchange tests particle–hole mixing more specifically than the peak separation alone.

Second application

Reconstruct a gap using corrected peak areas

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

In a different sample the peaks are separated by \(6.00\,\mathrm{meV}\), and corrected electron spectral areas have positive-to-negative ratio \(3:2\). Their normalized weights are \(0.600\) and \(0.400\), so

\[\xi/E=0.200.\]

With \(E=3.00\,\mathrm{meV}\) this gives \(\xi=0.600\,\mathrm{meV}\) and \(\Delta=\sqrt{3.00^2-0.600^2}=2.94\,\mathrm{meV}\). Half the separation overestimates the gap here because the probed momentum lies away from the normal Fermi surface.

Try it · Bcs weights

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Build excitations on a paired state

For a conventional fully gapped superconductor, a mean-field description mixes particle and hole amplitudes near the Fermi surface. With \(\xi_k=\epsilon_k-\mu\) and gap parameter \(\Delta\), a simplified matrix for a time-reversed pair of states is

\[\begin{pmatrix}\xi_k&\Delta\\\Delta^*&-\xi_k\end{pmatrix}\binom{u_k}{v_k}=E\binom{u_k}{v_k},\qquad E_\pm=\pm\sqrt{\xi_k^2+|\Delta|^2}.\]

A positive-energy quasiparticle has minimum energy \(|\Delta|\) where the normal band crossed the chemical potential. Breaking a pair into two excitations can cost at least \(2|\Delta|\). These are different thresholds, so the probe and transition being discussed must be specified. The matrix displays level repulsion, reminiscent of the nearly free electron calculation, but its basis states and the physical origin of the coupling are different.

Transform a density of states

Suppose the normal-state density is approximately constant over the gap scale and the gap is isotropic. For \(E>|\Delta|\),

\[|\xi|=\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2},\]
\[d|\xi|/dE=E/\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}.\]

Counting the paired branches relative to the normal-state convention yields \(D_s(E)/D_n=|E|/\sqrt{E^2-|\Delta|^2}\) outside the gap and zero inside in the ideal zero-broadening model. The edge singularity is integrable. States removed from the gap reappear near its edges. Thermal broadening, disorder, nodes or an anisotropic gap can change the measured shape.

A low-temperature tunnel junction with a normal counterelectrode of nearly constant density can use differential conductance as a probe of this spectrum, subject to matrix-element and broadening effects. Peaks at biases \(\pm1.5\) mV suggest \(|\Delta|\simeq1.5\) meV in this geometry. Peak separation on the voltage axis is 3.0 mV. Using the weak-coupling BCS ratio \(2|\Delta(0)|/(k_BT_c)\simeq3.53\) gives \(T_c\simeq9.86\) K. Measure \(T_c\) independently; agreement with this estimate supports the simple model, while deviation calls for closer investigation.

Select a second observation that distinguishes the mechanism

A nearly free electron band gap separates eigenvalues near a zone boundary. For conduction it also matters where the chemical potential sits. A superconducting gap opens at the Fermi surface on entering an ordered state, and has magnetic screening and a characteristic temperature dependence. A photonic stop band instead excludes real propagating wavevectors in a frequency range for the specified periodic optical medium. All three can produce missing spectral weight or reduced transmission. Their energies, frequency units, occupation rules and control parameters differ.

Return to the numerical optical stack with a prediction: making both layers identical closes its first stop band even though the artificial zone boundary remains. For a proposed superconducting gap, repeat the tunnelling and magnetisation measurements as temperature crosses the transition. For an electronic band edge, relate spectroscopy to filling, dispersion and transport. This combination of an analytic limit, a controlled change and an independent observable is what makes an interpretation of a spectrum persuasive.

For continued study, see the MIT lectures on Theory of Solids, including quasiparticles and superconducting electrodynamics.

Read the phase difference across a superconducting weak link

A single-particle tunnelling spectrum can reveal an excitation gap. A weak junction between two superconductors can also carry a coherent pair current that responds directly to their relative condensate phase. Use the gauge-invariant phase difference \(\varphi\), including the vector-potential line integral across the junction. For a simple tunnel junction in the low-transparency regime, its leading coupling energy is

\[U_J=-E_J\cos\varphi.\]

Its \(2\pi\) periodicity reflects invariance under an integer phase winding and the energy minimum at aligned phase for a conventional junction.

A potential difference \(V\) between the condensates causes their relative phase to advance at \(\dot\varphi=2eV/\hbar\), choosing a consistent orientation of current and voltage. Combining electrical power \(IV\) with the rate of change of junction energy gives \(IV=E_J\sin\varphi\,\dot\varphi\), hence

\[I=I_c\sin\varphi,\]
\[I_c=\frac{2eE_J}{\hbar}.\]

Under a steady subcritical current the phase can remain constant while \(V=0\). At constant nonzero voltage the phase winds and the ideal coherent current oscillates with frequency \(f_J=2e|V|/h\). These two statements distinguish a DC pair current with no terminal voltage from an AC pair response under bias. A real junction includes quasiparticle conduction and capacitance, so its observed total current can contain additional components.

Convert a microwave frequency into a voltage

A junction biased at 5.00 μV has Josephson oscillation frequency \(f_J=(2e/h)V=2.42\) GHz. Illuminate the junction with a stable microwave reference of frequency 10.0 GHz. If its mean phase advance locks to one full winding per reference cycle, then \(\langle\dot\varphi\rangle=2\pi f_{\rm ref}\), requiring mean voltage \(hf_{\rm ref}/(2e)=20.7\) μV. Locking to two windings per cycle gives 41.4 μV. Such voltage steps probe phase dynamics and the charge associated with coherent transfer.

Suppose the same device has measured critical current \(I_c=30.0\) nA at the operating temperature. Its coupling energy is \(\hbar I_c/(2e)=9.87\times10^{-24}\) J, corresponding to \(E_J/k_B=0.715\) K. At a bath temperature of 50 mK thermal energy is appreciably smaller, although phase fluctuations can still depend on capacitance and the surrounding circuit. If either electrode is driven normal, the equilibrium phase-link energy disappears and the ideal supercurrent relation ceases to apply. Observing quantized phase locking and an excitation gap therefore accesses two different signatures of the superconducting state; their characteristic frequencies need not be numerically identical.

The two Josephson relations yield a further calibration test that does not require measuring energy directly. At fixed microwave frequency, the voltage separation of successive locking steps is \(hf_{\rm ref}/(2e)\) independently of junction area in the ideal phase model. Increasing junction area can change total critical current and the visibility or width of current intervals over which locking persists, yet should leave the quantized voltage spacing fixed. Looking at spacing and current width separately distinguishes uncertainty in the reference frequency from a change in the junction coupling or its electromagnetic environment.

Estimate a spectral gap when peaks have finite width

Add a phenomenological lifetime broadening to the isotropic quasiparticle density of states by evaluating \(z/\sqrt{z^2-1}\) at \(z=(E+i\Gamma)/\Delta\) and taking the physical nonnegative real density. Hold the gap itself fixed in all three runs.

Why a polar crystal has two optical phonon frequencies

Condensed matter and band theory

A uniform relative displacement of positive and negative ions creates an electric polarisation. A longitudinal vibration induces a macroscopic restoring field while a transverse displacement has a different electrical boundary condition. An infrared spectrum can reveal both frequencies.

Separate the ionic and electronic contributions

For one infrared-active optical mode let \(u\) be relative displacement per primitive cell, \(M\) the effective reduced mass, \(Z^*\) the effective charge in coulombs, and \(N\) the number of cells per volume. The oscillator equation driven by the macroscopic field is

\[M(\ddot u+\gamma\dot u+\omega_{\rm TO}^2u)=Z^*E.\]

The zero-macroscopic-field stiffness defines the transverse optical frequency in this oscillator model. Bound electronic response at these frequencies supplies a real relative background permittivity \(\epsilon_\infty\). The ionic polarisation is \(P_{\rm ion}=NZ^*u\), so \(D=\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty E+P_{\rm ion}\).

With time convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\), solving for \(u\) gives

\[\epsilon_r(\omega)=\epsilon_\infty+\frac{S}{\omega_{\rm TO}^2-\omega^2-i\gamma\omega},\]
\[S=\frac{N(Z^*)^2}{\epsilon_0 M}.\]

The signed effective charge appears twice, once as drive and once in the dipole density. Its square therefore sets oscillator strength. At zero frequency the ions can follow the field and \(\epsilon_s=\epsilon_\infty+S/\omega_{\rm TO}^2\). Far above the phonon resonance but below electronic absorption the ionic term is small, making \(\epsilon_\infty\) the observed plateau.

Apply Gauss's law to a longitudinal vibration

Consider a long-wavelength, nonzero wavevector \(\boldsymbol k\) with no external free charge. Gauss's law gives

\[\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol D=0.\]

For a longitudinal field \(D_L=0\), so the ions create \(E_L=-NZ^*u/(\epsilon_0\epsilon_\infty)\). Substituting this opposing field into the undamped oscillator equation adds a restoring term. Thus

\[\omega_{\rm LO}^2=\omega_{\rm TO}^2+S/\epsilon_\infty,\]
\[\frac{\omega_{\rm LO}^2}{\omega_{\rm TO}^2}=\frac{\epsilon_s}{\epsilon_\infty}.\]

Equivalently the longitudinal collective mode is the zero of the lossless dielectric function. The transverse optical frequency is its pole. For a single isotropic mode this is the Lyddane–Sachs–Teller relation. With several infrared modes, anisotropy, damping or mobile free carriers, assigning one measured peak and one dielectric ratio to a single oscillator needs further justification.

Connect a reflectance band with the two modes

In the lossless limit rewrite \(\epsilon_r=\epsilon_\infty(\omega_{\rm LO}^2-\omega^2)/(\omega_{\rm TO}^2-\omega^2)\). Between the two frequencies this is negative. A transverse electromagnetic wave satisfies \(k^2=\epsilon_r\omega^2/c^2\), so its wavenumber becomes imaginary there in a nonmagnetic lossless crystal. At a planar vacuum boundary a semi-infinite crystal therefore has unit ideal reflectance over this interval. Finite damping makes the refractive index complex and allows absorption, smoothing the edges. The two mode frequencies have been deduced from mechanical response and electric boundary conditions; an electromagnetic wave interacting with the lattice is a coupled phonon–polariton excitation.

For \(f_{\rm TO}=6.0\,\mathrm{THz}\), \(\epsilon_\infty=4\) and \(\epsilon_s=9\), \(f_{\rm LO}=9.0\,\mathrm{THz}\). At 7.5 THz in the lossless limit \(\epsilon_r=4(81-56.25)/(36-56.25)=-4.89\). Below the mode, normal-incidence static-index reflectance is

\[[(\sqrt9-1)/(\sqrt9+1)]^2=0.25.\]

Above the phonon plateau the corresponding background reflectance approaches \(1/9\). The interactive plot evaluates the passive complex refractive index and the exact single-interface reflectance. Change the static permittivity and damping; compare the high-reflection interval with the lossless zero and pole.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on recovering mode parameters

Optical data give \(f_{\rm TO}=8.0\) THz and \(f_{\rm LO}=10.0\) THz for a single weakly damped mode. With \(\epsilon_\infty=5.0\), estimate the static permittivity and dielectric increment.

Work through the solution
\[\epsilon_s=5(10/8)^2=7.8125.\]

The increment attributed to the optical mode is

\[7.8125-5=2.8125.\]

Angular and cyclic frequency ratios are equal, although an absolute oscillator strength \(S\) requires angular frequencies in the equation of motion.

At a fixed wavevector a probe sees displacement perpendicular to the wavevector. Why is the longitudinal restoring term absent in the electrostatic argument, and what additional coupling can still occur?

Work through the solution

Its ionic polarisation has \(\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol P_{\rm ion}=0\), hence it creates no longitudinal bulk bound charge \(-i\boldsymbol k\cdot\boldsymbol P_{\rm ion}\). There is no obligatory depolarising longitudinal field. A transverse propagating electromagnetic field can still drive the infrared-active vibration, as encoded in \(\epsilon_r(\omega)\).

Further reading · MIT solid state physics, optical phonons and dielectric response.

Thermal radiance, étendue and detector calibration

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A square millimetre of a heated object emits thermal radiation into an entire hemisphere. A detector sees only the rays accepted by its optics and only wavelengths to which it responds. Derive the equilibrium spectrum, follow it through an optical collection system and infer what temperature or emissivity a calibrated reading can actually determine.

Count optical states in a large equilibrium cavity

Take a cubic airless cavity of volume \(V\) in thermal equilibrium at temperature \(T\). For its bulk mode density we can use periodic boundary conditions. Allowed wavevectors are separated by \(2\pi/L\) in each coordinate. Count a spherical shell in wavevector space, divide by the volume per state and include two independent transverse polarisations. Substituting \(k=2\pi\nu/c\) gives

\[g(\nu)d\nu/V=8\pi\nu^2d\nu/c^3.\]

Using standing waves in an ideally reflecting large box gives the same leading volume density when directions and polarisations are counted consistently.

One oscillator mode of frequency \(\nu\) can hold \(m=0,1,2,\ldots\) photons. Its thermal occupation has probabilities proportional to \(e^{-mh\nu/k_BT}\). Summing the geometric series and taking its logarithmic derivative gives

\[\langle m\rangle=1/(e^{h\nu/k_BT}-1).\]

Multiplying by the density of modes and energy per photon gives spectral energy density \(u_\nu\). The zero-point contribution of a harmonic oscillator is not part of the thermal power exchanged between these black surfaces. At low frequency the result approaches an energy \(k_BT\) per mode; extending that classical limit to arbitrarily high frequency would make the integrated power diverge.

An isotropic distribution of energy travelling at speed \(c\) produces a normal flux through a small aperture weighted by the direction cosine. Per unit solid angle its radiance is \(B_\nu=cu_\nu/(4\pi)\). Transform to equal wavelength intervals using \(B_\lambda d\lambda=B_\nu|d\nu|\). The two useful spectral forms are

\[B_\nu=\frac{2h\nu^3}{c^2}\frac1{e^{h\nu/k_BT}-1},\]
\[B_\lambda=\frac{2hc^2}{\lambda^5}\frac1{e^{hc/(\lambda k_BT)}-1}.\]

The wavelength expression has units of watts per square metre per steradian per metre of wavelength. To express it per micrometre multiply by one millionth. At 400 K and 10 micrometres the result is 33.56 W per square metre per steradian per micrometre. At 295 K it is 9.14 in the same units. Equal wavelength and equal frequency bins carry different widths, so their distributions reach their maxima at different converted wavelengths. A spectrum's peak should always name its horizontal variable and spectral-density convention.

Distinguish emitter power from brightness in a chosen direction

For a black surface with angle-independent radiance into its outward hemisphere, integrate \(\cos\theta\,d\Omega\). The result is \(\pi\), not \(2\pi\). The emitted spectral power per unit area is \(\pi B_\lambda\). Integrate over frequency using \(x=h\nu/(k_BT)\). The remaining integral \(\int_0^\infty x^3/(e^x-1)\,dx=\pi^4/15\) follows by expanding \(1/(e^x-1)\) as a sum of exponentials and integrating term by term. This yields Stefan–Boltzmann exitance \(M=\sigma T^4\) and identifies the constant \(\sigma=2\pi^5k_B^4/(15h^3c^2)\).

A black patch of 1.0 square millimetre at 400 K emits about 1.45 mW into its entire outward hemisphere over all wavelengths. The same patch at 295 K emits about 0.429 mW. Replacing one by the other changes all-wavelength hemispherical power by about 1.02 mW. This is not the power a small infrared receiver sees through a narrow filter. The integrated emitter number is valuable for energy conservation, but by itself contains no receiver area, direction, passband or optical transmission.

The surface spectrum of a body in thermal equilibrium has directional spectral emissivity equal to its absorptivity in the corresponding reciprocal optical modes under the usual reciprocal-material conditions. An opaque diffuse grey surface with emissivity \(\epsilon\) in an isotropically illuminated background can be modelled by \(\epsilon B_\lambda(T)+(1-\epsilon)B_\lambda(T_{\rm bg})\). It emits less than a black surface but also reflects background. A shiny object viewed in a warm room may consequently display a radiance temperature nearer the reflected scene than its own physical temperature. Specular surfaces require explicit directional knowledge of that scene.

Derive the geometric acceptance without counting the angle twice

An area element \(dA\) normal to the collection axis emits into \(d\Omega\) at angle \(\theta\) a spectral power \(B_\lambda\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega\). For a uniformly filled cone of half-angle \(\alpha\) and a uniform patch of area \(A\), integrating gives \(A\pi\sin^2\alpha\). This is its geometrical throughput in air when there is no vignetting. Optical étendue more generally uses \(n^2dA\cos\theta\,d\Omega\). A lossless passive optical relay in geometrical optics preserves this phase-space measure; correspondingly \(L/n^2\) is conserved along a ray in transparent media under the usual stationary conditions.

A direct lens example makes the restriction tangible. An image relay enlarging a uniformly bright patch by a factor of two in each transverse direction forms an image four times the area. In a lossless paraxial design filling the original pupil, each image point then occupies one quarter the solid angle. A detector covering the whole enlarged image and accepting that cone can receive the original power. Placing a fixed small sensor at the image plane intercepts less area instead. Higher magnification has not created additional radiance, even if it makes a small detail easier to resolve.

For the same 1.0 square millimetre patch seen within ten degrees, the projected acceptance is \(9.47\times10^{-8}\) square metre steradian. A wavelength-independent combined transmission and detector absorption of 0.65 leaves effective acceptance \(6.16\times10^{-8}\) square metre steradian. Integrating the Planck curve from 8 to 12 micrometres gives source and background band radiances 133.74 and 35.43 W per square metre per steradian. Their difference yields only 6.05 microwatts in the receiver. Keeping a single midpoint radiance times four micrometres happens to be fairly close here, but there is no fixed universal midpoint correction across temperatures or filters.

Open the live experiment

Set source and ambient temperatures equal. The emitted and ambient curves remain finite while the differenced instrument reading vanishes. Increase the cone half-angle from ten to twenty degrees and predict the scale factor \(\sin^2 20^\circ/\sin^2 10^\circ=3.88\). The spectral shape should stay fixed if transmission is separable from angle. If real data shift spectral shape with aperture, inspect filter angle dependence, source nonuniformity and stray light. The integral in the readout is computed on the specified 8–12 micrometre band even though the graph shows wavelengths beyond it.

Convert received power to calibrated voltage or counts

A thermal detector may respond to absorbed power through a temperature rise and an electrical responsivity. A photon-counting detector measures absorbed photons, so its count rate contains a factor \(\lambda/(hc)\) multiplying spectral power before integration. Two receivers with identical geometric acceptance and nominal quantum efficiency can respond differently to a changing source temperature if one output is proportional to energy and the other to photon number. Calibrate the actual electrical transfer and make the time interval explicit for accumulated counts.

For an ideal linear power detector let \(V=V_0+gP\), with offset \(V_0\) and gain \(g\). Two calibrated sources spanning the use range determine both. A black reference port at the same ambient temperature can also reject a common background in a chopped measurement. A filter, window or warm lens that absorbs thermal radiation also emits it. If these optics remain unchanged and stable, part of their own emission cancels in a reference difference. A reference traversing a different path generally leaves a residual input that is not captured by an electronic dark measurement.

Suppose effective source-minus-ambient powers of 2.00 and 8.00 microwatts yield 0.120 and 0.420 V. The fitted slope is 0.0500 V per microwatt with an offset of 0.020 V. A reading of 0.320 V implies 6.00 microwatts after offset correction. Substituting the same reading directly into a single-point gain with zero assumed offset would bias the inferred temperature. Rechecking a third reference near six microwatts tests linearity, while repeated zero-input readings at realistic instrument temperature test offset drift.

Invert the radiance and propagate uncertainties

A narrowband blackbody radiance measurement can be inverted analytically by solving Planck's expression for \(T\). In a broad passband use numerical quadrature across the calibrated spectral response and solve a scalar monotone equation when emissivity and background are known. The derivative at a single wavelength is particularly informative for estimating precision. With \(x=hc/(\lambda k_BT)\),

\[\frac{\partial\log B_\lambda}{\partial\log T}=\frac{x}{1-e^{-x}},\]
\[T=\frac{hc/(\lambda k_B)}{\log[1+2hc^2/(\lambda^5B_\lambda)]}.\]

At 10 micrometres and 400 K the logarithmic sensitivity is 3.70. A one-percent relative radiance uncertainty therefore gives about 1.08 K temperature uncertainty if all other quantities are exact. An unknown five-percent emissivity error can dominate that estimate. A two-colour temperature ratio may reduce uncertainty from an emissivity that is constant across the two narrow bands, but cannot cancel an arbitrary spectral emissivity or a large reflected ambient component.

As an example consider a grey object at 400 K and ambient 295 K with emissivity 0.70. At ten micrometres its outgoing radiance is \(0.70(33.56)+0.30(9.14)=26.24\) in the earlier spectral units. Inverting as a perfect blackbody gives a brightness temperature of about 375 K. The difference is a physical radiance–temperature degeneracy. Repeat at known ambient temperatures while keeping the object thermally stabilised to constrain the reflected fraction. If heating the background changes the actual object temperature, a second independent temperature sensor or a transient thermal model is necessary.

When detector heat capacity sets the measured signal

Consider a thermal receiver of heat capacity \(C_d\) coupled to its surroundings with thermal conductance \(G_d\). For small excursions its temperature obeys \(C_d\dot\theta+G_d\theta=P_{\rm abs}(t)\), where \(\theta\) is the excess above its operating point. A voltage thermometer with responsivity \(r_T\) converts this into output. Its frequency-dependent power responsivity is \(r_T/(G_d-i\omega C_d)\). The geometric and spectral calculations determine absorbed optical power, while this thermal equation determines how much of its modulation appears at the electrical output.

Take \(C_d=2.0\) microjoules per kelvin and \(G_d=0.10\) milliwatt per kelvin. The thermal time constant is 20 ms and ordinary corner frequency is 7.96 Hz. A sinusoidal absorbed-power amplitude of 6.0 microwatts would give a static temperature rise of 0.060 K. At 20 Hz the amplitude is reduced by \(1/\sqrt{1+(2\pi f\tau)^2}=0.370\), leaving about 22.2 mK with a phase lag magnitude near 68 degrees. A receiver calibrated with a steady optical power is not automatically calibrated for this chopped experiment.

A square-wave chopper also changes the relation between time-domain peak differences and a lock-in's fundamental reading. For equal hot and reference dwell times, a square wave switching between powers \(P_1,P_0\) has fundamental sinusoidal peak amplitude \(2(P_1-P_0)/\pi\), with different factors for rms and peak reports. The thermal transfer attenuates this component at the chopper frequency and the odd harmonics at their respective frequencies. Substituting the full hot-minus-reference power directly into the first-harmonic response would overestimate the expected signal.

Measure a frequency sweep on a stable reference and test whether amplitude and phase share a single time constant. A geometrical error rescales the amplitude but ordinarily leaves this thermal phase unchanged. A second thermal mass or an electronic filter can introduce another pole. Lowering chop frequency increases thermal signal until the static limit but can expose low-frequency environmental drift. Choose the frequency by comparing the calibrated response with the measured noise spectrum.

Finally, a reference and an unknown source must illuminate the same active area if responsivity varies across the receiver. Imaging one source as a small spot and the other as a filled patch can create a calibration bias even when total spectral powers agree. Translating a reference spot across the detector or imaging the same aperture successively at both temperatures tests that spatial uniformity assumption.

If the sensor is used on a source much smaller than the calibrated reference aperture, record an image or a field-stop scan to determine its filling fraction. Otherwise even exact knowledge of filter transmission and thermodynamic temperature leaves an unknown geometrical scale factor. Aperture filling is part of the measurement equation, not an electronic gain setting.

Tutorial · Infer emissivity using two controlled backgrounds

An opaque diffuse sample at fixed temperature is observed at 10 micrometres against two isotropic backgrounds with calibrated spectral radiances 8.00 and 16.00 W per square metre per steradian per micrometre. The resulting outgoing readings are 25.00 and 27.00 in the same units. Infer emissivity and the sample's blackbody-equivalent radiance. Estimate the significance of independent reading errors of 0.10 units.

Separate the reflected and emitted contributions

The increased outgoing radiance is 2.00 for an 8.00 increase of background. Thus reflectivity is 0.250 and emissivity 0.750. The first measurement gives intrinsic blackbody radiance \([25.00-0.25(8.00)]/0.75=30.67\). The second returns the same value. With 0.10 independent uncertainty on each outgoing reading and exact backgrounds, the slope uncertainty is \(\sqrt2(0.10)/8=0.0177\), as is the absolute emissivity uncertainty. Larger background separation can improve this slope estimate if sample temperature stays fixed.

If the sample transmits radiation, the residual fraction does not all correspond to reflection. A controlled change behind the sample helps detect that additional path. A directional specular reflector exposed to a warm enclosure also does not necessarily see the same angular average used in the diffuse model. Without these checks, a formally precise fitted emissivity can describe the experimental surroundings instead of the material.

Tutorial · Design a numerical spectral-integration check

An engineer tabulates spectral radiance per micrometre on a wavelength mesh. Another rewrites the detector model on an equally spaced frequency mesh, obtaining a signal that differs by several percent after independent mesh refinement. Explain what must transform, what stays dimensionless and how to compare a photon-counting channel with a power channel.

Transform the complete measurement integral

The differential power \(B_\lambda\,d\lambda\) must equal \(B_\nu\,|d\nu|\), with \(|d\lambda/d\nu|=c/\nu^2\). A dimensionless transmission curve becomes the same physical transmission at \(\lambda=c/\nu\); it acquires no additional Jacobian. The integration bounds reverse when increasing wavelength is replaced by increasing frequency. A response specified per unit wavelength, or a calibrated gain integrating a particular finite bin, must instead be interpreted from how it was defined.

Evaluate a unit-transmission test using a very narrow band, then the full 8–12 micrometre band whose radiance values are given above. Refine each grid independently, treating sharp filter edges consistently and converting per-micrometre densities before using metres. For a photon counter divide each spectral power contribution by photon energy before summation. Replacing that energy by a single central-wavelength value is an approximation that should be checked against the full band and source temperature. Close agreement between two different meshes using the same incorrect density is not a valid units check.

NIST radiance-temperature calibrations · spectral radiance, sources and uncertainty

Counting, absolute entropy and the entropy of mixing

Statistical mechanics from microscopic counting to material response

An ideal gas expands into an evacuated bulb and its temperature can remain essentially unchanged. What changed if a thermometer reports the same value? Begin by specifying a microscopic count, recover the pressure and temperature of the gas, and test the meaning of distinguishability by removing a partition between two samples. The calculation also explains why the classical formula for absolute entropy needs a microscopic action scale.

Describe a count before taking its logarithm

A macrostate specifies only outcomes resolved by a chosen preparation and measurement, for example particle number, energy within a narrow interval and container volume. A microstate specifies detailed positions and momenta consistent with those outcomes, or a quantum state in the corresponding energy shell. The multiplicity is the number of allowed states under the stated resolution and constraints. The logarithm makes multiplicities of statistically independent systems additive, because the number of joint possibilities is their product. Boltzmann entropy is

\[S=k_B\log\Omega.\]

Before computing a gas, inspect an easier count. If eight distinguishable sites each have a variable taking values zero or one and exactly three sites are occupied, there are \({8\choose3}=56\) configurations. Revealing which sites are occupied reduces the compatible set to one. This change of conditional information need not involve an actual heat flow in the original system. Thermodynamic entropy requires a physical specification of allowed states and operations; it cannot be inferred solely from how surprised a particular observer feels.

In equilibrium, equal a priori weighting in a narrow isolated energy shell is a postulate about the appropriate ensemble. It does not state that a finite trajectory visits every conceivable state in any chosen observation time. Long-lived constraints, such as a closed partition or a conserved component of angular momentum, change the accessible shell. A gas that has not exchanged particles between remote compartments can be assigned a separate particle number in each. Removing a constraint can enlarge the equilibrium count even while the volume of an exact fine-grained Hamiltonian distribution is preserved by microscopic dynamics. The thermodynamic description refers to a macroscopic resolved equilibrium state.

Count the spatial and momentum volumes of a monatomic gas

Take \(N\) indistinguishable noninteracting particles of mass \(m\) in volume \(V\), with total kinetic energy at most \(E\). Integrating over positions produces \(V^N\). Allowed momenta lie inside a sphere in \(3N\) dimensions of radius \(\sqrt{2mE}\). Its volume follows from computing a multidimensional Gaussian integral both in Cartesian and radial coordinates. Divide by a microscopic cell \(h^{3N}\) to make the state count dimensionless and by \(N!\) to prevent a permutation of indistinguishable particles from creating another physical state. The cumulative count is

\[\Phi(E,V,N)=\frac{V^N}{N!h^{3N}}\frac{(2\pi mE)^{3N/2}}{\Gamma(3N/2+1)}.\]

Using the shell count \(\partial_E\Phi\,\delta E\) instead changes subextensive terms for a conventional narrow shell that still contains many states. The cumulative formula is especially convenient for this gas. Equalities at finite small \(N\) depend on which entropy is chosen, so state that choice before claiming an exact finite-size temperature. The usual bulk relations follow from either in the thermodynamic limit for this unbounded, regular system.

For large \(N\), use \(\log N!\simeq N\log N-N\) and the analogous gamma-function asymptotics. After collecting the terms,

\[S\simeq Nk_B\left[\log\!\left(\frac VN\left(\frac{4\pi mE}{3Nh^2}\right)^{3/2}\right)+\frac52\right].\]

Doubling \(E,V,N\) at fixed ratios doubles the leading expression. Omitting \(N!\) would leave a term growing like \(N\log N\), violating this extensivity. The phase-space cell influences the entropy per particle and the chemical potential but drops out of classical fixed-particle derivatives for pressure and temperature. Quantum mechanics fixes the natural cell scale through state counting. It does not require physical particles to carry serial numbers while remaining otherwise identical.

Read temperature, pressure and chemical potential from one function

Write the entropy differential as \(dS=dE/T+(p/T)dV-(\mu/T)dN\). Differentiating the count at fixed remaining variables gives

\[1/T=3Nk_B/(2E),\]
\[p/T=Nk_B/V.\]

Thus an energy exchange measurement and a force on a moving wall follow from derivatives of the same count. The thermal wavelength \(\lambda_T=h/\sqrt{2\pi mk_BT}\) lets us rewrite the entropy and chemical potential as

\[\frac S{Nk_B}=\frac52-\log(n\lambda_T^3),\]
\[\mu=k_BT\log(n\lambda_T^3),\]
\[n=N/V .\]

The chemical potential uses the same single-particle energy zero as the energy expression. Shifting every particle energy by a constant shifts \(\mu\) by that constant, leaving equilibrium criteria involving consistent references unchanged. An unresolved internal ground-state degeneracy \(g\) would multiply the state count by \(g^N\) in the classical dilute limit and replace \(n\lambda_T^3\) by \(n\lambda_T^3/g\). Real molecules and atoms can have internal states and additional degrees of freedom; never assign monatomic entropy to a molecular gas without justifying its internal contribution.

For ideal monatomic argon at 300 K and 1.00 bar, using \(m=39.948\) unified atomic mass units, \(\lambda_T=15.95\) pm and \(n\lambda_T^3=9.79\times10^{-8}\). The result is approximately 154.97 J per mole kelvin. Its small degeneracy parameter supports the dilute classical approximation. Compressing isothermally by a factor of ten increases chemical potential by \(k_BT\log10\) and decreases entropy by \(Nk_B\log10\). Extending the classical formula down to zero temperature at fixed density would eventually give a negative unbounded entropy, signalling the breakdown of the classical state occupation assumptions.

Why a partition between identical samples is different

Take two ideal gases initially separated at equal temperature and pressure. Their volumes are proportional to their particle numbers. For two distinguishable species each component expands to the full volume after the partition is removed. The internal kinetic energies are unchanged in the ideal model. Summing the two entropy increases and writing mole fractions \(x_i=N_i/N\) gives

\[\Delta S_{\rm mix}=-Nk_B\sum_i x_i\log x_i .\]

One mole containing fractions 0.30 and 0.70 gains 5.08 J/K upon ideal mixing from the pure components. At 300 K a reversible isothermal separation needs minimum work \(T\Delta S_{\rm mix}=1.52\) kJ for this mole if the ideal components and reference states are restored. The free expansion itself need not deliver that work. It is available in a reversible mixing path using selective barriers or equivalent controlled separation operations.

If both compartments initially contain identical gas at equal density, the final macrostate has the same density and same single species throughout. Combining two extensive entropies gives the final entropy to leading bulk order. There is no extensive mixing increment \(Nk_B\log2\). Introducing artificial labels for the former left and right particles changes the physical question only if a real degree of freedom can be prepared, distinguished and selectively processed. A dye, isotope or internal spin state can serve as such a label under suitable experimental operations. Merely drawing colours on a computer representation of identical classical particles does not establish a membrane that could sort those labels.

A tempting argument says that making two different gases more and more similar must continuously reduce their ideal mixing entropy. In the ideal model the species occupation labels are either distinct or identical; the description does not specify a continuously degrading separation instrument. A model of partially distinguishable internal states or a noisy finite-resolution sorter is a different experiment. Its accessible work depends on the physical state preparation and measurement operations. Distinguish the mathematical idealisation from an actual membrane having imperfect chemical selectivity.

Check an entropy change using a reversible reference route

In an isolated free expansion of a dilute gas into a vacuum the external pressure vanishes and boundary work is zero. With negligible heat flow energy remains constant and for an ideal gas so does temperature. To compute the state-function entropy change choose a hypothetical reversible isothermal expansion between the same endpoints. Reversible heat input is \(\int p\,dV=Nk_BT\log(V_f/V_i)\), giving \(Nk_B\log(V_f/V_i)\). This reference heat is not a claim that heat actually crossed the boundary during free expansion.

For a more exact real-gas experiment, first check that the initial and final temperatures are indeed the same. Internal energy may depend on volume. From the fundamental differential and a Maxwell relation one obtains

\[(\partial U/\partial V)_T=T(\partial p/\partial T)_V-p.\]

A temperature change during an isolated free expansion thus carries information about intermolecular interactions. Measuring only final pressure and assuming constant temperature could misattribute an interaction-induced energy change to the ideal state count.

Use the existing energy-sharing experiment to see how multiplicity changes with a constraint

Recover the entropy cost of a number fluctuation

A gas can lose an extensive mixing entropy when a physically selective partition is imposed, yet the finite-size cost of constraining only a particle count is much smaller. To see this, divide a box into two equal geometric halves with an imaginary plane and let all particles travel freely. At fixed total particle number each classical ideal particle has probability one half to be on either side. The probability of exactly \(k\) particles on the left is \({N\choose k}/2^N\), independently of whether the particles have names in a bookkeeping algorithm. The binomial coefficient counts the spatial arrangements compatible with that number. The resulting variance of the left count is \(N/4\).

The chance of exactly the mean count for even \(N\) is approximately \(\sqrt{2/(\pi N)}\), from Stirling's formula. If a protocol constrains the left count to precisely \(N/2\), the entropy difference between allowing all counts and that constrained ensemble is therefore about \((k_B/2)\log(\pi N/2)\). It grows only logarithmically with total size. In contrast, if the constraint identifies which half contains every member of two distinguishable species, the mixing entropy is extensive. The physical variables that the partition controls decide which count is appropriate.

For \(N=100\) the probability of exactly fifty on the left is about 0.0796. The associated entropy cost of conditioning is \(2.53k_B\). A macroscopic observer resolving the fraction only to a few percent can count a whole interval of nearby values as the same macrostate. As \(N\) grows the relative width shrinks like \(1/\sqrt N\), placing almost all of the probability inside any fixed nonzero fractional interval about one half. This is how an apparently precise macroscopic density can coexist with an enormous number of fluctuating microscopic arrangements.

The far tails reveal a stronger distinction. For a fixed fraction \(x=k/N\) away from one half, applying Stirling to the binomial gives probability proportional to \(\exp[-N I(x)]/\sqrt{2\pi Nx(1-x)}\), with

\[I(x)=x\log(2x)+(1-x)\log[2(1-x)] .\]

This rate function is nonnegative, vanishes at one half and starts quadratically there. At \(x=0.7\) it is about 0.0823, so the leading exponential cost for a thousand particles is \(e^{-82.3}\). A Gaussian near the peak gives a similar rough scale here but fails to capture the exact tail or the hard limits \(0\leq k\leq N\). A useful numerical exercise calculates binomial coefficients using log factorials, compares the central Gaussian approximation with the full rate function and checks that the probabilities sum to one. Evaluating the three factorials directly can overflow while the final probability is perfectly representable.

A reversible separation engine and a deliberately imperfect measurement

Imagine two ideal distinguishable gases separated initially at equal pressure and temperature. To obtain work while mixing, use species-selective membranes and allow reversible isothermal expansion of each partial gas against an appropriate pressure. The membrane traversed by one component must not transmit that component to the wrong side, and every infinitesimal motion must occur at negligible pressure imbalance for the component doing the work. The sum of the recoverable works is the sum of each component's ideal expansion work. Reversing the device separates the mixture at the same thermodynamic cost when dissipation can be made negligible.

For a one-mole mixture containing 0.20 mole of one species and 0.80 of another at 300 K, the work contribution of the minority component is \(-0.20RT\log0.20=0.803\) kJ and that of the majority is \(-0.80RT\log0.80=0.445\) kJ. Although the minority has fewer particles, each expands across a larger volume ratio and its work contribution is larger. The total is 1.25 kJ. A bulk piston doing no selective separation sees equal total initial pressures and no obvious macroscopic pressure difference. The selective mechanical operation matters to the interpretation of free energy.

A useful idealised sorter determines a binary label with symmetric error probability \(e\), with equal prior fractions. The fraction of correctly sorted particles is \(1-e\), yet that single number by itself says little about its thermodynamic performance unless both output compositions are measured. For the specified symmetric device the posterior composition in each equal outlet is \((1-e,e)\) in opposite order. The increase in mixing free energy relative to one unseparated equimolar reservoir at the same total temperature, density and amounts is

\[\Delta F=Nk_BT[\log2+(1-e)\log(1-e)+e\log e] .\]

This is a state-function comparison of outlet compositions, not an assertion that the actual apparatus consumes this amount or that measuring a label is free. At \(e=0.10\) the bracket is 0.368, about 53 percent of the complete separation value. At \(e=0.5\) it vanishes even though a nominal label was printed for every particle. If one outlet loses particles or the two error probabilities differ, compute actual outlet fractions and chemical potentials from a material balance instead of using this symmetric formula.

An experiment can close that balance without seeing individual molecular trajectories. Measure both outlet amounts, composition, pressure and temperature, and account separately for work or heat supplied to the sorter. Compare the Gibbs free-energy change at controlled pressure with the actual electrical work. A device consuming several times the reversible minimum can still separate effectively. Conversely, apparent work below the calculated minimum suggests an omitted material stream, a different final pressure, an energy supplied by an uncounted reservoir or uncertainty in composition. The mixing formula is only as meaningful as the thermodynamic endpoints used.

Follow isotope and quantum effects to the edge of the classical formula

The appearance of \(h\) in a classical-looking entropy sometimes suggests that the Sackur–Tetrode expression already describes any temperature so long as gas interactions are weak. These are two different approximations. Diluteness relative to the interaction range controls the importance of forces. Diluteness in phase space, measured by \(n\lambda_T^3\), controls whether quantum exchange changes occupation weights. Atoms can interact weakly while exhibiting strong exchange statistics. Lowering temperature makes the thermal wavelength grow as inverse square root of temperature, quickly invalidating the classical count at a fixed density.

Consider two isotopes with one-particle masses in the ratio four to three, neglecting internal degeneracy differences and comparing at the same density and temperature. Their wavelengths have ratio \(\sqrt{3/4}\). The heavier isotope has a translational molar entropy greater by \((3R/2)\log(4/3)=3.59\) J per mole kelvin. This does not contradict the mass independence of the ideal mixing increment. The former compares two absolute entropy values of different substances; the latter is a change in each substance's accessible translational volume while its mass remains fixed.

The same mass scaling influences onset of quantum degeneracy. At fixed \(n\), the temperature for a chosen value of \(n\lambda_T^3\) varies inversely with mass. A heavier isotope reaches that value at a lower temperature. If the nuclei also have different spin degeneracies, distinguish the dimensionless density per internal component from the total density. Two isotopes can even have different Bose or Fermi exchange statistics, so a high-temperature entropy comparison need not extrapolate into similar low-temperature heat capacities.

A calorimetric consistency test illustrates the limits. Suppose an idealised solid has a measured low-temperature molar \(C_p=AT^3\) up to 5 K, with \(A=0.20\) mJ per mole per kelvin to the fourth. Its entropy increment there is \(AT^3/3=0.00833\) J per mole kelvin. Missing that contribution hardly changes a room-temperature entropy tabulated to a few tenths of a joule per mole kelvin. In contrast, omitting a latent heat of 1.0 kJ/mol at a transition near 100 K misses 10 J per mole kelvin. The low-temperature extrapolation and the phase transition enter the same entropy integral but can carry very different experimental leverage.

A finite thermometer measures a ratio of multiplicities

Temperature from an entropy derivative presupposes sufficiently smooth variation with energy. In a small discrete environment a two-level probe of gap \(\Delta\) can instead measure an exact finite difference. If the whole isolated system has total energy \(E\) and the two probe states have equal degeneracy, their equilibrium odds are \(\Omega_R(E-\Delta)/\Omega_R(E)\). Write the logarithm as \([S_R(E-\Delta)-S_R(E)]/k_B\). Interpreting it as \(-\Delta/(k_BT_{\rm probe})\) defines an operational inverse temperature averaged over an interval of width \(\Delta\).

For a reservoir whose cumulative multiplicity is proportional to \(E^\alpha\), this gives \(\beta_{\rm probe}=\alpha\log[E/(E-\Delta)]/\Delta\), while the local slope at \(E\) gives \(\alpha/E\). With \(\Delta/E=0.10\), the finite-gap value exceeds the endpoint slope by 5.36 percent. A second probe with one fifth that fractional gap reports only about a one-percent excess. Two well-calibrated probes can therefore disagree in a finite reservoir while each correctly reports its specified probability ratio.

The distinction vanishes for a macroscopically large heat bath whose entropy is locally almost linear over both gaps. It is experimentally testable in numerical energy-sharing models without calibrating a mechanical pressure. Count both occupancy ratios, establish whether the probe appreciably changes the reservoir's allowed states and compare against the exact discrete multiplicities before invoking a smooth temperature. If repeated measurements perturb the environment, the measured distribution is a sequence of new preparations and the equilibrium interpretation needs an explicit readout model.

Construct a thermodynamic cycle that isolates mixing entropy

The chemical-potential formula suggests a pressure measurement for testing mixing free energy. Let one side of a movable membrane transmit a solvent species freely and block a second dilute gas species, with common temperature maintained. In the ideal-gas case the permeable species equilibrates its partial pressure across the membrane. If on one side it is pure at pressure \(p_A\), while on the other side it has mole fraction \(x\) at total pressure \(p_{\rm mix}\), equality gives

\[p_A=xp_{\rm mix}.\]

The remaining pressure load on the membrane is the partial pressure of the blocked species. This is a mechanical consequence of equality of the permeable component's chemical potential.

For an ideal mixture with \(x=0.80\) and total pressure 1.00 bar, the pure compartment must reach 0.80 bar for zero net flow of the permeable species. A square centimetre membrane then supports a net pressure difference of 0.20 bar, corresponding to 2.0 N, before any additional elastic support forces. A membrane sensitive only to the total pressure might move and alter the volumes until mechanical and chemical constraints are both fulfilled. The quoted force is a held-geometry measurement, not a prediction that a freely moving unsupported membrane can remain at that position.

A particularly important control switches the membrane for a nonselective porous partition. Both species can then flow and equilibrate their own chemical potentials; at final full equilibrium there is no maintained composition difference across these gas reservoirs. A pressure reading alone cannot establish whether selectivity, partial blockage, a small temperature difference or an external pump generated the observed imbalance. Measure both compositions and isolate slow leaks over the relevant time. An apparently stable jump on a short trace can be a long-lived transient.

The same thought experiment gives a check on how gas-phase mixing connects to osmotic pressure in dilute solutions. The permeable solvent's chemical potential is lowered by mixing at fixed total pressure; applying enough pressure to the solution can restore equality across a solvent-selective membrane. The ideal dilute result has a form proportional to solute number density times thermal energy, although a liquid solution requires its own solvent partial molar volume model. Equal-looking formulas arise from a chemical-potential balance, not because dissolved solute molecules necessarily act as an ideal gas physically bouncing against every membrane.

Finally, a cyclic device must restore membrane positions, reservoir compositions and temperatures as well as a piston reading. Ignoring the work to regenerate selectivity or restore separated gas streams can turn a one-use release of stored mixing free energy into an apparent heat engine operating on one bath. Write the mole count of each component in every reservoir at start and finish and integrate mechanical and chemical work consistently. The criterion for a true thermodynamic cycle is return of the complete controlled system to its initial state.

A final microscopic check concerns the dimensions of the count. If one changed the formal classical cell size from \(h^3\) to \(a h^3\) per particle, the gas entropy would shift by \(-Nk_B\log a\) at every fixed state. Pressure, temperature and the entropy change of a fixed-\(N\) expansion would be unaffected. Chemical potential would shift by \(k_BT\log a\), and absolute comparisons between different particle species or chemical reactions would require a consistently calibrated microscopic count. This is why a piston experiment alone could never determine the natural action cell. Combining calorimetry with quantum level counting or fixed-reference chemical equilibria supplies information absent from fixed-\(N\) mechanical derivatives. The same bookkeeping distinction applies to any unobserved constant internal degeneracy: it can contribute entropy without producing a heat-capacity anomaly.

Connect ideal mixing to a separation-work measurement

Vary the mixture composition and the reservoir temperature. Compare the entropy with reversible work for separately identifiable ideal components.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · Measure an entropy difference by two routes

A 0.50 mol sample of ideal monatomic gas expands from 2.0 to 5.0 litres in a thermally insulated free expansion. Calculate entropy change and change of chemical potential at 290 K. Now identify what changes if the gas instead expands reversibly and adiabatically between the same volumes.

Hold the state variables appropriate to each route

For free expansion temperature stays at 290 K and entropy increases by \(0.50R\log(2.5)=3.81\) J/K. At fixed temperature \(\mu_f-\mu_i=k_BT\log(n_f/n_i)=-k_BT\log2.5\), or minus 2.21 kJ/mol. The chemical-potential difference is a single-particle or molar quantity, while the entropy answer refers to the entire sample. Dividing or multiplying by the sample amount twice produces an inconsistent unit.

In a reversible adiabatic expansion \(\Delta S=0\) and the gas does boundary work. The final temperature becomes \(290(2/5)^{2/3}\simeq157\) K. These are not the same endpoints as in the free expansion, despite identical start and end volumes. Substituting the free-expansion entropy into an adiabatic isentropic relation would falsely equate insulation with reversibility.

Tutorial · An isotope mixture and a finite-size count

Two equal amounts of isotopically distinct ideal monatomic gases at the same temperature and density are mixed. Explain why their masses enter absolute chemical potentials but cancel from each component's ideal isothermal mixing increment. For just ten sites individually occupied at random by one of two distinguishable isotope labels, compare the exact number of arrangements with five of each label to the leading extensive entropy.

Cancel fixed internal terms and keep the finite combinatorics

Each component's thermal wavelength is unchanged between its own initial and final states. Taking the difference of its entropy therefore removes the mass and internal factors. Both species expand by a factor of two and the large-sample increase is \(Nk_B\log2\). This conclusion assumes independently identifiable species and an ideal mixture, and does not state that their absolute entropies are identical.

For ten sites with five labels of each kind there are \({10\choose5}=252\) arrangements. Their logarithm is 5.529 in units of \(k_B\), versus \(10\log2=6.931\) from an extensive approximation neglecting the composition constraint's subextensive term. Summing over all possible label counts gives \(2^{10}\), recovering the latter count for an ensemble in which the composition itself fluctuates. A small-system discrepancy is an opportunity to ask which constraint the count imposes.

Tutorial · Infer what a calorimetric entropy determination misses

A crystal's heat capacity is measured from a low reference temperature up to a temperature above a reversible melting transition. How could this measurement be used to estimate entropy, and which missing physical contributions could spoil an apparent comparison with a dilute gas entropy?

Include the low-temperature reference and all phase changes

Along a constant-pressure reversible route add \(\int C_p(T)dT/T\) in each phase and add latent heat divided by transition temperature at every first-order change. To compare with gas one must also traverse vaporisation or another controlled path to the desired gas state and pressure. Missing a transition silently omits its finite entropy even if the heat capacities in both adjacent phases were measured perfectly.

Below the lowest measured temperature extrapolate using a physically justified low-temperature model and add any residual ground-state entropy consistent with the sample preparation. A unique ordered ground state has zero thermodynamic entropy in the ideal third-law limit. A crystal retaining configurational disorder on laboratory timescales can carry residual entropy associated with its accessible arrangements. Unknown isotopic composition or low-lying internal levels likewise change the absolute comparison. A discrepancy between a calorimetric integral and a monatomic ideal expression should first be traced to these explicit reference states and model assumptions.

Cambridge Statistical Physics · foundational postulates, entropy and statistical ensembles

Fließbach, Statistische Physik · statistical foundations, the ideal gas and entropy

Ensembles, thermodynamic potentials and measurable curvature

Statistical mechanics from microscopic counting to material response

A sample sealed inside an insulated container, a sample weakly coupled to a temperature bath and a surface exchanging particles with a reservoir need different probability laws. Derive the three laws from one composite-system count. Then use the curvature of their generating functions to predict noise, response and parameter uncertainty without assuming that microscopic quantities take their mean values in every run.

Expand the reservoir count rather than postulating a probability

Let a small system occupy a microstate \(i\) of energy \(E_i\) while a weakly coupled reservoir holds the remainder of a fixed total energy. The composite system is isolated. Conditional on \(i\), the number of compatible reservoir states is \(\Omega_R(E_{\rm total}-E_i)\). This weight is the same for degenerate system microstates of equal energy. For a macroscopic reservoir, expand its entropy about an appropriate reference energy. The first derivative is \(1/T\), and its second derivative at fixed other extensive parameters is \(-1/(T^2 C_R)\).

\[S_R(E_R-\epsilon)=S_R(E_R)-\frac{\epsilon}{T}-\frac{\epsilon^2}{2T^2C_R}+\cdots .\]

Exponentiating and taking the limit where the quadratic and further corrections are negligible yields \(p_i=e^{-\beta E_i}/Z\), \(\beta=1/(k_BT)\), and \(Z=\sum_i e^{-\beta E_i}\). A constant shift in all system energies multiplies both numerator and partition sum by the same factor. Relative probabilities are therefore independent of energy-zero convention. If a calculation sums over energy values rather than microstates, insert their degeneracies explicitly. These two equivalent ways of counting should never be applied simultaneously.

The derivation assumes weak coupling so that assigning energies separately makes sense, and a reservoir whose temperature does not respond significantly to typical exchanges. A small heat capacity in the bath can suppress sample fluctuations even at the same mean temperature. The relevant test concerns excursions explored by the sample, not simply whether the bath contains more particles. Near a transition or in an engineered small reservoir a canonical distribution may need a measurable correction.

Compare sample-energy fluctuations for finite and effectively infinite heat reservoirs

Differentiate the normalisation to obtain energy and its variance

Differentiate \(\log Z\) with respect to \(\beta\) at fixed energy levels. The first derivative yields minus mean energy. A second derivative gives the mean square minus the squared mean. Changing differentiation from \(\beta\) to temperature gives the equilibrium heat capacity:

\[\langle E\rangle=-\partial_\beta\log Z,\]
\[\operatorname{var}(E)=\partial_\beta^2\log Z=k_BT^2 C_V .\]

This proves nonnegative canonical heat capacity for a temperature-independent Hamiltonian. If the model Hamiltonian contains parameters that themselves vary with temperature after other degrees of freedom have been eliminated, the derivative also acts on those parameters. Inserting measured effective levels into the simple identity without that dependence need not reproduce the calorimetric capacity.

A two-level system has energies zero and \(\Delta\), with upper probability \(p=1/(1+e^{\beta\Delta})\) for equal degeneracies. Its variance is \(\Delta^2p(1-p)\) and \(C/k_B=(\beta\Delta)^2p(1-p)\). At \(\Delta/k_BT=2\), \(p=0.1192\) and \(C=0.420k_B\). In one thousand independent copies the expected upper count is 119 and its standard deviation about 10.2. An actual count of 130 is unremarkable. An experiment resolving only one sample at one time cannot identify the variance from its energy alone.

Higher derivatives of \(\log Z\) generate cumulants. A Gaussian approximation discards cumulants beyond the second. For a small two-level population near low occupancy the histogram is skewed and the approximation can yield artificial negative occupancies. An exactly binomial count or a direct discrete partition sum handles this elementary case without assuming that the central-limit regime has arrived. Sample-to-sample correlations can also prevent variances from scaling as the sum of independent variances.

Why the Helmholtz free energy knows the same equilibrium as entropy

Group the canonical sum by energy. A shell with entropy \(S(E)\) has weight proportional to \(e^{S(E)/k_B-\beta E}\). For a macroscopic system with a dominant smooth interior maximum this exponent is stationary at \((\partial S/\partial E)_{V,N}=1/T\). The leading free energy \(-k_BT\log Z\) is then the minimum of \(E-TS(E)\); the width of the peak gives the energy fluctuations. The exact finite-system integral is a Laplace transform. Replacing it by its stationary contribution is an approximation, often called the Legendre-transform limit.

An equivalent variational argument works for finite discrete states without a saddle point. Choose arbitrary trial probabilities \(q_i\), their mean energy \(U[q]\) and Shannon entropy \(S[q]=-k_B\sum_iq_i\log q_i\). Subtract the canonical free energy and insert the canonical weights. Algebra gives

\[U[q]-TS[q]-F=k_BT\sum_i q_i\log(q_i/p_i)\ge0 .\]

The final inequality follows from convexity of the logarithmic divergence and becomes an equality only for the canonical distribution on the permitted support. For an isolated system one instead maximises entropy subject to its prepared energy constraint. Helmholtz free-energy minimisation at fixed temperature is thus a consequence of reservoir exchange and normalisation, not an additional force sending every microstate to the lowest energy. At finite temperature high-multiplicity excited states can dominate the equilibrium probability.

Now include volume work in the fundamental differential \(dU=TdS-pdV+\mu dN\). Product differentiation gives \(dF=-SdT-pdV+\mu dN\), \(dG=-SdT+Vdp+\mu dN\), and \(d\Omega=-SdT-pdV-Nd\mu\) for \(\Omega=F-\mu N\). A potential's natural variables specify which gradient directly returns an observable. From \(F(T,V,N)\) one obtains

\[p=-(\partial F/\partial V)_{T,N},\]
\[S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N};\]

their cross derivatives agree if the potential is smooth. At phase boundaries use phase branches and appropriate coexistence conditions instead of differentiating through a kink as if it were smooth.

Let the reservoir exchange particles as well as energy

With both exchanges allowed, expand reservoir entropy in \(E\) and \(N\). Its differential includes \(-\mu_R dN_R/T\). A system microstate with \(E,N\) therefore acquires weight \(e^{-\beta(E-\mu N)}\). Summing normalises the grand canonical distribution. At fixed temperature and volume, derivatives with respect to \(\beta\mu\) give mean particle number and number variance. In a homogeneous equilibrium region with suitable thermodynamic limit, \(\Omega=-pV\).

\[\Xi=\sum_Ne^{\beta\mu N}Z_N,\]
\[\langle N\rangle=\partial_{\beta\mu}\log\Xi,\]
\[\operatorname{var}(N)=k_BT\left(\frac{\partial\langle N\rangle}{\partial\mu}\right)_{T,V}.\]
