Their ratio then removes \(D(E_F)\) from some combinations. Correlations can renormalise spin response differently from specific heat. Orbital diamagnetism, closed-shell ions and temperature-dependent impurity moments must also be separated from a measured total susceptibility. A disagreement is an opportunity to distinguish these separate ingredients, not immediate evidence that state counting itself has failed.

A nonlinear zero-temperature spin response beyond Pauli's slope

At low magnetic fields the shift of either spin population is linear and nearly invisible in total number. To see where the susceptibility approximation comes from, retain the exact zero-temperature band populations. For a parabolic three-dimensional band with field-independent mass, absorb the two equal spin degeneracies into a coefficient \(A\) such that \(n_\pm=A[\mu\pm\mu_BB]_+^{3/2}\). The positive part enforces an empty spin band if its shifted edge lies above the chemical potential. The total \(n_++n_-\) must remain fixed in an isolated neutral sample, while \(M=\mu_B(n_+-n_-)\).

Expand both powers at small field. Linear terms cancel in the total density, so the chemical potential changes only at quadratic order. At fixed original Fermi energy the solution is

\[\mu=E_F-(\mu_BB)^2/(4E_F)+\cdots.\]

Population imbalance retains the linear term \(3\mu_BB/(2E_F)\) as a fraction of total density. This derivation checks why simply leaving \(\mu\) fixed gives the correct weak-field magnetisation slope yet fails in higher-order density calculations.

Complete spin polarisation in this model occurs when \(\mu-\mu_BB\) reaches zero. All particles then occupy the lower spin band, with effective Fermi energy relative to that band's shifted bottom equal to \(2^{2/3}E_F\). It follows that \(\mu_BB_{\rm pol}=2^{-1/3}E_F\). For a 7.05 eV metal this corresponds to roughly \(9.7\times10^4\) tesla, far outside the regime of an ordinary static bench measurement and outside a naive description of unmodified material bands. The number is a scale comparison, not a proposed magnet design. A system with a much lower Fermi energy or an enhanced effective magnetic moment can show nonlinearity at far lower applied fields.

A practical diagnostic compares positive and negative fields after subtracting the holder signal. Pure weak Pauli response is odd and linear in field with little temperature dependence. Localised impurity spins may also be odd but exhibit curvature or saturation when their Zeeman energy competes with temperature. A carrier density changing with field can alter the inferred Pauli slope. Therefore pair a magnetic measurement with a density or transport measurement before attributing every nonlinearity to spin-band depletion.

Extract a two-dimensional density of states from a gate measurement

Take a uniform conducting layer gated by a planar metal through an insulating spacer. An increment in gate voltage partly changes electrostatic voltage across the insulator and partly shifts the electronic chemical potential. In a simple uniform single-sheet model the charge-density increment \(dq=e\,dn_{2D}\) obeys \(dV_g=dq/c_g+d\mu/e\), where lowercase \(c_g\) is geometric capacitance per area. Writing \(c_q=e^2\,dn_{2D}/d\mu\) gives

\[1/c_{\rm meas}=1/c_g+1/c_q.\]

This derivation assumes a stable uniform equilibrium state and a calibrated voltage reference with negligible leakage.

Suppose independent dielectric calibration gives \(c_g=5.00\,\mu{\rm F\,cm^{-2}}\), while a low-frequency gate measurement yields

\[c_{\rm meas}=3.00\,\mu{\rm F\,cm^{-2}}.\]

The inferred quantum capacitance is \(c_q=7.50\,\mu{\rm F\,cm^{-2}}=0.0750\) F per square metre. Dividing by elementary charge squared gives \(2.92\times10^{36}\) states per joule per square metre, or \(4.68\times10^{17}\) states per eV per square metre. Confusing total sample capacitance with capacitance per area would produce a quantity that cannot be compared across different device sizes.

Sensitivity of the inverse step depends strongly on the ratio of the two measured values. If \(c_q\gg c_g\), the measured value approaches the geometric one. Small calibration offsets then dominate their reciprocal difference. For example, a measured value only one percent below \(c_g\) implies \(c_q\simeq99c_g\); an absolute error of one percentage point can remove any useful upper bound. In that limit the data support a lower bound on density of states rather than a precise central value.

Vary gate frequency to test whether traps or charge-relaxation delays contribute. A legitimate equilibrium compressibility response should be approached in the low-frequency limit under conditions where the electronic layer remains coupled to its reservoir. Interface states with slower charging can add a dispersive contribution that looks like extra density of states at one chosen frequency. Compare in-phase capacitance and loss, control for parasitic capacitance and measure several layer areas. Area-proportional capacitance and perimeter-dependent fringe capacitance can often be separated with devices of differing aspect ratio.

A differential heat pulse resolves the small electronic component

At low temperature a metal frequently has \(C_{\rm sample}=\gamma T+\beta_D T^3\) over a limited range. A platform and thermometer add their own heat capacities. A measurement might apply a known energy pulse and fit a temperature relaxation through a calibrated weak thermal link. To infer instantaneous heat capacity from \(Q/\Delta T\), the pulse must be small enough that \(C\) is nearly constant across the excursion and losses over the pulse negligible, or the energy balance must explicitly include them.

For a 10 mmol sample with \(\gamma=0.80\) mJ per mole per kelvin squared and \(\beta_D=0.10\) mJ per mole per kelvin to the fourth, molar heat capacity at 2 K is \(1.60+0.80=2.40\) mJ per mole kelvin. The sample heat capacity is 24.0 microjoules per kelvin. A 1.2 microjoule pulse would raise it by about 0.050 K neglecting heat leakage and addenda. The electronic fraction here is two thirds. At 4 K the lattice contribution exceeds the electronic term, changing which temperatures most strongly constrain the intercept in a \(C/T\) fit.

An exact small-range energy calibration uses the integral \(Q=\gamma(T_f^2-T_i^2)/2+\beta_D(T_f^4-T_i^4)/4\) times the sample amount. This avoids confusing a finite pulse average with the capacity at the initial temperature. Measure the empty platform through the same range and apply the same thermal model. A mismatch in thermal contact may create a second relaxation time and imitate sample heat capacity. Two pulses of different sizes and a delayed thermometer provide an independent test of the assumed lumped-temperature approximation.

The density of states inferred from \(\gamma\) measures excitations that can equilibrate during the experiment. A superconducting transition, magnetic ordering, a low-lying impurity contribution or a gap around the Fermi level would change the temperature dependence qualitatively. Rather than forcing a linear plus cubic fit over such structure, identify a normal-state range or include a separately justified physical term. A visually straight line on a narrow interval can hide curvature large enough to alter the intercept materially.

How a Fermi window rounds a sharp density-of-states edge

A two-dimensional band with a constant density of states starting abruptly at energy zero provides an exactly soluble compressibility edge. Differentiating its number equation gives \(dn/d\mu=D_0/[1+e^{-\beta\mu}]\) if \(D_0\) is per area. At zero temperature the response jumps from zero to \(D_0\) as the chemical potential crosses the band edge; at finite temperature it rises as a logistic function. The width between ten and ninety percent equals \(2k_BT\log9\), approximately \(4.39k_BT\).

At 10 K that intrinsic energy width is about 3.79 meV. At 30 K it becomes 11.36 meV. A gate experiment converts voltage to energy using a lever arm that may depend on the same quantum capacitance being measured. Calibrate that conversion before inferring temperature from edge width. Static disorder, inhomogeneous local band edges or electrical noise broaden the edge even at low temperature. Plot width against temperature and compare the full convolution model rather than interpreting a nonzero intercept as an electronic thermal floor without independent evidence.

This edge example shows exactly where a low-temperature expansion in smooth density derivatives fails. Every derivative of the constant band interior is zero, yet if the chemical potential lies within a few thermal energies of its abrupt edge there is a strong measurable density response crossover. Numerical integration of the full Fermi function or the exact analytic expression automatically retains this boundary. A calculation based solely on the local derivative of a density of states sampled within the band cannot recover it.

Separate Pauli spin response from independent local moments with a concentration test

A common experimental model of weakly magnetic metallic matter combines an approximately constant itinerant-electron susceptibility with a dilute Curie term \(C/T\) from paramagnetic impurities. At modest fields the local moment of independent spin-half sites with magnetic moment magnitude \(\mu\) has average \(m=\mu\tanh(\mu B/k_BT)\). Its small-field derivative gives volume susceptibility \(\mu_0 n_i\mu^2/(k_BT)\). The conduction-electron spin response instead scales with the density of states at the Fermi level. Two subsystems exposed to the same bath can therefore have markedly different temperature dependences.

Suppose two volume-susceptibility readings in SI units are \(2.20\times10^{-5}\) at 10 K and \(1.45\times10^{-5}\) at 40 K. A fit to \(\chi_0+C/T\) gives \(C=1.00\times10^{-4}\) K and \(\chi_0=1.20\times10^{-5}\). For independent moment magnitude \(\mu_B\), the implied impurity number density is \(Ck_B/(\mu_0\mu_B^2)\simeq1.28\times10^{25}\) per cubic metre. Relative to a carrier density of \(8.5\times10^{28}\) this is only about 150 parts per million. A small population can dominate the temperature-dependent change without representing most of the material.

Two points cannot verify the Curie form, so repeat across temperatures and fields. At sufficiently high \(B/T\) the independent moments approach saturation while the ideal degenerate electron subsystem remains approximately linear. At fixed \(B/T\), plotting the temperature-dependent residual should approximately collapse for independent moments. Interacting or clustered impurities can display a shifted Curie–Weiss temperature, blocking or history dependence, and a distribution of moments can broaden the saturation shape. A successful two-point subtraction alone does not establish a unique background.

A related check uses calorimetry. A split two-level impurity contributes a field-dependent Schottky anomaly when its gap is several thermal energies. Changing field shifts this anomaly, whereas the leading electronic linear coefficient of a simple normal metal varies much less at modest fields. The integrated entropy of an isolated two-state population tends toward \(n_i k_B\log2\) when both orientations become equally populated. A count inferred from susceptibility can therefore be tested by a separately integrated excess heat capacity, once lattice and electronic backgrounds are constrained.

Take care with units and signs when comparing papers or instruments. Some report molar susceptibility, others volume susceptibility, and cgs and SI magnetic conventions differ by factors involving \(4\pi\) and volume conversion. Divide a volume susceptibility by number density only after identifying whether the number refers to all host atoms, carriers or the inferred localised impurities. Agreement to a few significant figures after an unverified unit conversion has little diagnostic value.

A final test estimates when local spins should start to saturate at a given field. At 5 tesla and 2 K a magnetic moment equal to one Bohr magneton has \(\mu_BB/(k_BT)\simeq1.68\). The independent-spin alignment is \(\tanh(1.68)=0.933\), already close to saturation. In the same field the example metal's relative itinerant spin imbalance remains only around \(6\times10^{-5}\). A common high-field measurement can therefore discriminate two very different microscopic populations even if both looked nearly linear at much smaller fields. Remanent magnetisation on returning to zero would be inconsistent with both ideal noninteracting equilibrium models. It would motivate a check for ferromagnetic contamination, anisotropic barriers or hysteresis in the measurement apparatus before refining a fit to either simple susceptibility law.

Decide which electronic states contribute to heat capacity

Change temperature relative to Fermi energy and compare the low-temperature approximation with the statistical occupation.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · Recover a density of states from a calorimeter

A sample exhibits a low-temperature molar heat capacity \(C/T=0.80\,{\rm mJ\,mol^{-1}K^{-2}}+bT^2\). Use the intercept to estimate the total density of electronic states per mole per electronvolt. Compare with the prediction for one conduction electron per atom and \(E_F=7.0\) eV.

Extract the electronic coefficient and convert energy units

In joules the total molar density is \(D=3(0.00080)/(\pi^2k_B^2)=1.28\times10^{42}\) states per joule per mole. Multiplying by the number of joules per electronvolt gives \(2.05\times10^{23}\) states per eV per mole. For one electron per atom the ideal value is \(3N_A/(2E_F)=1.29\times10^{23}\) states per eV per mole. The fitted effective density is therefore about 1.59 times larger. A molar heat capacity must be paired with a molar density of states; using a sample volume here without a molar volume would create a dimensional mismatch.

To decide whether the enhancement reflects a nonfree band, interactions or a wrong temperature window, examine the linearity of \(C/T\) versus \(T^2\) over several restricted fit ranges. A low-lying impurity or additional vibrational mode can produce curvature that changes the intercept. Compare with independent band information and spin response after relevant backgrounds are subtracted.

Tutorial · Determine a weak-field magnetisation and its background

At 1.0 T the measured volume magnetisation is 7.0 A/m, independent of temperature over a low-temperature range. The sample's predicted Pauli susceptibility is \(1.22\times10^{-5}\). Estimate the residual susceptibility if the magnetisation is linear and explain why its sign alone does not identify an orbital model.

Compare quantities in the same magnetic convention

One tesla corresponds to \(H\simeq7.96\times10^5\) A/m in this weakly magnetic setting. The predicted Pauli magnetisation is 9.71 A/m. The residual is minus 2.71 A/m or \(\chi_{\rm residual}\simeq-3.4\times10^{-6}\). Orbital diamagnetism of an ideal bulk free-electron gas supplies a negative contribution, but core diamagnetism, band orbital effects and sample-holder background can also contribute. Measure an empty holder and both field signs, consider specimen shape and calibrate the sample volume. A nearly temperature-independent negative residual does not uniquely identify any one mechanism.

Tutorial · Why a constant two-dimensional density of states behaves differently

For an ideal two-dimensional parabolic band of constant total density \(D_0\) above energy zero, derive the finite-temperature chemical potential at fixed \(N=D_0E_F\). Compare its leading low-temperature change with the power-law shift in three dimensions.

Evaluate the number integral before expanding

The integral gives

\[N=D_0k_BT\log[1+e^{\mu/(k_BT)}].\]

Solving yields

\[\mu=k_BT\log[e^{E_F/(k_BT)}-1]=E_F+k_BT\log[1-e^{-E_F/(k_BT)}].\]

Its deviation from \(E_F\) at low temperature is exponentially small, consistent with \(D'(E_F)=0\). A program that always imposes the three-dimensional negative quadratic shift would violate particle conservation in this band. The low-temperature heat capacity remains linear because \(D_0\) itself is nonzero.

At high temperature the same exact expression approaches the classical logarithmic chemical potential. Evaluate the low-temperature numerical form as \(E_F+k_BT\log(1-e^{-E_F/k_BT})\) to avoid overflowing the large exponential. Here numerical stability and correct dimensional density of states are independent requirements; solving one does not fix the other.

Cyclotron motion and quantised magnetic orbits

Condensed matter and band theory

Relate a magnetic-field measurement to band curvature, level spacing and a cross-section of the Fermi surface.

From first principles

Find the orbit and energy spacing imposed by a magnetic field

Consider an electron in an isotropic parabolic band with effective mass \(m^*\), no electric field and a uniform magnetic induction \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\). The Lorentz force is perpendicular to velocity, so it changes the direction of the transverse velocity without changing its magnitude. Taking absolute values in the radial force balance gives

\[\frac{m^* v_\perp^2}{r_c}=e v_\perp B,\]
\[r_c=\frac{m^*v_\perp}{eB},\]
\[\omega_c=\frac{eB}{m^*}.\]

To obtain energy levels one also needs quantum mechanics. Choose \(\mathbf A=(0,Bx,0)\) and electron charge \(-e\) with \(e>0\). The Hamiltonian \((\mathbf p+e\mathbf A)^2/(2m^*)\) is independent of \(y,z\), so their canonical momenta can be treated as fixed in a plane-wave state. Complete the square:

\[H=\frac{p_x^2}{2m^*}+\frac{m^*\omega_c^2}{2}\left(x+\frac{p_y}{eB}\right)^2+\frac{p_z^2}{2m^*}.\]

This is a harmonic oscillator about a centre set by \(p_y\). Its transverse levels have energies \(\hbar\omega_c(n+1/2)\); longitudinal translation remains free. Spin splitting, anisotropic band curvature and collision broadening require further information. The same frequency occurs in the classical orbit and the quantum level spacing for distinct reasons made explicit by these two constructions.

Derivation and applications

Determine which mass a cyclotron resonance measures

An orbit on an anisotropic band need not be circular. Consider a weak uniform magnetic induction \(B\) along \(z\), and motion in a single band with elliptical constant-energy contours in the \(xy\) plane. Neglect scattering over an orbit, electric fields, and spin splitting. Write the local dispersion as \(E=\hbar^2 k_x^2/(2m_x)+\hbar^2 k_y^2/(2m_y)\), measured from its minimum. For charge \(-e\), the semiclassical force law and the velocity components give

\[\dot k_x=-\frac{eB}{m_y}k_y,\]
\[\dot k_y=\frac{eB}{m_x}k_x .\]

Taking a second derivative cancels the other component. Both coordinates oscillate at the same fundamental angular frequency even though their amplitudes differ,

\[\ddot k_x=-\frac{e^2B^2}{m_xm_y}k_x,\]
\[\omega_c=\frac{eB}{m_c},\]
\[m_c=\sqrt{m_xm_y}.\]

A geometric measurement leads to the same mass. The two semiaxes of the contour at energy \(E\) are \(\sqrt{2m_iE}/\hbar\). Its reciprocal-space area \(A(E)\) and energy derivative are

\[A(E)=\frac{2\pi E}{\hbar^2}\sqrt{m_xm_y},\]
\[\frac{\hbar^2}{2\pi}\frac{dA}{dE}=m_c .\]

The derivative explains how a measurement of orbit area over a small energy range tests the resonance result. Two bands with the same area at one energy can have different derivatives there. For a circle, both principal masses and the cyclotron mass coincide. A single resonance frequency for this orientation measures their geometric mean and leaves their ratio undetermined.

Worked example

Predict the resonance of an elliptical pocket

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A layer has \(m_x=0.100m_e\), \(m_y=0.900m_e\) and is placed in \(B=1.50\,\mathrm T\). The cyclotron mass is \(0.300m_e\), so the ordinary frequency is

\[f_c=eB/(2\pi m_c)=140\,\mathrm{GHz}.\]

The period is \(7.14\,\mathrm{ps}\). The long reciprocal-space semiaxis lies along \(y\) and is three times the short one, even though both components complete their cycle in exactly the same time.

Second application

Use two nearby contour areas as a mass measurement

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

In a different sample the orbit area rises by \(0.200\,\mathrm{nm^{-2}}\) when energy rises by \(20.0\,\mathrm{meV}\). Treat the measured slope as locally constant. In SI units \(dA/dE=2.00\times10^{17}/(3.204\times10^{-21})\,\mathrm{m^{-2}J^{-1}}\). The area formula gives

\[m_c=1.10\times10^{-31}\,\mathrm{kg}=0.121m_e.\]

An independent determination of \(m_x=0.0600m_e\) would then imply \(m_y=m_c^2/m_x=0.245m_e\) at the displayed precision.

Try it · Cyclotron mass

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Follow a wave packet in a band

A narrow wave packet in a well-isolated band moves with \(\mathbf v=\hbar^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf k}E\). In a static uniform magnetic field the semiclassical force law is

\[\hbar\dot{\mathbf k}=-e\,\mathbf v\times\mathbf B.\]

Thus \(\nabla E\cdot\dot{\mathbf k}=0\): the energy is constant because a magnetic field does no work. The wavevector component along \(\mathbf B\) is also constant. The orbit in reciprocal space lies along the intersection of a constant-energy surface and a plane perpendicular to the field. Tilting the field samples different sections of a nonspherical Fermi surface.

For an isotropic parabolic band, motion transverse to the field is circular at \(\omega_c=eB/m^*\). The real-space orbit radius is \(v_\perp/\omega_c\). One can measure an effective mass from the resonant angular frequency without separately knowing the elastic scattering time. Whether the motion survives long enough to be resolved depends on \(\omega_c\tau\). A very short scattering time smears the resonance and suppresses oscillatory magnetotransport.

Derive the quantised spacing

For a two-dimensional electron choose \(A_y=Bx\) and \(A_x=0\). Since the Hamiltonian for charge \(-e\) is \(H=[p_x^2+(p_y+eBx)^2]/(2m^*)\), a state of definite \(p_y=\hbar k_y\) leaves a one-dimensional harmonic oscillator in \(x\), centred at \(-\hbar k_y/(eB)\). Its levels are \(E_j=(j+1/2)\hbar\omega_c\), neglecting spin. At fixed level, changing \(k_y\) moves the orbit centre without changing its energy. The centres allowed inside a rectangle of area \(A\) count to \(eBA/h\) per spin, up to edge corrections. In three dimensions motion along the field adds a dispersing kinetic-energy term.

Take \(B=5\) T and \(m^*=0.10m_e\). The orbital spacing is about 5.79 meV, equivalent to \(k_B T\) at 67 K. At 4 K the thermal scale is about 0.345 meV, smaller than the spacing. An observation still requires sufficiently weak disorder broadening and appropriate carrier filling. As the field changes, both the level spacing and the number of states carried by each level change.

Extract a reciprocal-space area from a measurement

For a closed semiclassical orbit of area \(A_k(E)\), quantisation gives

\[A_k=2\pi eB(j+\gamma)/\hbar,\]

where the phase offset \(\gamma\) depends on the orbit. Successive levels crossing the Fermi energy give oscillations periodic in reciprocal field with frequency \(F=\hbar A_k(E_F)/(2\pi e)\), expressed in tesla. For \(F=100\) T, \(A_k\simeq9.55\times10^{17}\) m⁻². A circular section of that area has radius \(5.51\times10^8\) m⁻¹. In a three-dimensional metal dominant oscillations come from extremal sections. Inferring a full Fermi volume from one frequency requires additional assumptions about shape and symmetry; measurements at several orientations can test them.

Count orbit centres and Hall filling

Landau level spacing determines the energy cost of changing orbital index. A second question is how many independent states fit into each level. Take a rectangular two-dimensional layer of area \(L_xL_y\), magnetic field \(B\) normal to it and Landau gauge \(A_y=Bx\). Translation symmetry along \(y\) gives wavevectors \(k_y=2\pi j/L_y\). In the transverse Hamiltonian the term \((\hbar k_y+eBx)^2/(2m^*)\) describes a harmonic oscillator with centre \(x_0=-\hbar k_y/(eB)\).

To place an orbit centre inside the sample, \(k_y\) must range over an interval of length \(eBL_x/\hbar\). Dividing by the allowed spacing \(2\pi/L_y\) gives \(N_\phi=eBL_xL_y/h\) bulk states per spin-resolved orbital level, neglecting edge corrections. Each magnetic flux quantum \(h/e\) adds one state per level. For sheet density \(n_s\) define filling factor \(\nu=n_sh/(eB)\). This counts occupied spin-resolved levels when it is an integer; if a residual spin degeneracy is unresolved, levels arrive in pairs.

In an ideal low-temperature integer quantum Hall state, with the chemical potential in a mobility gap, longitudinal dissipation can vanish and Hall conductance magnitude is \(\nu e^2/h\). The classical expression \(n_se/B\) has the same value at exact integer filling, but alone cannot explain persistence of plateaus over a finite interval of field. Localization and edge transport are essential for that observation.

Locate an integer filling in a measurement

A two-dimensional electron system has \(n_s=3.00\times10^{15}\) m⁻² and effective mass \(0.067m_e\). The field for total filling \(\nu=2\) is

\[B=n_sh/(2e)=6.20\,\mathrm{T}.\]

The number of states per area per spin-resolved level is \(eB/h=1.50\times10^{15}\) m⁻², half the measured sheet density. The orbital spacing at this field is

\[\hbar eB/m^*=10.7\,\mathrm{meV}.\]

At 4.2 K the thermal energy is 0.362 meV, so thermal smearing is smaller than the orbital gap; disorder and spin structure still matter.

The ideal Hall resistance magnitude on the \(\nu=2\) plateau is \(h/(2e^2)=12.9\) kΩ. A weak current of \(0.500\) μA would produce a transverse voltage magnitude of \(6.45\) mV. Increasing the field at fixed density decreases filling and can move the Fermi level into another broadened level. A simultaneous longitudinal voltage measurement is indispensable: a Hall voltage of the right numerical size at a single field can also occur in a classical conductor. A broad flat Hall trace together with a longitudinal-resistance minimum gives stronger evidence for quantized transport.

Changing the sample area at constant sheet density and magnetic field increases the number of occupied particles and the number of orbit centres in the same proportion. Filling factor and the ideal Hall resistance therefore remain unchanged, even though total particle count can be very different. In contrast, a gate that varies sheet density at fixed field moves the layer through successive fillings. The expected density spacing per spin-resolved level is \(eB/h\). A gate-calibrated scan can thus test Landau degeneracy independently of the cyclotron energy spacing inferred from optical or thermal observations.

When can a Landau-level spacing clear thermal broadening?

For a two-dimensional band with isotropic mass \(0.12m_e\) and negligible spin splitting, calculate \(\hbar e B/m^*\). Compare the result with the thermal energy for two temperatures. This gives a scale for designing a sweep, rather than a sharp visibility criterion.

Superconductivity, magnetic screening and flux quantisation

Condensed matter and band theory

Separate persistent current from magnetic flux expulsion and derive two measurable length and flux scales of a superconducting state.

From first principles

Connect a material current law to magnetic screening

A vanishing electrical resistance by itself allows an already existing current to persist. To predict the equilibrium field profile in a thick superconductor one needs additional physical input. In a uniform isotropic superconductor with no vortices the second London relation connects the curl of the supercurrent to the induction. Here \(n_s\) counts carriers of mass \(m\) and signed charge \(q\). Combine this constitutive law with static Ampère’s law:

\[\nabla\times\mathbf j_s=-\frac{n_s q^2}{m}\mathbf B,\]
\[\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf j_s .\]

Take a second curl of the field law and use \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). The sign from the vector identity cancels that in the London relation:

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\frac{\mathbf B}{\lambda_L^2},\]
\[\lambda_L=\sqrt{\frac{m}{\mu_0n_s q^2}}.\]

Fill \(x>0\) with the material and impose a parallel surface field \(B(0)=B_0\). With no field growth deep in the bulk, only one exponential survives:

\[B(x)=B_0e^{-x/\lambda_L}.\]

Thus the single measured length constrains a ratio \(m/(n_s q^2)\). This construction assumes linear local response and a field low enough for the vortex-free state and its prescribed surface condition to apply.

Background · London length
\[B(x)=B(0)e^{-x/\lambda_L}\]

This is a field profile in a thick homogeneous superconductor in a linear screening regime. A thin slab requires both boundary conditions.

Derivation and applications

Determine how deeply a parallel field penetrates a finite slab

The exponential London solution assumes a sample extending far beyond a single exposed surface. A film has two surfaces whose screening currents can overlap. Consider an infinite slab of total thickness \(2d\), occupying \(-d<x<d\). Apply a static induction \(B_a\) parallel to its faces, along \(z\), and assume a uniform London penetration length \(\lambda\). Work in the vortex-free Meissner state with fields small enough for linear response. Translational invariance parallel to the slab gives the boundary problem

\[\frac{d^2 B_z}{dx^2}=\frac{B_z}{\lambda^2},\]
\[B_z(-d)=B_z(d)=B_a .\]

A general solution has a hyperbolic cosine and a hyperbolic sine. Reflection about the midplane makes the field even, which removes the sine term. The surface field fixes the remaining coefficient. Ampère’s law then determines the screening current, whose sign reverses at the midplane,

\[B_z(x)=B_a\frac{\cosh(x/\lambda)}{\cosh(d/\lambda)},\]
\[J_y(x)=-\frac{B_a}{\mu_0\lambda}\frac{\sinh(x/\lambda)}{\cosh(d/\lambda)}.\]

A probe at the centre and a probe integrating flux over the cross-section read different quantities. The latter reports the thickness average obtained by integrating the field profile,

\[\frac{\overline B}{B_a}=\frac{1}{2dB_a}\int_{-d}^d B_z(x)\,dx=\frac{\lambda}{d}\tanh(d/\lambda).\]

For a thick slab the mean fraction tends to \(\lambda/d\), consistent with two active surface layers. For a thin slab it is \(1-(d/\lambda)^2/3\) to leading order. Thin samples can therefore exhibit weak parallel-field exclusion even when the material is fully superconducting throughout their volume.

Worked example

Compare central and average field in a thick slab

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A sample of thickness \(600\,\mathrm{nm}\) with \(\lambda=100\,\mathrm{nm}\) has \(d/\lambda=3\). In \(B_a=2.00\,\mathrm{mT}\) its central field is

\[2.00/\cosh3=0.199\,\mathrm{mT}.\]

The mean induction is

\[2.00\,\tanh(3)/3=0.663\,\mathrm{mT}.\]

The higher mean follows directly from the substantial contribution of material close to both exposed faces.

Second application

Extract a penetration length using a local probe

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A probe halfway through a \(120\,\mathrm{nm}\) film measures \(0.800 B_a\). The central-field solution gives \(d/\lambda=\operatorname{arcosh}(1.25)=0.693\), so \(\lambda=86.6\,\mathrm{nm}\). The same film should transmit a mean fraction \(\tanh(0.693)/0.693=0.866\). Comparing this value with an independent flux integral tests the assumptions of uniform thickness and a single penetration length.

Try it · Solid optics methods london slab

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Compare two magnetic histories

A normal conductor dissipates energy from a sustained current. A superconductor can support persistent current and, in an appropriate low-field equilibrium state, expels magnetic flux from its bulk. Imagine cooling a specimen through its transition in a field and subsequently measuring its interior. Perfect conductivity alone would imply that a previously existing flux could remain frozen. Equilibrium flux expulsion supplies additional evidence about the state; sample shape, vortices and pinning affect what a real experiment detects. A resistance measurement and a magnetisation measurement therefore probe different properties.

Derive the London screening length

Let mobile condensate carriers have density \(n_s\), mass \(m_s\) and charge \(q_s\), using a consistent convention for particles or pairs. In a bulk region without vortices the London relation is

\[\nabla\times\mathbf j_s=-(n_s q_s^2/m_s)\mathbf B.\]

Combine this with magnetostatic Ampère's law \(\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf j_s\) and \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). Taking a curl gives

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\mathbf B/\lambda_L^2,\]
\[\lambda_L=\sqrt{m_s/(\mu_0n_s q_s^2)} .\]

For a half-space \(x>0\) with surface field parallel to the surface and deep interior field zero,

\[B(x)=B(0)e^{-x/\lambda_L}.\]

At depth \(3\lambda_L\), 4.98% of the surface amplitude remains. Taking \(n_s=2.0\times10^{28}\) m⁻³ electrons and electron mass in the simple carrier convention gives \(\lambda_L\approx37.6\) nm. Real effective masses, temperature-dependent superfluid density and nonlocality can change this value. Mixing pair density with single-electron charge would introduce a systematic factor error.

Quantise a circulating phase

A coherent condensate has a phase \(\phi\) whose change around a closed path must be \(2\pi n\) for integer \(n\) if the complex order parameter is single-valued and nonzero along the path. The gauge-invariant momentum is \(m_s\mathbf v_s=\hbar\nabla\phi-q_s\mathbf A\). Integrating around a loop relates circulation and flux. If a path lies sufficiently deep in a thick ring that its current density is negligible, the velocity term vanishes and enclosed flux comes in units \(\Phi_0=h/|q_s|\). For paired carriers \(|q_s|=2e\) the unit is \(2.068\times10^{-15}\) Wb. In thin rings where the kinetic term matters, it is the full fluxoid combination that is quantised.

Use length scales to interpret the experiment

The penetration depth characterises field screening. A separate coherence length \(\xi\) measures the distance over which the condensate amplitude recovers after a perturbation. In bulk Ginzburg–Landau theory near the transition, the ratio \(\lambda/\xi\) distinguishes type I and type II at \(1/\sqrt2\). In a type-II material between the lower and upper critical fields, flux can penetrate in quantised vortices with normal-like cores surrounded by screening currents. A specimen in this mixed state can retain superconducting regions while admitting magnetic flux. Stating field, temperature and cooling history is essential when interpreting the observed signal.

Locate the upper critical field from the linearized order parameter

The London equation assumes an established superconducting density. Near the loss of superconductivity at a high applied field, the order parameter itself becomes small and its spatial variation matters. For a bulk type-II material near its transition use Ginzburg–Landau free-energy density terms \(\alpha|\psi|^2+\beta|\psi|^4/2+|(-i\hbar\nabla-q_s\mathbf A)\psi|^2/(2m_s)\) with \(\alpha<0\), \(\beta>0\) and pair charge magnitude \(|q_s|=2e\). Very close to the upper critical field the density is small, field screening is weak and a uniform induction \(B\) is a suitable leading approximation.

Minimizing the free energy with respect to \(\psi^*\) and discarding the cubic term gives a charged-particle magnetic eigenproblem. The lowest transverse kinetic eigenvalue is the lowest Landau energy \(\hbar|q_s|B/(2m_s)\), achieved with no longitudinal variation. A nontrivial small solution is possible at the onset when this cancels \(\alpha\). Defining \(\xi^2=\hbar^2/(2m_s|\alpha|)\) and \(\Phi_0=h/|q_s|\) gives

\[B_{c2}=\frac{\Phi_0}{2\pi\xi^2}.\]

Thus a coherence length inferred from upper critical induction measures the scale of condensate recovery. Penetration depth instead controls field screening in an already developed condensate. The GL derivation is controlled near the critical temperature and for orbital suppression. Strong paramagnetic pair breaking or pronounced anisotropy can complicate the interpretation of a measured high-field transition.

Connect critical induction to vortex spacing

At a specified temperature close to the transition a bulk specimen loses its superconducting response at \(B_{c2}=1.20\) T. The inferred coherence length is

\[\sqrt{2.068\times10^{-15}/(2\pi\times1.20)}=16.6\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

An independent penetration-depth measurement at the same temperature yields \(\lambda=120\) nm, giving \(\lambda/\xi=7.24\), above the type-II boundary. Using lengths measured at unrelated temperatures would weaken that check.

At mean induction 0.200 T within the mixed state each singly quantized vortex carries one flux quantum. Its share of sample area is

\[\Phi_0/B=1.03\times10^{-14}\,\mathrm{m^2}.\]

For a triangular lattice that area also equals \(\sqrt3\,a_v^2/2\), giving vortex spacing \(a_v=109\) nm. Increasing induction fourfold halves the spacing if the array remains in the mixed state and retains its geometry. Near \(B_{c2}\) cores overlap and an isolated-vortex picture becomes unreliable even though the linearized onset scale remains useful. Comparing a measured spacing with \(\Phi_0/B\) tests flux counting independently of the inferred coherence length and of the magnetic field profile around any single vortex.

A useful unit check compares the superconducting flux quantum with the electron flux scale from ordinary Landau-level counting. A condensate pair has twice the charge magnitude of one electron, giving half the flux quantum for a phase winding. At 0.200 T a square micrometre should contain about \(BA/\Phi_0=96.7\) singly quantized vortices away from edges. Counting vortices in an image therefore gives a bulk average induction estimate independent of a local peak-field calibration. A surface barrier, pinning or an insufficiently large field of view can account for a local deviation from the average.

Imaging successively larger regions tests how well such local counts represent the bulk.

Counting carriers and weighing a cyclotron orbit

Condensed matter and band theory

A conical band has no constant quadratic band mass at its touching point. A carrier on a finite-energy orbit still has a measurable cyclotron mass. Counting states fixes the Fermi circle and determines how this mass changes when a gate adds charge.

Count states in a two-dimensional disc

Put a two-dimensional sheet of area \(A=L_xL_y\) under periodic boundary conditions. Neighbouring allowed wavevectors differ by \(2\pi/L_x\) and \(2\pi/L_y\), so each state occupies reciprocal-space area \((2\pi)^2/A\). At zero temperature fill all conduction states up to radius \(k_F\), measured from a Dirac point. There are two spin states and two inequivalent valleys in ideal monolayer graphene, giving total degeneracy \(g=4\). Density relative to charge neutrality is therefore

\[n=g\frac{\pi k_F^2}{(2\pi)^2}=\frac{k_F^2}{\pi},\]
\[E_F=\hbar v_F\sqrt{\pi n}.\]

For electron and hole densities use \(|n|\) under the square root and give \(E_F\) the sign of \(n\) relative to the touching point. Differentiating the state count with respect to energy gives the density of states per area \(D(E)=g|E|/[2\pi(\hbar v_F)^2]\). It tends to zero at an ideal neutral cone. Thermal broadening, disorder and a gap all change what a finite-resolution probe observes near neutrality.

Background · Cyclotron mass
\[m_c=(\hbar^2/2\pi)\,\partial A/\partial E\]

The area is that of a closed constant-energy orbit in reciprocal space normal to the magnetic field. This is a measured orbital mass rather than a universal free-particle parameter.

Let a perpendicular field bend the orbit

For a wavepacket in a band with weak magnetic field,

\[\hbar\dot{\boldsymbol k}=-e\,\boldsymbol v\times\boldsymbol B,\]

where \(e\) denotes the positive elementary charge. An isotropic band has velocity normal to a circular constant-energy contour. The orbit is traversed with speed \(|\dot{\boldsymbol k}|=e v_F B/\hbar\). Dividing its circumference \(2\pi k_F\) by that speed gives the period. Express its angular frequency as \(eB/m_c\) to identify

\[m_c=\frac{\hbar k_F}{v_F}=\frac{|E_F|}{v_F^2}.\]

The mass measures the time needed to travel around a contour. It depends on the radius even when the magnitude of the group velocity stays fixed. For a general band area \(\mathcal A(E)\), neighbouring contours separated perpendicular to themselves by \(dk_\perp\) differ in energy by \(\hbar v\,dk_\perp\). Thus \(|d\mathcal A/dE|=\oint dl/(\hbar v)\), which gives \(m_c=\hbar^2|d\mathcal A/dE|/(2\pi)\) by integrating the same equation of motion.

Convert a gate density into an energy and a mass

At \(n=10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}=10^{16}\,\mathrm{m^{-2}}\) and \(v_F=10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\), one obtains \(k_F=1.772\times10^8\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}\), \(E_F=0.117\,\mathrm{eV}\) and \(m_c=1.87\times10^{-32}\,\mathrm{kg}=0.0205m_e\). Quadrupling the density doubles the Fermi energy and cyclotron mass. By contrast a single isotropic parabolic band \(E=\hbar^2 k^2/(2m)\) has the same counting geometry but constant \(m_c=m\). Transport mobility or disorder scattering time does not enter the state count, although scattering can obscure the cyclotron motion.

Change velocity and signed density. Raising the velocity at fixed density increases \(|E_F|\) while reducing the mass because the orbit radius stays fixed and the wavepacket moves faster. Exactly at charge neutrality, a single classical Fermi orbit no longer describes the carriers. Finite-field quantum levels replace the continuum picture in the next chapter.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a density sweep

Two cyclotron measurements on the same gapless sheet return masses \(0.024m_e\) and \(0.036m_e\). Assume the velocity is independent of density. Infer the ratio of absolute carrier densities and the change in the weak-field cyclotron frequency at fixed field.

Work through the solution

Since \(m_c\) scales as \(\sqrt{|n|}\), the second density is \((0.036/0.024)^2=2.25\) times the first. The frequency \(eB/m_c\) drops to two thirds of its first value. The masses alone cannot reveal whether either measurement used electrons or holes.

Find the weak-field cyclotron radius at the worked density \(10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\) and field \(0.10\,\mathrm T\). How does raising the velocity at fixed density affect it?

Work through the solution

The semiclassical radius is

\[r_c=v_Fm_c/(eB)=\hbar k_F/(eB).\]

With \(k_F=1.772\times10^8\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\),

\[r_c=1.17\,\mu\mathrm m.\]

At fixed density and field the radius stays fixed when velocity changes. The orbit period changes because the speed around that circle changes.

A measurement doubles positive \(E_F\) at constant temperature sufficiently close to zero for the step filling approximation. How do state density per area and density of states per energy change?

Work through the solution

For fixed \(v_F\), the occupied density scales as \(E_F^2\), so it rises fourfold. The derivative \(D(E_F)\) scales as \(|E_F|\) and doubles. Confusing the count with its derivative would give the wrong charge change inferred from spectroscopy.

Further reading · Novoselov and colleagues, measured energy dependence of the cyclotron mass.

Quantising the orbit and finding the zero Landau level

Condensed matter and band theory

In a magnetic field the two kinetic momentum components cannot be specified simultaneously. In a conical band their commutator turns the two sublattice equations into coupled oscillator equations. Their spectra differ by one state, leaving an unpaired level at zero energy.

Construct the oscillator from kinetic momentum

In one valley of a gapless monolayer use the long-wavelength Hamiltonian \(H=v_F(\sigma_x\Pi_x+\sigma_y\Pi_y)\), with Pauli matrices acting on sublattices. For an electron of charge \(-e\),

\[\boldsymbol\Pi=\boldsymbol p+e\boldsymbol A.\]

In a uniform perpendicular field \(B>0\), applying \([p_j,A_k]=-i\hbar\partial_j A_k\) gives

\[[\Pi_x,\Pi_y]=-ie\hbar B.\]

Introduce \(a=(\Pi_x-i\Pi_y)/\sqrt{2e\hbar B}\). Its adjoint uses the opposite sign, giving \([a,a^\dagger]=1\). The magnetic length \(\ell_B=\sqrt{\hbar/(eB)}\) sets the spatial size of the oscillator.

\[H=v_F\sqrt{2e\hbar B}\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\a^\dagger&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad H^2=2e\hbar Bv_F^2\begin{pmatrix}aa^\dagger&0\\0&a^\dagger a\end{pmatrix}.\]

In the B component the squared Hamiltonian has oscillator numbers \(0,1,2,\ldots\). In the A component \(aa^\dagger=a^\dagger a+1\), so its sequence starts at one. For integer \(n\ge1\) combine \(|n-1\rangle\) on A with \(\pm|n\rangle\) on B in equal magnitudes. Acting with \(H\) confirms energies \(E_{n,\pm}=\pm v_F\sqrt{2e\hbar Bn}\). For \(n=0\), the B-only spinor with \(|0\rangle\) is annihilated by \(a\). Its energy vanishes. Valley and field conventions can exchange which sublattice carries that state.

Count independent centres at the same energy

Choose gauge \(A_y=Bx\) and retain translation along \(y\). Quantisation gives

\[k_y=2\pi j/L_y.\]

The kinetic momentum \(\Pi_y=\hbar k_y+eBx\) places the orbit centre at \(x_0=-\hbar k_y/(eB)\). Successive centres differ by \(2\pi\ell_B^2/L_y\). Counting centres in a width \(L_x\) gives \(L_xL_y/(2\pi\ell_B^2)\), or \(eB/h\) states per area for each spin and valley, apart from edge corrections. This degeneracy is independent of level index. Spin splitting, lattice effects and interactions are set aside here.

At \(B=1\,\mathrm T\), \(v_F=10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\), \(\ell_B=25.7\,\mathrm{nm}\) and the first positive level is \(36.3\,\mathrm{meV}\) above zero. The next sits at \(51.3\,\mathrm{meV}\). The spacing between the first two positive levels is consequently only 15.0 meV. At 4 T all these energies double while the number of orbit centres per unit area quadruples. A parabolic band would instead give equally spaced levels at fixed field.

Identify the measurable dependence

Compare the first few positive levels as field changes. Dividing \(E_n\) by \(\sqrt{Bn}\) should return one material-dependent coefficient. An optical measurement sees transitions between occupied and available states, subject to matrix-element selection rules. A measured transition energy therefore need not coincide with the energy of an individual level. At zero field this discrete-level construction loses its normalisable magnetic oscillator; the limiting energies on the graph describe the approach from finite field.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on two spectral lines

At \(B=2\,\mathrm T\) an ideal sample has a zero-to-first level spacing of \(48\,\mathrm{meV}\). Infer its velocity relative to \(10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\). Predict the first-to-second positive-level spacing at \(8\,\mathrm T\).

Work through the solution

The reference first level at 2 T is \(36.3\sqrt2=51.3\,\mathrm{meV}\), so \(v_F\simeq0.935\times10^6\,\mathrm{m/s}\). Raising the field by a factor of four doubles all energies. The first level at 8 T is 96 meV, and \(E_2-E_1=96(\sqrt2-1)=39.8\,\mathrm{meV}\). This inference assumes an ungapped cone without interaction shifts.

At \(B=2\,\mathrm T\), estimate the number of orbit centres in area \(1\,\mu\mathrm m^2\) for a single level. Give both the per-flavour count and the full spin–valley count.

Work through the solution

Use \(eBA/h\), with \(A=10^{-12}\,\mathrm m^2\). This gives roughly 484 centres per flavour and 1934 across four flavours. The numbers are approximate because a finite sample admits edge corrections, whereas the area formula is a bulk degeneracy density.

At high positive level index show how the level spacing regains the semiclassical cyclotron frequency \(eB/m_c\).

Work through the solution

Write \(E_n^2=2e\hbar B v_F^2 n\). At large \(n\) the difference \(E_{n+1}-E_n\simeq dE/dn=e\hbar Bv_F^2/E_n\). Dividing by \(\hbar\) gives \(eB/m_c\) with \(m_c=E_n/v_F^2\). This connection uses many occupied levels, while the unpaired zero state is outside the semiclassical limit.

Further reading · Jiang and colleagues, infrared spectroscopy of Landau levels in monolayer graphene · Li and colleagues, spatially resolved graphene Landau levels.

Identify mobile charge with a transverse field

Fields and the response of matter

Derive a Hall-voltage measurement from transverse force balance, reverse current and field to reject offsets and explore what fails when two carrier types conduct.

Choose signs from a drawing

A rectangular conducting sample has its long axis along \(x\), width \(w\) along \(y\), and thickness \(t\) along \(z\). A uniform magnetic field \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\) is applied. An external circuit maintains conventional current \(I>0\) in \(+x\). High-impedance transverse probes draw negligible current. Define the measured signal as \(V_H=V(y=0)-V(y=w)\). For uniform transverse field this convention gives

\[V_H=E_yw.\]

Reversing the voltmeter leads reverses the reported sign; writing a carrier sign without a lead convention is incomplete.

With one charge carrier species of number density \(n\) and signed charge \(q\), the drift velocity satisfies

\[j_x=nqv_x=I/(wt).\]

If the transverse velocity vanishes, its magnetic force component is \(-qv_xB\), opposed by \(qE_y\). Thus \(E_y=v_x B=j_xB/(nq)\). The resulting port measurement is

\[V_H=\frac{IB}{nq t}.\]

The width cancels for a uniform bar; changing the contact geometry or using a current source with a nonuniform injection profile can spoil that simplification near the terminals. The cancellation also exposes the sign. For electrons \(q<0\), positive conventional current means that the mobile charge drifts in \(-x\). The inferred transverse field is negative for positive \(B\) in the chosen convention.

Reconstruct density and mobility from separate observations

Take \(t=0.50\) mm, \(I=5.0\) mA, \(B=0.20\) T and a transverse signal of \(-1.25\) mV. The inferred signed Hall coefficient is

\[R_H=tV_H/(IB)=-6.25\times10^{-4}\ {\rm m^3/C}.\]

Using singly charged electrons gives

\[n=1/(e|R_H|)\simeq1.00\times10^{22}\ {\rm m^{-3}}.\]

A longitudinal voltage measurement, after correcting for probe separation, can yield the material conductivity. If \(\sigma=80\) S/m, the single-carrier mobility is

\[\mu=\sigma |R_H|=0.050\ {\rm m^2/(V\,s)}.\]

These operations use different pieces of information. The longitudinal measurement alone gives only the product \(n|q|\mu\). The transverse measurement supplies a sign and, in the one-carrier model, the density independently of mobility. This is an inverse experiment whose two observables have different sensitivities, not a more precise measurement of the same scalar.

One can derive the same result from a stationary Drude equation \(0=q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)-m\mathbf v/\tau\). The longitudinal drag gives

\[E_x=mv_x/(q\tau);\]

force balance in \(y\) gives the transverse field just obtained. In a homogeneous single-carrier sample, \(E_y/E_x=q\tau B/m\), the signed Hall angle tangent. At fixed sample and field, doubling \(I\) doubles both voltages in this linear regime and leaves their ratio unchanged. Significant current-dependent apparent density can indicate heating or nonlinear contacts.

Isolate the transverse voltage from a misaligned contact

Practical side contacts seldom sit at exactly the same \(x\). A fraction of the longitudinal voltage can leak into the transverse pair. Write the readout at a fixed current as \(V_\mathrm{m}(B)=V_0+\alpha V_{xx}+V_H(B)\). A background that is even in field cancels in \([V_\mathrm m(B)-V_\mathrm m(-B)]/2\). For example, readings of 3.05 mV and 5.55 mV at \(+B,-B\) return \(-1.25\) mV Hall signal and 4.30 mV of even background. Taking a single measured sign would have diagnosed the wrong carriers.

Open the live experiment

The plot deliberately superposes a constant 4.3 mV pickup on a signed Hall signal. Vary density and current independently before reading the pair at \(B=\pm0.2\) T. Large density weakens the signal; current increases it. Verify that field reversal recovers the correct transverse voltage even when both instrument readings have the same sign. If the unwanted background changes between readings, reverse the field several times and check for repeatable drift.

With both current and field reversal, the double-odd combination \([V(+I,+B)-V(+I,-B)-V(-I,+B)+V(-I,-B)]/4\) isolates terms odd in both. A thermal emf that is independent of current cancels along with a stable contact offset. This symmetry decomposition is a physical assumption about the parasitics, so preserve all four readings and inspect the rejected channels too.

When a Hall density stops being a particle count

With electrons and holes, longitudinal conductivities add. In a weak magnetic field their transverse contributions also depend on squared mobilities. For positive mobilities \(\mu_e,\mu_h\) and electron and hole densities \(n,p\), the two-carrier weak-field coefficient in the sign convention above is \(R_H=(p\mu_h^2-n\mu_e^2)/[e(p\mu_h+n\mu_e)^2]\). To see where the square appears, note that one factor of mobility converts the longitudinal field into drift; a second converts the resulting transverse magnetic force into a drift contribution. Summing species currents and imposing zero total transverse current gives the denominator management through the summed longitudinal conductivity.

Equal electron and hole densities with equal mobilities give zero Hall signal while current still flows readily. A single-species inversion would suggest unbounded density. At equal densities but unequal mobilities, the faster species can set the sign even though neither species is more numerous. Additional field dependence, temperature variation and independent structural information are needed before treating a Hall number as a carrier census.

Tutorial

A measurement doubles the width \(w\) at fixed thickness, total current and field in a uniform sample. Predict both \(E_y\) and \(V_H\).

Work through the solution

The current density halves, so \(v_x\) and \(E_y\) halve. The voltage integrates that halved field over twice the width and stays the same. If fixed current density were held instead, the transverse voltage would double.

A nominal thickness is overestimated by 5%. What bias does this cause in the single-carrier density inferred from a fixed transverse signal?

Work through the solution

\(n=|IB|/(e t|V_H|)\), so using \(1.05t\) gives \(n_\mathrm{fit}=n/1.05\), approximately 4.8% low. This systematic error is common to all repeated field sweeps.

Why might a metal yield a smaller Hall voltage than a semiconductor with the same dimensions at the same current?

Work through the solution

In the one-carrier regime the signal is inversely proportional to carrier density. A large density allows the imposed current at a very small drift speed, requiring a correspondingly small transverse balancing field. Conductivity alone cannot determine this comparison without mobility information.

Follow the transport model

Compare the one-carrier assumptions with the treatment of mobile carriers in David Tong's solid-state physics course. Connect an independently measured conductivity with the signed transverse response before interpreting an effective mass or scattering time.

Distinguish magnetic screening from electrical diffusion

Fields and the response of matter

Compare the frequency dependence, phase and thickness dependence of field penetration in a normal metal and a superconducting slab.

Perfect conductivity and magnetic expulsion are different statements

A normal conductor has current density \(\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf E\). Combining Faraday's and low-frequency Ampère laws in a homogeneous medium gives

\[\partial_t\mathbf B=(\mu\sigma)^{-1}\nabla^2\mathbf B.\]

A step in field diffuses inwards over a length of order \(\sqrt{t/(\mu\sigma)}\). Under sustained sinusoidal drive the skin depth is \(\delta=\sqrt{2/(\mu\sigma\omega)}\). As \(\omega\) tends to zero, this skin depth tends to infinity. An ordinary nonmagnetic conductor in its final static equilibrium generally admits a uniform applied magnetic field.

The London description of an isotropic superconductor in a Meissner state makes a distinct, equilibrium constitutive assumption:

\[\nabla\times\mathbf J_s=-\mathbf B/(\mu_0\lambda_L^2),\]

with penetration length \(\lambda_L\). In a homogeneous specimen carrying supercurrent and at frequencies where displacement current and normal losses may be neglected,

\[\nabla\times\mathbf B=\mu_0\mathbf J_s.\]

Curl once more and use \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). The resulting field satisfies

\[\nabla^2\mathbf B=\mathbf B/\lambda_L^2.\]

Unlike the diffusive skin depth, the equilibrium length remains finite for a static applied field.

Taking the formal limit \(\sigma\to\infty\) in a normal conductor suppresses the electric field for a finite current and can freeze a pre-existing flux during changes. That argument does not determine which static field a specimen adopts when cooled through its transition. Magnetic expulsion is extra thermodynamic physics. The comparison below assumes a weak enough field and a suitable state, omitting flux vortices, trapped flux and nonlinear or nonlocal effects.

Solve a slab with equal applied fields on both faces

Let a broad slab fill \(-d/2<x<d/2\), and impose identical tangential magnetic fields \(B_a\hat{\mathbf z}\) on its two faces. Symmetry requires \(B'(0)=0\). Applying both boundaries to the London equation gives

\[B(x)=B_a\frac{\cosh(x/\lambda_L)}{\cosh[d/(2\lambda_L)]},\qquad\]

\frac{\overline B}{B_a}=\frac{2\lambda_L}{d}\tanh\frac{d}{2\lambda_L}.

Differentiating gives the screening current \(J_y=-B'_z/\mu_0\). Currents flow in opposite directions at opposite faces; their net transport current across the thickness is zero. For a thick slab each face approaches a half-space with exponential decay. For \(d\ll\lambda_L\), the two surface layers overlap. Expanding the average gives \(1-d^2/(12\lambda_L^2)+\cdots\), so even a superconductor can have a substantial mean internal field in a sufficiently thin specimen under this parallel-field geometry.

Take \(d=100\) nm and \(\lambda_L=50\) nm. The centre field is \(B_a/\cosh 1\simeq0.6481 B_a\); the mean is \(\tanh1\simeq0.7616 B_a\). The distinction between centre value and volume average is already larger than many instrumental error bars. A measurement of magnetic moment in a finite non-slab geometry additionally requires its demagnetising field. Do not directly substitute the infinite-slab mean into an arbitrary sample-shape calibration.

Obtain the equilibrium length as an energy minimum

The same static profile follows from minimising \(\int_{-d/2}^{d/2}[B^2+\lambda_L^2(B')^2]\,dx/(2\mu_0)\) with boundary values imposed. The first term is magnetic field energy; the second is the energy associated with the screening current in this effective description. Vary \(B\) by a function \(\eta\) vanishing at the faces. Integrating \(B'\eta'\) by parts leaves an interior factor \(B-\lambda_L^2B''\). Its vanishing returns the London equation. This route makes the physical tradeoff clear: complete expulsion would require extremely sharp gradients and costly currents, while uniform penetration pays magnetic energy throughout the bulk.

The reduction assumes a fixed superconducting condensate amplitude. Near a critical temperature or critical field, allowing that amplitude to change introduces an additional coherence length and may alter both the field state and the validity of London theory. A numerical fit giving a stable value of \(\lambda_L\) at one temperature does not prove this approximation over an entire phase diagram.

Compare two experimental controls

Open the live experiment

Change the thickness and the penetration length independently. Find two different specimens with the same \(d/\lambda_L\); their normalised profiles against fractional thickness match, even though their local physical gradients and current densities can differ. If all you can measure is a single normalised profile, geometry calibration is part of the inference of \(\lambda_L\).

A frequency sweep supplies another distinction. In a normal sheet the complex field has both attenuation and phase lag with depth, and \(\delta\) changes as \(f^{-1/2}\). The static London profile above is real and determined by a material length. At higher frequencies superconductors can also exhibit complex response and dissipation, so measuring a single attenuation length in one frequency band cannot by itself establish static flux expulsion. Use the normal-film impedance experiment for a controlled comparison with its different rear boundary condition.

Tutorial

In a thick specimen, how far from one isolated face must a probe be to see 1% of the applied static field? How does the answer depend on field amplitude in this model?

Work through the solution

The half-space profile is \(e^{-x/\lambda_L}\). Set it to 0.01 to obtain

\[x=\lambda_L\ln100\simeq4.605\lambda_L.\]

Linearity makes this scaled distance independent of \(B_a\), until the assumed Meissner and weak-field conditions fail.

For a symmetric slab with \(d=4\lambda_L\), compute the centre and mean fractions.

Work through the solution

The centre gives \(1/\cosh2\simeq0.266\). The mean is \(\tanh2/2\simeq0.482\). Both surface layers contribute substantially to the average even when the centre field is much smaller.

A phase-sensitive measurement detects a substantial depth-dependent phase lag in a slowly driven slab. Does the static London solution predict this?

Work through the solution

The static real solution has no phase lag. Assess normal-fluid losses, vortex motion, measurement phase reference and the finite drive frequency. Comparing only magnitudes can conceal these distinct mechanisms.

Read the equilibrium model

The MIT applied-superconductivity course places London's laws alongside microscopic and order-parameter treatments. Use that ordering to identify the assumptions needed when taking a static limit.

How a magnetic field rotates the polarisation of transmitted light

Fields and the response of matter

Resolve a linearly polarised wave into circular eigenmodes, infer a refractive-index difference from an analyser and distinguish nonreciprocal rotation from a simple change of reference axes.

Derive rotation from propagation rather than drawing an arrow

Let monochromatic light propagate along \(+z\) through a transparent magneto-optic sample, with an externally prepared axial magnetic field. Define fixed laboratory vectors \(\mathbf e_\pm=(\hat x\pm i\hat y)/\sqrt2\) and use phasors \(e^{i(kz-\omega t)}\). These definitions specify the handedness algebraically. Calling a vector simply “right circular” without stating the viewing and time conventions can reverse a reported rotation sign.

An input linearly polarised in \(x\) is \((\mathbf e_++\mathbf e_-)/\sqrt2\). Suppose the medium supports wave numbers \(k_\pm=2\pi n_\pm/\lambda_0\) for the two bases, with negligible absorption. After a distance \(L\), factor out the common phase \(e^{i(k_++k_-)L/2}\). The remaining Cartesian phasor is \(\hat x\cos\Delta-\hat y\sin\Delta\), where \(\Delta=(k_+-k_-)L/2\). Thus the polarisation angle in the fixed \(x,y\) plane is

\[\theta=\frac{\pi L}{\lambda_0}(n_- - n_+).\]

Equal transmissions of the two components preserve a linearly polarised output. Unequal absorption makes the circular amplitudes unequal and generally produces an ellipse. Rotating a linear analyser then finds a finite minimum, even for a perfectly coherent field. Residual intensity at the nominal extinction angle can also arise from stray light or imperfect polariser extinction, which must be measured independently.

Derive a magneto-optic eigenbasis

For axial magnetisation, a simple lossless transverse relative-permittivity block is \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\epsilon&i g\\-i g&\epsilon\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) with real \(g\) at the frequency of observation. Acting on \((1,i)\) gives \((\epsilon-g)(1,i)\); acting on \((1,-i)\) gives \((\epsilon+g)(1,-i)\). For relative permeability near one, \(n_+^2=\epsilon-g\), \(n_-^2=\epsilon+g\). At small \(g\) their difference \(n_- - n_+\simeq g/\sqrt\epsilon\). Reversing magnetisation reverses the sign of the off-diagonal coefficient and interchanges the two eigenvalues. Both the existence of circular modes and the sign-reversal test now follow from a constitutive model.

If \(n_--n_+\) is proportional to magnetic field over a specified range, write \(\theta=VBL\), with Verdet constant \(V\) in rad/(T m). This is a measurable material and wavelength dependent coefficient. It is not determined by the mean refractive index alone. A strong nearby absorption line can give substantial frequency dependence and dichroism, invalidating a lossless narrow-band extrapolation.

Work through an analyser calibration

For \(\lambda_0=600\) nm, \(L=0.20\) mm and measured index difference \(5.0\times10^{-4}\), the rotation is \(\pi(2.0\times10^{-4})(5.0\times10^{-4})/(6.0\times10^{-7})=\pi/6\), or 30°. An ideal analyser at angle \(\alpha\) transmits \(I/I_\mathrm{in}=\cos^2(\alpha-\theta)\). Its maxima occur at \(30^\circ\) and \(210^\circ\); an intensity measurement determines a linear polarisation angle modulo 180°. For a sample thick enough to rotate several half turns, an intensity maximum alone cannot give an unwrapped \(\theta\). Vary field or path length continuously from a known reference.

If the applied field is independently 0.40 T in that example, \(V=\theta/(BL)\simeq6.54\times10^3\) rad/(T m). The arithmetic is dimensionally correct, but the large value warrants a physical check of thickness, field calibration, units and material spectrum before reporting it as an ordinary glass coefficient. Degrees substituted for radians would make the inferred material coefficient too large by \(180/\pi\).

Use field reversal to reject alignment errors

Open the live experiment

The experiment shows the predicted analyser traces for opposite index splitting, with the same input polariser. Increase sample thickness to see why tracking the maximum by one measurement can select the wrong branch. Reversing the splitting reverses the tilt in fixed laboratory axes. A fixed analyser offset shifts both inferred angles equally and cancels in half their difference, provided the branch has been unwrapped consistently.

For small rotation, an analyser exactly at a crossed position transmits intensity proportional to \(\theta^2\). Its response has no sign sensitivity close to zero. Set the analyser at 45° instead:

\[\cos^2(\pi/4-\theta)=[1+\sin(2\theta)]/2\simeq1/2+\theta.\]

The difference between fields \(+B\) and \(-B\) then measures a signed signal linear in small rotation. This trades extinction of the background for sensitivity to sign. Balance the two orthogonal analyser outputs if source intensity fluctuates appreciably during a sweep.

Ask what happens on a return pass

For the ideal Faraday medium at fixed magnetisation, the circular eigenmodes relative to a fixed laboratory basis are determined by its transverse material tensor. On propagation back through the sample, the accumulated positive propagation path again distinguishes the same laboratory circular basis after accounting for the reflection boundary. With a suitable simple mirror at normal incidence, the resulting material rotation doubles on a round trip in fixed transverse coordinates. In a reciprocal optically active material, the eigenvalue assignment with respect to the laboratory basis reverses on reversing propagation, and the single-pass rotation cancels on the ideal return. Write both transformations in one fixed basis before comparing signs.

A real double-pass setup includes mirror phase shifts, stress birefringence of windows and possible ellipticity. Reversing magnetic field, removing the sample and recording both analyser quadratures provide separate checks. A nonzero rotation in one transmitted beam proves only that its polarisation changed; establishing nonreciprocity requires comparing a reversed propagation experiment with a stated polarisation convention.

Tutorial

Two analyser maxima occur at 47° and 39° for opposite weak fields. If the response is linear and the same branch is tracked, what field-induced rotation and static offset do they imply?

Work through the solution

Half the difference gives 4 degrees of signed rotation for the first field relative to zero. The mean is 43 degrees, the combined input polarisation and analyser zero. Conversion to a Verdet constant additionally needs the applied field and path length.

In the 600 nm example, what thickness uncertainty corresponds to 1 degree of rotation uncertainty at fixed index difference?

Work through the solution

Rotation is proportional to thickness. Relative uncertainty \(1/30\) therefore gives about \(0.20/30=0.0067\) mm, or 6.7 μm. Uncertainty in wavelength or field would add additional contributions.

An ideal analyser gives a minimum 4% of its maximum. Can this be explained solely by a rotation of an ideal linearly polarised monochromatic input in a lossless circularly birefringent sample?

Work through the solution

Pure rotation preserves a linear state, so an ideal analyser should extinguish it completely. Differential loss, ellipticity, depolarisation, finite bandwidth or a detector background are possible alternatives. Use circular-analysis or full Stokes measurements and an empty-path baseline to distinguish them.

Compare with a full polarisation measurement

Use the Stokes reconstruction experiment to determine which intensities reveal a circular component. A fitted linear rotation alone cannot measure that missing component.

Relativistic particle dynamics and the field tensor

Waves, radiation and relativity

A circular track measures momentum per charge. A clock following the particle registers proper time, and a detector records how much kinetic energy it has gained. We will put these observations in one dynamical equation, solve that equation in uniform fields and exhibit a numerical pusher that can get the energy right while its orbit phase slowly slips.

What the track, clock and calorimeter measure

Consider a particle of invariant mass \(m\), signed charge \(q\) and ordinary velocity \(\mathbf v\). Inertial observers agree on the proper time between neighbouring events on its timelike worldline,

\[d\tau=dt\sqrt{1-v^2/c^2}.\]

Adopt coordinates \(x^\mu=(ct,x,y,z)\) and metric \(g=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\). Dividing the displacement by proper time produces a vector of fixed Minkowski norm. Multiplying it by invariant mass gives four-momentum.

\[u^\mu=\gamma(c,\mathbf v),\]
\[p^\mu=mu^\mu=(\mathcal E/c,\mathbf p),\]
\[\mathcal E^2-c^2|\mathbf p|^2=m^2c^4 .\]

Energy includes the rest term. Momentum is \(\gamma m\mathbf v\). Differentiating the last equation on a trajectory gives

\[d\mathcal E/dt=\mathbf v\cdot d\mathbf p/dt.\]

This identity replaces the nonrelativistic step of dotting \(m\mathbf a\) with velocity. A magnetic field can rotate momentum with fixed magnitude and energy, whereas an electric field can change energy by doing work.

Suppose an electron begins at rest and gains 10.0 keV of kinetic energy across an electrostatic potential difference in vacuum. Its rest energy is 511 keV, giving \(\gamma=1.01957\) and \(v/c=\sqrt{1-1/\gamma^2}=0.1950\). The nonrelativistic estimate is 0.1978. In a modest energy measurement the discrepancy could lie below the experimental spread. For a precise orbit calculation the 1.96 percent change in relativistic cyclotron period is measurable independently of that discrepancy in speed.

A linear map that produces the Lorentz force

Seek a local equation linear in field and four-velocity and covariant under boosts. For the energy component to give \(q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v\), and for the spatial components to give the electric force and the oriented cross product, construct the antisymmetric field tensor. With the stated metric and \(\epsilon^{123}=1\), set

\[F^{0i}=-E_i/c,\]
\[F^{i0}=E_i/c,\]
\[F^{ij}=-\epsilon^{ijk}B_k,\]
\[\frac{dp^\mu}{d\tau}=qF^{\mu\nu}u_\nu .\]

Lowering the index on \(u\) reverses the signs of its spatial components. The spatial product becomes \(q\gamma(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\), and division by \(\gamma\) converts the proper-time derivative to \(d\mathbf p/dt\). The time component similarly yields

\[d\mathcal E/dt=q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v.\]

Contract the force with \(u_\mu\). The symmetric product \(u_\mu u_\nu\) annihilates the antisymmetric tensor, which proves that the evolution preserves the mass shell.

To recover Maxwell's kinematic constraints from potentials, put \(A^\mu=(\phi/c,\mathbf A)\) and \(F^{\mu\nu}=\partial^\mu A^\nu-\partial^\nu A^\mu\). Then \(F^{0i}=(\partial_t A_i+\partial_i\phi)/c=-E_i/c\), while the spatial-spatial components are the magnetic curl. Adding a four-gradient to \(A^\mu\) leaves the tensor unchanged because ordinary derivatives commute. The particle action \(S=-mc^2\int d\tau-q\int A_\mu dx^\mu\) gives the same force after integrating variations by parts with fixed endpoints. Gauge changes add only an endpoint term to this action. A trajectory measured with local forces cannot distinguish such potential representatives.

Compare the potentials and field-based description in the field-tensor chapter

Constant force, variable coordinate acceleration

Put a particle initially at rest in a uniform static electric field along \(x\). Let \(a_0=qE/m\) be signed. Integration of \(dp_x/dt=qE\) gives \(p_x=ma_0t\), so the speed tends to \(c\) even while momentum increases linearly. Proper-time evolution is simplest in terms of rapidity \(\eta\), where \(u^0=c\cosh\eta\), \(u^x=c\sinh\eta\). The two tensor equations imply \(d\eta/d\tau=a_0/c\).

\[t=\frac{c}{a_0}\sinh(a_0\tau/c),\]
\[x=\frac{c^2}{a_0}\{\cosh(a_0\tau/c)-1\},\]
\[\frac{dv_x}{dt}=\frac{a_0}{\gamma^3}.\]

The initial time and position are zero. The work \(qEx=mc^2(\cosh\eta-1)\) equals kinetic energy, which checks both the integration constant and the dimensional factor in the displacement. For an electron use the direction of the electric force as positive to simplify signs. At 20.0 kV/m it gains 1.0 keV in 5.0 cm. The energy gain follows from the static potential even though the speed does not grow exactly as the square root of distance. The field region must last long enough for the particle to cross it and fringe fields can alter the entry trajectory.

Compare momentum, coordinate time and proper time in the charged-worldline experiment

Helical motion, radius and period

A uniform magnetic field along \(z\) keeps \(p_z\), energy and \(|\mathbf p_\perp|\) fixed. Write \(p_x+ip_y\) as a complex number. The Lorentz force reduces to a first-order rotation equation and a single time integration gives the corresponding position. With positive \(B\), positive charges starting along \(+x\) initially turn towards \(-y\).

\[\frac{d}{dt}(p_x+ip_y)=-i\frac{qB}{\gamma m}(p_x+ip_y),\]
\[R=\frac{p_\perp}{|q|B},\]
\[T=\frac{2\pi\gamma m}{|q|B}.\]

The helical pitch is \(v_zT\). Radius gives transverse momentum and a calibrated period gives total energy through \(\gamma\). A track seen only in projection along the field gives no direct evidence that longitudinal momentum vanishes. A second view or a timing measurement can resolve this ambiguity.

At 10.0 keV the electron has \(pc=\sqrt{K(K+2mc^2)}=101.6\) keV. With entry perpendicular to a 2.0 mT field its radius is 0.169 m and its period 18.2 ns. Doubling the field halves both radius and period at fixed injection energy. Doubling momentum at fixed field doubles radius but changes period by the ratio of Lorentz factors. These are different tests of a track calibration. A beam with a spread of energies will dephase relative to a narrow timing gate even when every single particle remains in a perfectly uniform field.

A drift frame gives an exact crossed-field solution

For constant crossed fields with \(E<cB\), transform to the frame moving at \(\mathbf v_d=\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/B^2\). There \(E'=0\) and \(B'=\sqrt{B^2-E^2/c^2}\); calculate the transformed initial momentum and integrate a helix. Transform two successive equal-phase events back to the lab. Their perpendicular displacement in the electric-free frame is zero. If the motion has no component parallel to \(\mathbf B\), the entire displacement there is zero, and the average lab cycle velocity is exactly \(\mathbf v_d\). The detailed lab trace need not be circular and the instantaneous lab energy may oscillate.

Take \(E=30.0\) kV/m along \(y\) and \(B=0.50\) mT along \(z\). The drift is along \(+x\) at \(6.00\times10^7\) m/s. A particle injected at that velocity crosses the uniform fields on a straight line because the forces cancel. An initially stationary positive particle instead begins by accelerating along \(+y\). These observations can use the same field with different injected particles. If \(E\) exceeds \(cB\), this electric-free frame is unavailable. Transform to a magnetic-free frame and integrate electric acceleration instead.

Continuous rotation and a discrete experiment

A naive forward step \(\mathbf p_{n+1}=\mathbf p_n+q\Delta t\,\mathbf v_n\times\mathbf B\) adds a vector perpendicular to the previous momentum. Its squared norm increases by the squared magnitude of that kick. The apparent heating will persist in perfectly static uniform magnetic fields. For a purely magnetic update, evaluate the direction using the mean of old and new momenta. At fixed \(\gamma\), the resulting implicit two-dimensional linear map can be solved algebraically and is an exact rotation by a modified angle.

\[\delta=2\arctan\!\left(\frac{\omega_c\Delta t}{2}\right),\]
\[\omega_c=\frac{qB}{\gamma m}.\]

The sign of rotation follows the earlier complex equation. Dotting the update with the mean momentum gives zero change in squared norm, which verifies the energy property even for relatively large time steps. A common electromagnetic pusher surrounds the magnetic rotation by two electric half-kicks. In spatially varying fields, specify at what time and position each field is sampled, and check spatial interpolation and time convergence. For ultrarelativistic nearly cancelling electric and magnetic fields, preserving rotation in a pure field alone does not ensure cancellation is represented accurately.

Solve the constant tensor without transforming every time step

When uniform electric and magnetic fields are constant in spacetime, the Lorentz equation is a linear constant-coefficient equation for four-velocity as a function of proper time. Nonlinearity in ordinary time hides that simple structure, because the rate at which proper time accumulates changes along the orbit. Define the mixed tensor \(M^\mu{}_\nu=(q/m)F^{\mu\alpha}g_{\alpha\nu}\). In a uniform field it does not vary along the trajectory. A formal solution is then

\[u(\tau)=e^{M\tau}u(0),\]
\[x(\tau)-x(0)=\int_0^\tau e^{Ms}u(0)\,ds .\]
