Householder reflections and practical QR

Numerical linear algebra

Build one reflection explicitly, use it to triangularise a rectangular system and locate the residual in transformed coordinates.

From first principles

Construct a reflection that zeros a column tail

A reflection reverses the component of a vector perpendicular to a chosen plane and leaves the component in the plane fixed. If a nonzero vector \(v\) is normal to the plane, the perpendicular projection of \(x\) is \(v(v^Tx)/(v^Tv)\). Subtracting twice that projection defines

\[Hx=x-2v\frac{v^Tx}{v^Tv},\]
\[H=I-2\frac{vv^T}{v^Tv}.\]

The reflection preserves lengths because the parallel and perpendicular components remain orthogonal. To reduce a column \(x\), target an axis vector \(y=\alpha e_1\) of equal length, so \(|\alpha|=\|x\|_2\). Set \(v=x-y\). Expanding the dot products gives \(2v^Tx=v^Tv\) because \(x\) and \(y\) have equal norm. Consequently

\[Hx=x-v=y .\]

Choosing \(\alpha\) opposite in sign to \(x_1\) avoids cancellation in \(v_1\). Reflecting only the unprocessed rows of each successive column yields an upper triangular factor while preserving previously generated zeros. An already zero column needs no transformation.

Derivation and applications

Recover the least-squares residual from Householder coordinates

A QR solver can report how much an orthogonal transformation sends into rows below a triangular factor. That part has a precise measurement interpretation. Assume a real matrix \(A\) with \(m>n\) and independent columns. Apply the same sequence of Householder transformations to the augmented matrix \([A\ b]\). Their product \(Q^T\) is orthogonal. Partition its result into an upper block with \(n\) rows and a lower block,

\[Q^T[A\ b]=\begin{pmatrix}R&c\\0&d\end{pmatrix}.\]

Because orthogonal transformations preserve the Euclidean norm, the squared residual of any trial vector decomposes into the sum of two nonnegative terms. Only the first can be changed by the fit,

\[\|b-Ax\|_2^2=\|c-Rx\|_2^2+\|d\|_2^2 .\]

The optimal \(x\) solves \(Rx=c\), leaving residual norm \(\|d\|_2\). In exact arithmetic the original-coordinate residual is obtained by reversing the transforms on a vector whose upper block is zero,

\[r=b-Ax=Q\binom{0}{d},\]
\[A^Tr=R^T[\,I\ \ 0\,]\binom{0}{d}=0.\]

The lower coordinates are combinations of observations. Their individual values depend on the particular reflections, whereas their squared norm is invariant. Retaining that norm directly avoids subtracting two large nearly equal squared norms when the fit is excellent. If some triangular pivots are numerically negligible, ranking the columns and checking the model’s effective rank become necessary before interpreting all \(n\) fitted coefficients.

Worked example

Inspect residuals after fitting a constant

Fit \(A=(1,1)^T\) to readings \(b=(8,10)^T\). Rotate to the orthonormal directions \((1,1)/\sqrt2\) and \((-1,1)/\sqrt2\). The transformed data are \(18/\sqrt2\) and \(2/\sqrt2\), while the transformed model is \((\sqrt2,0)^T x\). The fit gives \(x=9\), residual norm \(\sqrt2\), and original residual \((-1,1)^T\). Its sum is zero as the constant column requires.

Second application

Compare the numerical consequences of subtracting squared norms

Suppose transformed coordinates of another fit have \(\|c\|=10^8\) and \(\|d\|=0.1\). Computing \(\|b\|^2-\|c\|^2\) would subtract two values near \(10^{16}\) to recover \(0.01\). Binary64 spacing near \(10^{16}\) is 2, so the small addition can disappear entirely. Keeping the transformed tail returns residual norm \(0.1\) directly, subject to normal transformation errors.

Eliminate a column without stretching it

An orthogonal transformation applied simultaneously to the data matrix and right-hand side leaves every Euclidean residual length unchanged. A Householder reflector is \(H=I-2vv^T/(v^Tv)\) for a nonzero vector \(v\). Decompose any vector into components parallel and perpendicular to \(v\). The parallel component changes sign and the perpendicular component stays fixed, proving both length preservation and \(H^2=I\). Implementation requires just a dot product and a scaled vector subtraction rather than a dense matrix.

To make the entries below the first element of a column \(x\) vanish, choose \(v=x-\alpha e_1\) where \(|\alpha|=\|x\|_2\). A short calculation gives \(v^Tv=2(\|x\|^2-\alpha x_1)\) and \(v^Tx=\|x\|^2-\alpha x_1\), hence

\[Hx=x-v=\alpha e_1.\]

Choose \(\alpha\) with sign opposite to \(x_1\) to avoid subtracting nearly equal positive numbers when computing \(v_1\). A zero column requires a separate decision about rank.

Follow the data into the triangular system

For \(x=(3,4)^T\), use \(\alpha=-5\) and \(v=(8,4)^T\). The reflection matrix is \(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-.6&-.8\\-.8&.6\end{smallmatrix}\right)\). Take a one-column least-squares problem with measurements \(b=(4,3)^T\). Multiplication sends \(x\) to \((-5,0)^T\) and \(b\) to \((-4.8,-1.4)^T\). The optimum coefficient matches the first transformed measurement, giving \(c=0.96\). The second transformed measurement remains a residual of magnitude 1.4. Back in physical coordinates the predicted vector is \((2.88,3.84)\), with residual \((1.12,-0.84)\) and the same length.

For several columns, apply the first reflector to the entire remaining block. Construct the next reflector only on rows below the first pivot, protecting the already triangular part. Saving the reflector vectors permits application of \(Q\) or \(Q^T\) later without explicitly forming either full matrix. Additional right-hand sides can then use the same orthogonal transformations and triangular solve.

Detect when a coefficient is poorly determined

If a new column is nearly in the span of earlier columns, little of it survives below the preceding pivots. A small trailing diagonal of \(R\) signals potential coefficient sensitivity, but its scale depends on column units and order. An instrument model with one parameter measured in micro-units can naturally have a small column. Scale the columns according to the intended range of the corresponding parameters before interpreting a near-zero diagonal.

Column-pivoted QR selects a remaining column with a large unrepresented component at each stage. It can help reveal effective rank and identify an independent set of measurements or basis functions. A numerical rank decision still needs a tolerance tied to data uncertainty or floating-point arithmetic. Reporting a fitted coefficient to ten digits after discarding an almost invisible direction would conceal the limitation that the factorisation has revealed.

Check a nearly aligned column

Consider \(x=(1,10^{-8})^T\). Its norm differs from one by only about \(5\times10^{-17}\), below the spacing between one and the next larger double precision number. Computing the exact difference \(x_1-\|x\|\) as that subtraction may therefore give zero. Choosing the target with opposite sign makes the first entry of the reflector vector about two and the remaining small entry is retained. This calculation explains the sign convention geometrically: two opposite points on a sphere provide valid reflection targets, and only one directs the subtraction away from severe cancellation.

Update a triangular fit when observations arrive sequentially

A Householder QR factorisation is efficient when the entire design matrix is available. Suppose instead that a new row arrives after the original observations have been reduced to an upper triangular factor \(R\), a transformed data vector \(z\), and an accumulated residual sum of squares \(S\). The original least-squares objective equals \(\|Rx-z\|^2+S\). Appending measurement row \(a^T\) and value \(b\) adds \((a^Tx-b)^2\). Stacking \(a^T\) below \(R\) produces a matrix that is triangular except for one row. Orthogonal plane rotations can restore triangular form by eliminating each incoming entry in turn.

To remove a pair \((r,s)^T\), let \(\rho=\sqrt{r^2+s^2}\), \(c=r/\rho,\ d=s/\rho\). Multiplication by the rotation with rows \((c,d)\) and \((-d,c)\) gives \((\rho,0)^T\). Apply precisely the same two-row operation to all remaining columns and to the transformed data. Its final bottom data entry has no corresponding design column, so its square adds to \(S\). If \(r=s=0\) there is nothing to eliminate. A numerical implementation obtains the length with a scaled hypotenuse evaluation to avoid overflow from squaring a large observation.

Insert a point into an existing two-parameter fit

Suppose the existing transformed problem has \(R=I,\ z=(1,2)^T,\ S=0\). A new sensor reports \(x_1+x_2=5\). The best prior estimate was \((1,2)^T\), which predicts three and therefore disagrees with the incoming reading by two. To reconcile all observations, stack the new row \((1,1\mid5)\) below \((1,0\mid1)\) and \((0,1\mid2)\). Rotate the first and last rows with \(c=d=1/\sqrt2\). The first becomes \((\sqrt2,1/\sqrt2\mid6/\sqrt2)\); the last becomes \((0,1/\sqrt2\mid4/\sqrt2)\).

The second rotation combines leading entries \(1\) and \(1/\sqrt2\), yielding \(\rho=\sqrt{3/2}\), \(c=\sqrt{2/3}\), \(d=1/\sqrt3\). Its surviving right-hand side is

\[2\sqrt{2/3}+4/\sqrt6=8/\sqrt6.\]

Thus the second-coordinate estimate is

\[(8/\sqrt6)/\sqrt{3/2}=8/3.\]

Back substitution in the first row gives

\[x_1=(6-8/3)/2=5/3.\]

The final bottom data entry is \(-2/\sqrt3+4/\sqrt3=2/\sqrt3\), contributing \(4/3\) to the residual sum of squares.

Verify directly: the three residual magnitudes are all \(2/3\), so their squared sum is \(4/3\). The new reading moved both coordinates equally because their original transformed information was identical and its row is symmetric. No historical measurement needed to be reread. Conversely deleting an earlier reading can expose rank deficiency and is a different numerical operation; simply running the same update with a negated data value would add another squared term rather than remove information.

When a stream contains different measurement variances, divide each arriving row and its right-hand side by its independently known standard deviation before applying the rotations. The accumulated \(S\) then measures squared standardised discrepancies. If the new reading shares an error with earlier readings, dividing its row alone is insufficient. One must first account for the conditional error and correlated design contribution or refactor a suitable joint whitening transformation. This is a measurement-model limitation, even when the rotations themselves remain perfectly orthogonal.

Repeated readings of a common reference are one practical source of precisely this kind of dependence.

Arnoldi iteration and GMRES

Numerical linear algebra

Construct an orthonormal basis for a nonsymmetric operator and use a small least-squares problem to choose an approximation.

From first principles

Project a large operator onto successive Krylov directions

For \(Ax=b\), start with residual \(r_0=b-Ax_0\ne0\). Choose the first basis vector as \(v_1=r_0/\beta\), where \(\beta=\|r_0\|_2\). Repeated matrix products generate a search space, but their columns can become nearly parallel. At step \(j\), remove previously resolved components of \(Av_j\) before normalizing the remainder,

\[h_{ij}=v_i^TAv_j,\]
\[w=Av_j-\sum_{i=1}^j h_{ij}v_i,\]
\[h_{j+1,j}=\|w\|_2 .\]

If \(h_{j+1,j}>0\), take \(v_{j+1}=w/h_{j+1,j}\). The resulting Arnoldi identity \(AV_k=V_{k+1}\bar H_k\) expresses each large product using a small upper-Hessenberg matrix. A correction \(V_ky\) gives the residual norm

\[\|r_0-AV_ky\|_2=\|\beta e_1-\bar H_k y\|_2 .\]

Orthonormality justifies the equality. GMRES solves the smaller least-squares problem to minimize the actual residual over this search space. When the new norm is zero the subspace is already invariant, and no further independent direction is available through another ordinary Arnoldi step.

Background · Krylov subspace
\[\mathcal K_k(A,b)=\operatorname{span}(b,Ab,\ldots,A^{k-1}b)\]

The starting vector determines which parts of the spectrum are visible. Orthogonalisation avoids storing nearly dependent raw powers.

Derivation and applications

Calculate exactly when the first GMRES step stalls

For one Krylov step the only available correction is a multiple of the initial residual \(r=b-Ax\). The resulting change in the residual is parallel to \(Ar\). Assume a real nonsingular \(A\) and \(r\ne0\), which guarantees \(Ar\ne0\). To choose the coefficient \(\alpha\), expand the squared residual norm,

\[\|r-\alpha Ar\|_2^2=r^Tr-2\alpha(Ar)^Tr+\alpha^2\|Ar\|_2^2 .\]

The derivative vanishes at the coefficient of the orthogonal projection of \(r\) onto \(Ar\). Hence the new residual is perpendicular to the direction in which it was allowed to change,

\[\alpha_*=\frac{(Ar)^Tr}{\|Ar\|_2^2},\qquad (Ar)^T(r-\alpha_*Ar)=0 .\]

Let \(\theta\) denote the angle between \(r\) and \(Ar\). At the minimum the squared length removed is exactly the squared length of that projection. The remaining fraction of residual is

\[\frac{\|r_{\rm new}\|}{\|r\|}=\sqrt{1-\frac{[(Ar)^Tr]^2}{\|Ar\|^2\|r\|^2}}=|\sin\theta|.\]

Thus equal or opposite directions allow an exact one-step solve, while perpendicular directions deliver no change. This is purely geometric and applies without symmetry. It also clarifies why restarting after one step can be costly: later basis vectors could expose a direction inaccessible on the first line.

Worked example

Compute the residual reduction for a shear

Use \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and \(r=(0,1)^T\). Then \(Ar=(2,1)^T\), \(\alpha_*=1/5\), and \(r_{\rm new}=(-0.4,0.8)^T\). Its norm is

\[\sqrt{0.8}=0.894.\]

The dot product with \(Ar\) is \(-0.8+0.8=0\), an independent check that the selected coefficient minimizes the residual along this line.

Second application

Detect stagnation despite a nonsingular operator

Take \(A=\operatorname{diag}(2,-1)\) and \(r=(1,\sqrt2)^T\). The dot product \(r^TAr=2-2=0\) forces the first coefficient to zero. A second direction completes a two-dimensional space because \(Ar=(2,-\sqrt2)^T\) is independent of \(r\). In fact \(A^{-1}r=(0.5,-\sqrt2)^T=-0.5r+0.5Ar\). The two-direction correction solves the problem exactly although a one-step restart would repeat the same state.

A residual as a polynomial

Consider a square nonsingular matrix and an initial residual \(r_0=b-Ax_0\). A correction in the span of \(r_0,Ar_0,\ldots,A^{k-1}r_0\) is a polynomial in \(A\) applied to \(r_0\). After subtracting the action of \(A\), the new residual is \(p_k(A)r_0\) with \(p_k(0)=1\). This constraint matters because the zero polynomial would certify convergence before the calculation began. Generalised minimal residual iteration, or GMRES, minimises the Euclidean norm over all such residuals. The spaces are nested, so the exact-arithmetic residual norms cannot increase during a cycle.

Replacing large products by small coordinates

Start with \(v_1=r_0/\beta\), where \(\beta=\|r_0\|_2\). For each new direction form \(w=Av_j\), subtract its projections onto the existing orthonormal columns, and normalise what remains. Each projection coefficient occupies one row of column \(j\) of a small matrix. The final normalising length occupies its first subdiagonal. This Arnoldi construction gives

\[AV_k=V_{k+1}\bar H_k.\]

If the length vanishes, the current subspace is invariant and no further normalisation is possible.

\[\|b-A(x_0+V_ky)\|_2=\|V_{k+1}(\beta e_1-\bar H_ky)\|_2=\|\beta e_1-\bar H_ky\|_2.\]

The second equality uses orthonormality. Solve this small rectangular least-squares problem, for example by updating triangular factors using plane rotations. The resulting residual is orthogonal to the range of \(AV_k\). Repeated projection may be necessary when rounding spoils orthogonality. A freshly calculated physical residual can test the small model's estimate before reporting convergence.

One eigenvalue and two directions

Take \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&4\\0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), \(x_0=0\), \(b=e_2\). The first correction is \(\alpha e_2\), so the squared residual is \(16\alpha^2+(1-\alpha)^2\). Differentiation gives \(\alpha=1/17\) and length \(4/\sqrt{17}\), about 0.9701. Almost the entire initial residual survives despite every eigenvalue being one. The second direction comes from the component of \(Ae_2\) perpendicular to \(e_2\), namely \(4e_1\). With both directions available the exact solution \((-4,1)^T\) is accessible, and its residual vanishes.

For a diagonal matrix equal to the identity, a single step would suffice for every right-hand side. Here a nonzero off-diagonal entry couples two coordinates and the minimal polynomial is \((z-1)^2\). Counting distinct eigenvalues alone misses that coupling.

Paying for a long memory

Each new direction needs storage and projections against earlier directions. Restarting after a fixed number releases memory, but discards those directions. A new cycle still starts from the best current iterate. It lacks the previous cycle's simultaneous freedom to alter its old coefficients. Record matrix-vector products and the residual after each restart when comparing costs. On the example, two directions retained together already achieve the exact answer, providing a direct check on a proposed implementation.

Read a preconditioned residual

Left preconditioning with \(M^{-1}\) makes GMRES minimise \(\|M^{-1}(b-Ax)\|\) instead. If \(M=\operatorname{diag}(1,1000)\), a physical residual \((0,1)^T\) has preconditioned norm 0.001. A small number in the stopping display could therefore leave the second physical balance comparatively inaccurate. Right preconditioning solves \(AM^{-1}y=b\), then forms \(x=M^{-1}y\); for exact preconditioner actions its minimisation still uses the original residual. Compare work and physically meaningful residuals when assessing either choice, especially if the equations use different units.

How restarting can erase a necessary direction

GMRES minimises the full residual norm over its current correction space. A one-step restart retains only the first correction direction generated from the new residual. If that direction makes no angle with the gradient of the residual objective in the appropriate sense, progress can cease even for an invertible, perfectly conditioned matrix. To see the geometry, suppose the current residual is \(r\). The one-dimensional trial residual is \(r-\alpha Ar\). Minimising its squared norm for real arithmetic yields

\[\alpha=(Ar)^Tr/\|Ar\|^2.\]

Thus no reduction occurs when \(Ar\) is perpendicular to \(r\). Restarting immediately recreates precisely the same subproblem.

Unrestarted GMRES can still add \(Ar\) and further powers to its Krylov space. In exact arithmetic it can therefore escape one-step stagnation. This distinction matters for storage allocation: reducing the basis length can change the convergence mechanism itself. Merely multiplying the allowed number of restart cycles cannot recover directions if every cycle begins and ends with the same residual. Conversely increasing the restart length helps only if the additional space contains directions that produce a lower residual.

Compare restart lengths for a quarter-turn operator

Set \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) and \(b=(1,0)^T\), beginning at zero. Its transpose times itself is the identity, so its two-norm condition number is one. Yet \(Ab=(0,1)^T\) is perpendicular to the initial residual \(b\). The residual after trial step \(\alpha b\) is \((1,-\alpha)^T\), whose squared length is \(1+\alpha^2\). The best step is exactly zero. GMRES restarted every step thus leaves the residual norm at one forever.

With a two-vector basis use corrections \(x=\alpha b+\beta Ab=(\alpha,\beta)^T\). The residual is \((1+\beta,-\alpha)^T\), which vanishes for \(\alpha=0,\beta=-1\). The exact solution is reached. In polynomial language no degree-one real polynomial satisfying \(p(0)=1\) can vanish at both eigenvalues \(+i,-i\). The degree-two choice \(p(z)=1+z^2\) does so because \(A^2=-I\). Both formulations explain the same measurement without attributing the failure to singularity or loss of precision.

For a nonsingular real skew-symmetric matrix the one-step numerator vanishes for every real \(r\), since a scalar \(r^TAr\) equals its own negative after transposition. This family therefore offers a repeatable stress test for short-restart implementations. A preconditioner or a shift that alters the search geometry can change the result, but any claimed gain should be checked using the residual of the original equations and a comparable count of matrix-vector products.

There is also a simple residual recomputation check at convergence: for the two-step state \((0,-1)^T\), multiplying by the original matrix gives \((1,0)^T\) exactly. If a code reports the same state after one iteration, it has either used a different search direction or mislabelled its iteration count. Exposing the current basis length alongside the total matrix-vector count makes a restart experiment easier to reproduce.

For complex arithmetic, use the real or complex least-squares minimisation appropriate to the actual correction space; the perpendicularity argument above assumes real data.

How a moderately accurate mode can give a precise frequency

Numerical linear algebra

Use the Rayleigh quotient to estimate an eigenvalue, understand its stationary error and check a proposed mode with a residual.

A frequency estimate from a trial shape

For \(Kq=\lambda Mq\) with symmetric \(K\) and positive definite mass \(M\), a trial vector \(v\) fixes a direction but leaves an amplitude free. Substituting \(q(t)=a(t)v\) in kinetic and elastic energy gives effective mass \(v^TMv\) and stiffness \(v^TKv\). Their ratio \(R(v)\) estimates the squared frequency. Rescaling \(v\) has no effect. For \(M=I\), differentiating \(v^TKv/(v^Tv)\) gives a gradient proportional to \(Kv-Rv\). Its zeros are precisely the eigenvectors.

Angular error and the residual

Take exact orthonormal modes \(u_1,u_2\) and a trial \(v=u_1\cos\theta+u_2\sin\theta\). Orthogonality removes all cross terms. Then

\[R(v)-\lambda_1=(\lambda_2-\lambda_1)\sin^2\theta,\]
\[\|Kv-R(v)v\|_2=|\lambda_2-\lambda_1|\,|\sin\theta\cos\theta|.\]

The eigenvalue error is second order in a small angular error. The residual is first order. For general dimension write \(v=\sum c_i u_i\) with unit norm. The squared residual is \(\sum |c_i|^2(\lambda_i-R)^2\), so at least one eigenvalue lies within one residual norm of \(R\). Identifying which mode requires more information about the spectrum.

Two measurements

For squared frequencies 4 and 9 in s⁻² and a 10-degree error in the first trial mode, \(R=4+5(0.03015)=4.1508\) s⁻². The estimated angular frequency is 2.0373 rad/s. The relative error in frequency is about half the relative error in its square at small errors.

For eigenvalues 4 and 4.01, a 45-degree mixture gives a residual of just 0.005. This very small number is consistent with a large uncertainty in shape. If the two eigenvalues were identical, every combination in the eigenspace would be an exact mode.

Tilt the trial vector

The plot shows the quotient error and residual across trial angles for a chosen spectral gap. Move the angle marker, halve a small angle and compare the two responses.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on frequency estimates

For eigenvalues 2 and 10, a trial produces \(R=2.08\). Assuming the two-mode model, estimate its angular error.

Worked solution

The mixture fraction is

\[(2.08-2)/(10-2)=0.01.\]

Hence \(|\sin\theta|=0.1\), or about 5.74 degrees near the lower mode.

A trial gives \(R=5\) and residual zero. Does it prove that 5 is the lowest eigenvalue?

Worked solution

It proves that the trial vector is an exact eigenvector for 5. To certify the lowest eigenvalue one also needs a lower bound or a description of the remaining spectral subspace.

Compare how quickly an eigenvector direction converges with the rate for its Rayleigh-quotient eigenvalue estimate. Driscoll and Braun · Power iteration

Estimating a cantilever frequency from energy

Machine dynamics

A clamped beam carries a small sensor at its tip. Its precise mode shape is unknown. Use the static deflection shape to approximate the fundamental frequency and see why concentrating a small mass at the tip has a large effect.

Choose a displacement field that respects the support

Write \(w(x,t)=q(t)\phi(s)\) with \(s=x/L\), taking \(q\) as tip displacement. Choose the shape from a uniform cantilever under a static transverse tip force. The bending moment varies linearly from root to tip. Integrating curvature twice with \(w(0)=w'(0)=0\), then normalizing at \(s=1\), gives

\[\phi=s^2(3-s)/2.\]

The shape satisfies both essential clamp conditions. Its free-tip shear need not match the exact unloaded vibration mode. The energy formulation can use such admissible trial shapes.

Background · Rayleigh quotient
\[R(u)=u^\mathsf TK u/(u^\mathsf TM u)\]

For symmetric stiffness and positive mass this estimates squared angular frequency. A kinematically admissible displacement is required for a useful structural estimate.

Compute effective stiffness and mass from the same field

The strain energy density of a slender beam in bending is \(\tfrac12 EI w_{xx}^2\). Its translational kinetic energy is \(\tfrac12\rho A w_t^2\) per unit length. Changing variables from x to s introduces different powers of L into the two integrals. Include a point mass M at the tip, neglecting its rotary inertia.

\[U={EI q^2\over 2L^3}\int_0^1(\phi'')^2\,ds,\]
\[K={\dot q^2\over2}\left[\rho AL\int_0^1\phi^2\,ds+M\right].\]

Since \(\phi''=3(1-s)\), the stiffness integral is 3. Squaring \(s^2(3-s)/2\) and integrating \( (9s^4-6s^5+s^6)/4\) gives \(33/140\). The Euler–Lagrange equation for q is consequently a single oscillator equation with

\[\omega_R^2={3EI/L^3\over(33/140)\rho AL+M}.\]

For M=0 this gives the dimensionless coefficient \(\sqrt{140/11}=3.5675\). Solving the exact unloaded cantilever condition \(\cosh\beta\cos\beta=-1\) gives

\[\beta_1^2=3.5160.\]

The estimate is high by about 1.5%. The Rayleigh minimum principle explains that direction of error for conservative systems with admissible trial shapes. Restricting motion to a single shape restricts the set over which the energy quotient can attain its minimum.

Choose a sensor after estimating its dynamic effect

Take \(EI=10\) N m², \(L=0.50\) m and total beam mass 0.40 kg. The tip stiffness is 240 N/m and the distributed effective mass is only 0.0943 kg. The estimate gives 8.03 Hz without a sensor and 6.49 Hz with a 0.05 kg tip sensor. The sensor weighs one eighth of the beam, yet it has over half the effective mass associated with the chosen tip coordinate.

Place the same sensor at half-span instead. There \(\phi(0.5)=0.3125\). Its kinetic energy contributes \(M\phi(0.5)^2=0.00488\) kg to the effective mass, so the estimate rises to 7.83 Hz. The sensor measures a smaller local motion as it perturbs the frequency less. Compare that trade-off at different attachment positions with the plot.

Move along the position axis with a fixed sensor mass. Identify the interval in which frequency is most sensitive to changing attachment position. Double the sensor mass and check whether the change in frequency doubles at the tip.

Open this experiment online

A related OneSimulation calculation

The course frame model has several modes and an independently variable payload rotary inertia. Compare a pure translation-mass change with a rotary-inertia change; the scalar point-mass estimate above addresses the first mechanism.

Open the OneSimulation frame eigenvalue experiment

Tutorial · estimate an unknown attached mass

The same bare beam is fitted with a small tip part and resonates at 6.0 Hz. Estimate the attached mass. Give one reason the inferred mass could differ from a scale reading even when the frequency measurement is accurate.

Show the mass estimate

The required effective mass is

\[240/(2\pi\cdot6)^2=0.1689\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

Subtract 0.0943 kg for the beam to obtain about 0.0746 kg. Clamp compliance would reduce the effective stiffness and frequency, which this fit would interpret as extra tip mass. The trial shape and omission of rotary inertia also limit the estimate for a part with appreciable extent.

Further reading · Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids · Rayleigh–Ritz methods

Recover a trajectory from a family of actions

Classical mechanics

In Hamilton–Jacobi theory a partial differential equation for one function replaces several trajectory equations. A falling bead makes the different roles of position, energy and arrival time explicit.

Use the end of the path as a variable

Let a bead move between a fixed initial event and an adjustable final event. Denote by \(S(q,t)\) the action evaluated on the actual trajectory ending at \((q,t)\). For a small change in the endpoint, the interior variation cancels by the Euler–Lagrange equation. If time stays fixed the boundary term is \(p\,\delta q\). If time changes, part of the endpoint displacement comes from continuing along the existing path by \(\dot q\,\delta t\). The remaining independent displacement is \(\delta q-\dot q\,\delta t\). Thus

\[\delta S=p(\delta q-\dot q\,\delta t)+L\,\delta t=p\,\delta q-H\,\delta t .\]

It follows that \(p=\partial_q S\) and \(-H=\partial_t S\). Eliminate \(p\) to obtain

\[\partial_t S+H(q,\partial_q S,t)=0 .\]

The equation involves partial derivatives because it describes an entire family of possible endpoints. By contrast, \(dS/dt=L\) along a single trajectory. A useful dimensional check is that a derivative of action with respect to length is momentum, whereas a derivative with respect to time is energy.

Separate time and build a clock from energy

For a time-independent Hamiltonian try \(S=W(q;E)-Et\). With \(H=p^2/(2m)+U(q)\), this gives

\[(\partial_qW)^2=2m(E-U).\]

A trajectory on the increasing-position branch has positive momentum, so an integral from a fixed reference position \(q_0\) supplies \(W\). Differentiate at fixed final position with respect to the family parameter \(E\):

\[W=\int_{q_0}^{q}\!\sqrt{2m(E-U(s))}\,ds,\]
\[\partial_EW=\int_{q_0}^{q}\!\frac{m\,ds}{\sqrt{2m(E-U(s))}} .\]

The integrand of the second integral is \(1/v\). Hence \(\partial_E W\) is the travel time from the reference point. Setting \(\partial_E S=0\) chooses a departure time of zero with this lower integration limit. A different constant amounts to shifting the clock. At a turning point \(p=0\), the one-way branch must be changed to continue the motion.

Work two arrival-time problems

Let \(q=x\) point downwards, \(U=-mgx\), and release the bead through \(x=0\) with speed \(u\). Its energy is

\[E=mu^2/2.\]

The integral yields

\[t=(\sqrt{u^2+2gx}-u)/g.\]

Squaring the relation after isolating the square root gives \(x=ut+gt^2/2\), the same trajectory as Newton's equation. For \(u=2.0\) m/s, \(g=9.81\) m/s² and \(x=3.0\) m the arrival time is about \(0.604\) s. The endpoint momentum uses the final speed, about \(7.93\) m/s, rather than the average over the fall.

Reverse the experiment by pointing \(x\) upward in the same field. If \(u=4.0\) m/s, the maximum height is \(0.815\) m. A gate \(0.50\) m above launch is crossed twice. From \(x=ut-gt^2/2\) the two times are \(0.154\) s and \(0.661\) s. The need for two branches of \(W\) is experimentally visible in those two timestamps. A calculation that takes the positive square root everywhere loses the return journey.

Compare two launch speeds

The plot shows upward trajectories and their common observation height. Lower the launch speed towards the value that barely reaches the gate. Watch what happens to the separation between the two arrival times.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on the double root

A gate records two crossings 0.40 s apart in a uniform field of 9.81 m/s². How high does the trajectory rise above that gate?

Work through the solution

The two crossings are symmetric about the maximum. The fall from the maximum takes 0.20 s, giving \(g(0.20)^2/2=0.1962\) m. Equivalently the speed at the gate is \(g(0.40)/2=1.962\) m/s and its kinetic energy per unit mass gives the same height.

Estimate the arrival-time sensitivity of a gate on the rising branch. At \(u=4.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\) compare \(h=0.50\) m with \(h=0.80\) m.

Work through the solution

Implicit differentiation of \(h=ut-gt^2/2\) at fixed launch speed gives

\[dt/dh=1/(u-gt).\]

On ascent this is \(1/\sqrt{u^2-2gh}\). Its value grows from \(0.402\,\mathrm{s/m}\) at 0.50 m to \(1.81\,\mathrm{s/m}\) at 0.80 m. Close to the top a small height error produces much larger timing uncertainty because the trajectory crosses slowly.

Does adding a constant \(C\) to a potential alter the predicted arrival times from Hamilton–Jacobi theory when the initial position and speed are unchanged?

Work through the solution

Both the total energy label and potential shift by \(C\). Hence \(p=\sqrt{2m(E-U)}\) and the abbreviated action as a path integral of \(p\) are unchanged for that orbit. The full action acquires \(-Ct\). Its spatial derivative and the time crossing predictions remain the same. Comparing the same numerical energy before and after the shift would specify different trajectories.

Further reading · Fließbach, Hamiltonformalismus · Tong, Hamilton–Jacobi theory.

Read a period from the area enclosed by an orbit

Classical mechanics

A track with a V-shaped potential has straight force segments but a strongly amplitude-dependent period. Its area in phase space gives a useful way to predict both the period and a slow change of the track.

Construct the closed area

For one bound degree of freedom at energy \(E\), position runs between two turning points. In a mechanical Hamiltonian \(p^2/(2m)+U(q)\), the upper branch of the phase portrait has positive momentum and the lower branch has its negative. The directed integral around one full orbit counts the enclosed area. Define the action \(J\) using the area divided by \(2\pi\). This choice makes its conjugate phase an angle measured in radians:

\[J(E)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint p\,dq=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{q_-}^{q_+}\sqrt{2m(E-U(q))}\,dq .\]

Now change the energy slightly. The integrand vanishes at each regular endpoint, so the moving-endpoint contributions to the derivative vanish. In the interior \(\partial p/\partial E=m/p=1/v\). The derivative of the upper-branch area therefore measures the time to reach the opposite turning point.

\[\frac{dJ}{dE}=\frac{T(E)}{2\pi},\]
\[\omega(E)=\frac{dE}{dJ}.\]

This argument also explains why the construction becomes delicate near a separatrix. A trajectory can spend an arbitrarily long time near an unstable equilibrium, and \(T\) can diverge even though the enclosed area stays finite.

Background · Action variable
\[J=(2\pi)^{-1}\oint p\,dq\]

This area measure is defined for a closed cycle. It can change strongly near a separatrix even under a slow parameter variation.

Carry out the integral for a constant restoring force

Use \(U(q)=F|q|\) with \(F>0\). The turning amplitude is \(A=E/F\). Integrate the positive branch from \(0\) to \(A\) and use reflection symmetry. Substitution \(s=E-Fq\) gives

\[J=\frac{4\sqrt{2m}}{3\pi F}E^{3/2},\]
\[T=\frac{4\sqrt{2mE}}{F}=4\sqrt{\frac{2mA}{F}} .\]

The answer has an independent mechanical check. From a turning point the bead accelerates uniformly with magnitude \(F/m\) until the centre. Covering a distance \(A\) from rest takes \(\sqrt{2mA/F}\), a quarter period. Doubling the amplitude increases the full period by \(\sqrt{2}\). The abrupt change of force direction at the centre leaves the velocity continuous.

Slowly steepen the track

A slowly adjusted parameter can preserve the orbit action to leading order when the motion remains periodic and many cycles fit inside the adjustment time. For this trap \(m\) stays constant, so constant \(J\) requires \(E^{3/2}/F\) to stay fixed. Therefore \(E\propto F^{2/3}\),

\[A=E/F\propto F^{-1/3},\]
\[T\propto F^{-2/3}.\]

Work done by adjusting the slope changes the energy despite the approximate conservation of action.

Start with \(m=0.25\) kg, \(F=2.0\) N and \(A=0.080\) m. The initial energy is \(0.160\) J and the period \(0.566\) s. A slow eightfold increase of \(F\) makes the energy four times larger, \(0.640\) J, and the amplitude half as large, \(0.040\) m. The period becomes \(0.141\) s. In contrast, switching slopes instantaneously at the centre leaves the kinetic energy and position unchanged at that instant. The same final slope would then give amplitude \(0.010\) m. The route by which a parameter changes matters.

Compare closed orbits at constant action

The axes are position and momentum. Change the slope ratio. Predict how the turning positions and the central momentum move in opposite directions. Their changes balance in the enclosed area.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on inferring a change

After a slow adjustment the period is 64% of its original value. Find the force and amplitude ratios.

Work through the solution

From \(T_f/T_i=(F_f/F_i)^{-2/3}\), the force ratio is

\[0.64^{-3/2}=1.953125.\]

The amplitude ratio is its inverse cube root, \(0.800\). Squaring the period formula with these values provides an independent check.

Derive a relation between the area action and the product of energy and period in the V trap. What fraction of the total energy is kinetic on a time average over one orbit?

Work through the solution

The explicit formulas give \(J=ET/(3\pi)\), a quantity with units of action. Along the orbit \(p\,dq=p\dot q\,dt=2K\,dt\). Hence \(2\pi J=2T\langle K\rangle\) and \(\langle K\rangle=E/3\), leaving \(2E/3\) in potential energy. This is an independent check using a measurable time average.

A trap is weakened gradually until the amplitude doubles. Find the predicted changes in energy and period, and state when the slow-ramp argument is most vulnerable.

Work through the solution

The amplitude varies as \(F^{-1/3}\), so doubling it requires reducing \(F\) to one eighth. The energy then falls to one quarter and the period increases fourfold. A ramp duration adequate initially can become too short compared to the final period. Checking the endpoint period alone is insufficient if the control has sharp changes during the ramp.

Further reading · Tong, action–angle coordinates and adiabatic invariants.

Why two finite-element wave solvers propagate the same mode at different speeds

Numerical partial differential equations

Build the element mass matrix by integrating shape functions and track the effect of diagonal lumping on the wave spectrum.

From a continuum wave to nodal masses

For \(u_{tt}=c^2u_{xx}\), multiply by a test function and integrate by parts. On a periodic line the boundary contributions cancel. A piecewise-linear element of width \(h\) uses shape functions \(1-\xi,\xi\), with \(\xi\in[0,1]\). Integrating products of shape functions gives

\[M_e=(h/6)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

Integrating derivative products gives

\[K_e=(c^2/h)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

Row-sum lumping replaces each mass row by its sum on the corresponding diagonal, \(h/2\). This preserves the mass associated with a uniform velocity but changes the response to a velocity that varies within an element.

Background · Numerical dispersion
\[c_{\rm ph,h}=\omega_h(k)/k\]

A discretised wave can retain its amplitude and still propagate at the wrong speed. Compare phase and group errors over the actual resolved wavelengths.

Diagonalise the assembled periodic problem

A periodic nodal mode has \(u_j=e^{ij\theta}\). Each neighbour contributes \(e^{\pm i\theta}\) relative to node \(j\). Thus \(K\) multiplies by \(2c^2(1-\cos\theta)/h\), consistent \(M\) by \(h(2+\cos\theta)/3\), and lumped \(M\) by \(h\). Dividing stiffness by mass gives the discrete squared frequency. Relative to the exact frequency \(c\theta/h\),

\[\frac{\omega_L}{c\theta/h}=\frac{2\sin(\theta/2)}{\theta},\]
\[\frac{\omega_C}{c\theta/h}=\frac{2\sin(\theta/2)}{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2+\cos\theta}}.\]

Both tend to one for long waves; the leading quadratic errors have opposite signs. This is a spatial effect, present even with exact time integration.

Two wavelengths in one mesh

At four cells per wavelength \(\theta=\pi/2\). The lumped ratio is \(2\sqrt2/\pi\approx0.9003\); the consistent ratio is about 1.1027. One wave solver lags by roughly ten per cent and the other leads by roughly ten per cent. At eight cells per wavelength the ratios move much closer to one, but repeated propagation over many wavelengths can still accumulate visible phase error.

At the shortest grid mode \(\theta=\pi\), the consistent frequency is \(\sqrt{12}c/h\) and the lumped frequency \(2c/h\). A central-difference time integrator stable for \(h_t\omega\le2\) therefore requires a smaller step for the consistent mass. Accuracy at one wavelength and stability at the grid scale are different design criteria.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on interpreting a convergence test

Which mass gives the exact kinetic energy for a linear velocity varying from \(v\) to \(-v\) on one element?

Worked solution

The continuum energy with unit density is \(hv^2/6\). The consistent matrix reproduces it by exact integration. The lumped kinetic energy is \(hv^2/2\), because it assigns both end masses the full endpoint speed.

How would you isolate spatial dispersion from time stepping in a measurement of a cavity eigenfrequency?

Worked solution

Solve the generalised eigenproblem \(Kq=\omega^2Mq\) directly for increasingly fine meshes or converge time steps on each fixed mesh. Then compare with the continuum frequency and report both \(h\) and the time tolerance.

Noether's theorem, boundary terms and measurable constants

Analytical mechanics from constraints to invariants

A constant of motion can survive when neither laboratory energy nor angular momentum is separately conserved. Construct the conserved quantity from an infinitesimal transformation of the action. Then use boosts, a screw symmetry, and a rotating potential to determine what measured combinations should stay fixed.

Vary a path and its time labels

Let \(q_i(t)\) be a path for \(L(q,\dot q,t)\). Consider a continuous family of transformations \(t'=t+\varepsilon\tau(q,t)\), \(q'_i(t')=q_i(t)+\varepsilon\xi_i(q,t)\), to first order in a constant parameter. The transformation at the corresponding new time has displacement \(\xi_i\); at the original time the path perturbation is \(\eta_i=\xi_i-\dot q_i\tau\). Distinguishing these variations prevents a time translation from being mistaken for a vertical displacement in a configuration-versus-time plot. Differentiating the transformed coordinates gives

\[\delta\dot q_i/\varepsilon=\dot\xi_i-\dot q_i\dot\tau,\]

with dots on the generator functions meaning derivatives along the path.

The transformed integration element is \(dt'=(1+\varepsilon\dot\tau)dt\). A variational symmetry need only preserve the action up to an endpoint term. If its first-order change in the Lagrangian times the time element is \(\varepsilon\,dF/dt\,dt\), the equations of motion with fixed endpoints are unchanged. Substitute the variations to write

\[\sum_i\left(L_{q_i}\xi_i+p_i\dot\xi_i-p_i\dot q_i\dot\tau\right)+L_t\tau+L\dot\tau=\frac{dF}{dt},\]
\[p_i=L_{\dot q_i}.\]

On a physical path \(L_{q_i}=\dot p_i\). Combine the first two terms into \(d(\sum p_i\xi_i)/dt\). Define \(H=\sum p_i\dot q_i-L\); its derivative on a solution is

\[\dot H=-L_t.\]

The terms with \(\tau\) then combine into \(-d(H\tau)/dt\). Thus \(Q=\sum_i p_i\xi_i-H\tau-F\) is conserved. Reversing the sign of the generator reverses the sign of the charge, which has no bearing on conservation. Scaling the group parameter also rescales the charge, so specifying the transformation matters when reporting its units.

Recover the familiar examples and test broken symmetry

For a common infinitesimal translation along fixed unit vector \(\hat n\), every particle has \(\xi_a=\hat n\), \(\tau=0\). A potential depending solely on pair separations is invariant, so \(F=0\). The conserved charge is total momentum along \(\hat n\). For an infinitesimal rotation about the origin,

\[\xi_a=\hat n\times\mathbf r_a;\]

the scalar triple product gives \(\hat n\cdot\sum_a\mathbf r_a\times\mathbf p_a\), the chosen angular momentum component. For time translation \(\tau=1,\xi=0\), an explicitly time-independent Lagrangian gives \(Q=-H\). In each case the symmetry refers to the system including the prescribed external fields. Internal pair forces may conserve momentum while a fixed trap exerting an external force breaks the translation symmetry of the particles.

The algebra also gives an exact diagnostic for an approximate symmetry. If the variation of \(Ldt\) differs from the boundary term by \(\varepsilon{\cal D}\,dt\), the same steps give

\[\dot Q={\cal D}.\]

In a weak potential \(V(x)=V_0+\epsilon U(x)\), translation of \(x\) gives

\[\dot p_x=-\epsilon U'(x).\]

A drifting computed momentum is therefore not automatically a failure of the time integrator. Compare its change with the integrated physical symmetry-breaking force. Reversing the small external bias helps separate the first-order physical drift from a numerical offset that does not reverse.

Derive a boost charge that needs an endpoint term

Consider a free Newtonian particle in one dimension and a small Galilean boost \(x'=x+\varepsilon t\). Here \(\xi=t\), \(\tau=0\), and \(L=m\dot x^2/2\) changes to first order by \(m\dot x\varepsilon=d(mx\varepsilon)/dt\). The Lagrangian is not pointwise invariant, but its change is a total derivative. The charge is \(Q=pt-mx\); its constancy is equivalent to \(x-(p/m)t\) remaining equal to the initial position. Dropping the endpoint term would give \(pt\), whose derivative is generally the nonzero momentum itself.

For mutually interacting particles with translationally invariant internal forces, summing the argument gives

\[Q=P t-MR_{\rm cm}.\]

Constancy of total momentum and boost charge together imply that the centre of mass moves uniformly along a straight line. Take two pucks of masses \(0.20\) and \(0.30\,{\rm kg}\) with \(x_1(0)=0\), \(x_2(0)=1.0\,{\rm m}\), and velocities \(2.0\) and \(-0.5\,{\rm m/s}\). Their total momentum is \(0.25\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\) and centre of mass starts at \(0.60\,{\rm m}\). At \(t=0.40\,{\rm s}\), including any intervening isolated elastic collision, it must be at \(0.80\,{\rm m}\). The boost charge \(0.25t-0.50R_{\rm cm}\) remains \(-0.30\,{\rm kg\,m}\). Internal impact changes the individual momenta but preserves both total quantities.

Rotate and translate together in a helical landscape

Let a particle move in cylindrical coordinates in a potential \(V(r,z-c\varphi)\), with the combination physically interpreted on a helical branch or within a periodic potential compatible with \(2\pi\) rotation. Rotation through a small angle \(\varepsilon\) combined with translation \(z\to z+c\varepsilon\) leaves the potential unchanged. The kinetic energy \(m(\dot r^2+r^2\dot\varphi^2+\dot z^2)/2\) also stays unchanged. The generator has \(\xi_\varphi=1,\xi_z=c\), hence \(Q=p_\varphi+c p_z\) is conserved. The constant \(c\) has units of length per radian, so both terms have angular-momentum units.

Differentiate directly as a check. Writing \(u=z-c\varphi\), Hamilton's equations give

\[\dot p_\varphi=+c\,V_u,\]
\[\dot p_z=-V_u.\]

Each can change, yet the combination has zero derivative. A guided particle receiving a burst of axial momentum can exchange a specific amount of angular momentum with the helical potential. For \(c=0.030\,{\rm m}\) and \(\Delta p_z=0.080\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\), the relation predicts \(\Delta p_\varphi=-0.0024\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\). A circularly symmetric potential would conserve angular momentum on its own and represents a different field configuration.

Exchange energy and angular momentum in a rotating pattern

A potential \(V(r,\varphi-\Omega t)\) with constant pattern speed is unchanged when time increases by \(\varepsilon\) and the particle coordinates rotate by \(\Omega\varepsilon\). Take \(\tau=1,\xi_\varphi=\Omega\). The conserved combination is \(H-\Omega p_\varphi\), up to the sign chosen for \(Q\). A torque from the rotating apparatus can change ordinary energy and ordinary angular momentum. Their changes obey \(dH=\Omega\,dp_\varphi\) along the trajectory when the Hamiltonian contains only the prescribed rotating potential as explicit time dependence. The external power equals angular speed times the torque acting through that pattern.

Suppose \(\Omega=12\,{\rm rad/s}\) and a particle gains \(0.015\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\) in axial angular momentum during a passage past the rotating potential. Its mechanical energy must gain \(0.180\,{\rm J}\). A numerical run reporting \(0.195\,{\rm J}\) calls for accounting for other time dependence, sampling uncertainty or integration error. If \(\Omega\) itself slowly varies, introduce a pattern angle \(\alpha(t)\), \(\Omega=\dot\alpha\). One still finds \(\dot H=\Omega\dot p_\varphi\), but \(d(H-\Omega p_\varphi)/dt=-\dot\Omega p_\varphi\). Testing an exact rotating-frame invariant with a ramped motor would therefore diagnose the wrong experiment.

Find the conserved momentum in a magnetic field directly from the action

A charge \(q\) of mass \(m\) moves in a uniform static field \(B\hat z\). Choose vector potential \(A_y=Bx\), scalar potential zero. Its Lagrangian in the perpendicular plane is \(L=m(\dot x^2+\dot y^2)/2+qBx\dot y\). The position \(y\) is cyclic, so \(p_y=m\dot y+qBx\) is constant. The physical force is also invariant under translations in \(x\), although \(x\) appears in this chosen Lagrangian. Under \(x\to x+\varepsilon\), its change is \(qB\dot y\,\varepsilon=d(qBy\varepsilon)/dt\). The corresponding Noether charge is \(p_x-qBy=m\dot x-qBy\). Thus both spatial translation symmetries survive once the endpoint term is included.

Write \(\omega_c=qB/m\), which retains the sign of the charge. Rearranging the two constants gives the coordinates of the guiding centre \(X_c=x+\dot y/\omega_c,\ Y_c=y-\dot x/\omega_c\). Differentiate using \(m\ddot x=qB\dot y,\ m\ddot y=-qB\dot x\) to see both are independent of time. A uniform field changes velocity direction but does no work. In a force experiment the centre of the measured circular arc, its radius and its angular orientation can be estimated without measuring any vector potential. The endpoint terms explain why two gauge choices can assign different canonical momenta while predicting the same observed guiding centre.

There is a useful algebraic surprise. Denote the translation charges by \(K_x=p_x-qBy\) and \(K_y=p_y\) in the chosen gauge. Their canonical bracket is \(\{K_x,K_y\}=-qB\). Each is conserved, yet the bracket does not vanish. The result is a constant, so Jacobi's identity is satisfied. The mechanical effect of their infinitesimal flows on positions commutes; a central constant in the charge bracket has zero Hamiltonian vector field. Conservation, commutation of the generated coordinate motions, and zero bracket of the generating functions are related but distinct statements. This example supplies a direct check when applying symmetry counting in phase space.

For an illustrative particle with \(q/m=2.0\,{\rm C/kg}\), \(B=0.50\,{\rm T}\), initial \((x,y)=(0.20,0.10)\,{\rm m}\) and velocity \((0.30,-0.40)\,{\rm m/s}\), the signed cyclotron frequency is \(1.0\,{\rm s^{-1}}\). The guiding centre is \((-0.20,-0.20)\,{\rm m}\), and the radius from that centre is \(0.50\,{\rm m}\). The speed is \(0.50\,{\rm m/s}\), consistent with radius \(v/|\omega_c|\). Replacing the field by its negative at the same initial state moves the guiding centre to the opposite side of the initial velocity and reverses the sense of rotation. A constant numerical offset in the vector potential should leave both reconstructed centres unchanged.

Use scaling to decide when a formal transformation creates a charge

Many equations of motion have scaling relations. A map taking one solution into another need not preserve the action up to an endpoint term, and therefore need not supply a Noether charge. To test an example, let \(L=T-V\) in three dimensions with \(V\) homogeneous of degree \(n\), so \(V(\lambda\mathbf r)=\lambda^nV(\mathbf r)\). Take \(\mathbf r\to e^\varepsilon\mathbf r\), \(t\to e^{a\varepsilon}t\). Kinetic energy times \(dt\) scales as \(e^{(2-a)\varepsilon}\), while potential energy times \(dt\) scales as \(e^{(n+a)\varepsilon}\). Equating the exponents gives \(a=(2-n)/2\), a map that rescales the entire action by a common factor and preserves stationary paths. Exact invariance of the action additionally requires the common exponent to vanish, giving \(a=2,n=-2\).

For a potential proportional to \(1/r^2\), the continuous symmetry has \(\xi=\mathbf r,\tau=2t\), \(F=0\), and charge \(D=\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf p-2tH\). Direct differentiation gives \(d(\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf p)/dt=2T-\mathbf r\cdot\nabla V=2T+2V=2H\), verifying conservation. At fixed positive energy this particular scalar product grows linearly in time. This does not require every coordinate to grow linearly; it follows from the scaling structure of the combined kinetic and potential energies. Collision singularities may limit the interval on which a classical solution exists.

For a Newtonian potential \(n=-1\), the path scaling gives \(a=3/2\), precisely the scaling of orbital periods with orbital size. The common action exponent is \(1/2\), so this scaling does not produce the preceding conserved \(D\). Instead the virial identity \(d(\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf p)/dt=2T+V\) implies \(2\langle T\rangle+\langle V\rangle=0\) on a bounded periodic trajectory after time averaging. This distinction can be checked numerically on one elliptical orbit: the virial combination generally oscillates during a cycle, whereas its integrated full-cycle average vanishes. Mistaking a scaling of solutions for exact invariance of the action would turn that averaged result into a false instantaneous conservation law.

Derive a centre-of-energy statement from a boost charge

For a free relativistic particle in one spatial dimension use \(L=-mc^2\sqrt{1-\dot x^2/c^2}\). An infinitesimal Lorentz boost, with a parameter carrying velocity units and the sign convention \(x'=x+\varepsilon t\), has generators \(\xi=t,\tau=x/c^2\). Proper time is invariant, so \(Ldt\) is invariant and \(F=0\). The Noether charge is \(K=pt-Hx/c^2\), where \(H=\gamma mc^2\), \(p=\gamma m\dot x\). Its derivative vanishes because \(p=H\dot x/c^2\). At low speed \(H/c^2\) approaches \(m\), and the Newtonian boost charge reappears up to relativistic corrections.

For an isolated set of freely propagating particles away from local collisions, sum the charges to obtain

\[K=P t-(E_{\rm tot}/c^2)X_E,\]

where \(X_E=\sum E_i x_i/E_{\rm tot}\) is the centre of energy. If collisions conserve total energy and momentum and occur at the same spacetime point for their participants, the summed charge passes continuously across them. Its constancy implies

\[dX_E/dt=c^2P/E_{\rm tot}.\]

In an interacting field problem energy stored between particles also belongs in the weighted centre; assigning every bit of energy to particle positions can violate the balance. A field-carrying interaction illustrates why the Newtonian centre of mass alone is insufficient in a relativistic energy accounting.

Imagine equal positive rest masses at \(x=0\) and \(x=2.0\,{\rm m}\). At the observation instant their energies are \(2mc^2\) and \(mc^2\), with the more energetic particle moving to the right and the second at rest. Their ordinary mass centre is \(1.0\,{\rm m}\), while their energy centre is \(2/3\,{\rm m}\). The total momentum is \(\sqrt3\,mc\), the total energy \(3mc^2\), so the centre of energy moves at \(c/\sqrt3\). The numerical difference is not a correction to the measured coordinates but a change in the physical quantity whose motion follows a boost symmetry. A centre-of-momentum frame for the pair moves at the same speed.

Diagnose symmetry from noisy finite-time balances

In an experiment one rarely has symbolic access to every component of the Lagrangian. A practical symmetry test measures the candidate charge at two times and independently integrates the physical source expected to change it. For the rotating potential, use \(R=\Delta H-\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\Omega(t)\,dL_z\). If \(\Omega\) is measured on the same clock as the trajectory, the integral can be estimated by summing the mean \(\Omega\) across each angular-momentum increment. This avoids taking a second time derivative of position merely to reconstruct the torque. An equivalent form obtained by parts is \(\Delta(H-\Omega L_z)+\int L_z\,d\Omega\). Both versions should agree if endpoint times and signs are consistent.

Take a short programmed ramp in which \(\Omega\) increases linearly from \(10\) to \(12\,{\rm s^{-1}}\) and \(L_z\) from \(0.030\) to \(0.050\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\) over the same time. If the rotating pattern is the only external work source, the energy change is mean speed times angular-momentum change,

\[11(0.020)=0.220\,{\rm J}.\]

The product \(\Omega L_z\) changes by \(0.600-0.300=0.300\,{\rm J}\), so \(H-\Omega L_z\) falls by \(0.080\,{\rm J}\). The missing amount equals

\[-\int L_z\,d\Omega=-0.040(2)=-0.080\,{\rm J}.\]

These two balances provide a nontrivial sign test when the drive speed is varied deliberately.

When measurements are noisy, an observed discrepancy must be compared with the uncertainties of both charge and physical source. An erroneous angular zero in a central problem can affect the torque reconstruction while leaving a scalar speed unchanged; a simple time shift between the motor encoder and position camera can mimic an extra source of work. Change the drive direction, repeat with constant speed, and estimate the effect of clock offsets using resampled records. These experiments test the specific symmetry under investigation. A plot that appears almost flat on an arbitrary vertical scale supplies neither a physical tolerance nor a discrimination among competing error sources.

Derive a conservation law for an elastic field

Noether's argument also applies when the coordinates form a continuous field \(u(x,t)\). Consider a uniform string or rod with density per length \(\rho_l\), displacement field \(u\) and stiffness coefficient \(C\), so \({\cal L}=\rho_l u_t^2/2-Cu_x^2/2\). A constant shift \(u\to u+\varepsilon\) changes no strain and leaves the density invariant. The Euler–Lagrange equation becomes \(\partial_t(\rho_lu_t)+\partial_x(-Cu_x)=0\), a local conservation law. For an axial rod the density \(\rho_l u_t\) is ordinary longitudinal momentum per unit length, and the boundary term is axial traction with its outward sign. A uniform displacement symmetry survives a free translation of the whole rod but is broken if an end displacement is imposed relative to a clamp.

Integrate from \(x=0\) to \(L\). The total physical momentum changes as \(dP/dt=Cu_x(L)-Cu_x(0)\). A fixed end can exert a nonzero reaction and spoil conservation of the rod's momentum even when it does no power because its actual velocity vanishes. The distinction between a momentum source and an energy source is experimental: a rigid wall reflecting a pulse can accept net impulse while taking no mechanical energy. Including the support in the system restores the corresponding balance. Checking only the field density's invariance in the interior neglects boundary conditions, which are part of a physical symmetry.

The uniformity of material parameters also gives an invariance under translations of the material coordinate \(x\) in an infinite or periodic homogeneous domain. The associated density is proportional to \(-\rho_lu_tu_x\), often called wave pseudomomentum under this convention. Derive its local balance by multiplying \(\rho_lu_{tt}=Cu_{xx}\) by \(-u_x\) and applying the product rule. One obtains

\[\partial_t(-\rho_lu_tu_x)+\partial_x[Cu_x^2/2+\rho_lu_t^2/2]=0.\]

This conserved quantity differs from the ordinary momentum found from shifting \(u\) itself. A small-amplitude sinusoidal travelling wave can have zero mean material velocity over one period yet positive mean transport of this wave quantity.

For \(u=A\cos(kx-\omega t)\),

\[u_t=\omega A\sin(kx-\omega t),\]
\[u_x=-kA\sin(kx-\omega t).\]

The cycle-averaged wave density is \(\rho_l\omega kA^2/2\) while ordinary momentum density averages to zero. With \(\rho_l=0.20\,{\rm kg/m}\), \(A=1.0\,{\rm mm}\), \(k=5.0\,{\rm m^{-1}}\), and \(\omega=100\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), the average wave density is \(5.0\times10^{-5}\,{\rm kg/s}\). Its units are momentum per unit length, but equal dimensions do not make the two conserved charges interchangeable. To identify the charge, specify whether the symmetry shifts every displacement equally or shifts the material-coordinate pattern.

Test the evolution of a phase-space patch

Follow a family of initial conditions in a Hamiltonian flow. Compare changes in its shape with the conservation of its phase-space area.

Open this experiment online

Derive an identity without assuming the equations of motion

The usual on-shell proof substitutes the Euler–Lagrange equations just before a total derivative appears. Retaining the difference gives an equally useful off-shell identity. Define the residual \(R_i=d p_i/dt-\partial L/\partial q_i\). For a true variational symmetry and any differentiable trial path, repeat the calculation with \(\eta_i=\xi_i-\dot q_i\tau\). The result is

\[dQ/dt=\sum_i R_i\eta_i.\]

Thus the drift of a Noether charge evaluated on an approximate path is a particular projection of its dynamical residual. A path can have zero drift of one charge while violating equations in directions to which that charge is insensitive.

For a free particle in two dimensions, perfect conservation of \(p_x\) enforces zero residual acceleration in the \(x\) direction but says nothing about a spurious force in \(y\). Conversely tracking two independent translational charges checks both components. Energy conservation tests a different projection, \(dH/dt=\dot{\mathbf q}\cdot\mathbf R\) for a time-independent Lagrangian. An error force always perpendicular to velocity can leave the speed and energy unchanged while bending an originally straight trajectory. That is one reason an invariant energy graph cannot replace a force residual or a comparison of trajectories.

A numerical planar free particle follows a circle of constant speed even though the programmed potential vanishes. Its energy is conserved exactly. Which Noether quantities expose the problem most directly?

Work through the solution

The two laboratory translation momenta should each be constant. On the reported circle their vector changes direction continuously, so their residuals immediately reveal the missing transverse equation. A check of only the magnitude of momentum would also pass because that magnitude is fixed by energy. The angular momentum about the circle centre can remain constant too. Testing a set of charges selected for the actual symmetry and measured geometry identifies information lost by each scalar test on its own.

If the Lagrangian acquires a small explicitly time-dependent conservative potential \(V_1(t)q^2\), what quantity should replace a flat-energy test?

Work through the solution

The energy change on a physical path is \(\Delta H=\int\dot V_1(t)q(t)^2\,dt\). Positive or negative changes are allowed according to how the externally imposed stiffness is varied during the motion. Compare the measured change with this integral while keeping the time dependence inside the Hamiltonian itself. A flat-energy requirement would incorrectly penalise physical work by the control, whereas a residual after the input integral tests the dynamics.

Separate a discrete symmetry from a continuous one

A potential \(V(q)=a(q^2-b^2)^2\) is invariant under the discrete reflection \(q\mapsto-q\). Reflecting both position and velocity maps any solution into another solution with equal energy. There is no continuously tunable parameter that takes the identity smoothly to this isolated reflection while preserving the same one-dimensional potential at every intermediate step. No infinitesimal translation generator follows from this symmetry, and the momentum generally changes along either path. A pair of measured trajectories launched from mirrored initial data should match after reflection, but neither trajectory is required to have zero or constant momentum.

For \(a=1\) and \(b=1\) in consistent scaled units, release particles from \(q=0.50\) and \(-0.50\) at rest. The force is \(-4q(q^2-1)\), giving initial accelerations \(+1.50/m\) and \(-1.50/m\). The two accelerations are mirror images, not individually zero. A numerical method that accidentally introduces a constant bias force breaks the reflection comparison and shifts the two turning times in opposite senses. Energy can remain nearly conserved with respect to a slightly tilted effective potential, so trajectory pairing is a complementary diagnostic. Reflection symmetry imposes a relationship between solutions even when Noether's continuous-symmetry construction supplies no new conserved scalar.

Tutorial on identifying symmetries

A Lagrangian is replaced by \(L'=L+dG(q,t)/dt\). Does the numerical value of an appropriately derived Noether charge have to change?

Work through the solution

The new momentum is \(p'_i=p_i+G_{q_i}\), and \(H'=H-G_t\). Under the given symmetry the new boundary term is \(F'=F+\sum_iG_{q_i}\xi_i+G_t\tau\). Substitution into \(p'\xi-H'\tau-F'\) cancels the extra terms exactly. Charges can still be shifted by a physically irrelevant additive constant through the choice of boundary term. Comparing raw canonical momentum in two such conventions without also transforming \(F,H\) confuses intermediate variables with observations.

A central potential is changed into an axisymmetric potential \(V(r,\theta)\). Which angular-momentum tests remain exact?

Work through the solution

Rotation about the symmetry axis remains a continuous symmetry, so its angular-momentum component is conserved. Tilt rotations generally change the potential, so the transverse components need not stay constant. The torque may cause the angular momentum direction or magnitude to evolve while maintaining its axial component. A code test that requires all components to remain fixed would reject many legitimate axisymmetric trajectories.

A table exerts an unmodelled approximately constant horizontal drag \(0.010\,{\rm N}\) opposite the motion of the two pucks for \(0.40\,{\rm s}\). Estimate how the translation and boost checks change.

Work through the solution

Total momentum changes by \(-0.0040\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\). The derivative of \(Pt-MR_{\rm cm}\) is \(t\dot P\), since \(P=M\dot R_{\rm cm}\). Its change is therefore \(-0.010(0.40)^2/2=-0.00080\,{\rm kg\,m}\) under constant drag. The differing time dependence gives an additional way to distinguish a sustained external force from an initial position calibration error.

Follow the action of an infinitesimal generator on phase space.

David Tong treats continuous symmetries and Noether's theorem in Lagrangian dynamics.

Kepler orbits, the eccentricity vector and the loss of apsidal closure

Analytical mechanics from constraints to invariants

An orbit can conserve energy and angular momentum yet gradually rotate its periapsis. The inverse-square problem possesses one more conserved vector that fixes the direction of closest approach. Derive it, turn a single position and velocity measurement into a conic, and measure what a weak perturbation does to the inferred apsidal direction.

Reduce the two-body system without freezing either mass

Two bodies of masses \(m_1,m_2\) have positions \(\mathbf r_1,\mathbf r_2\). Define total mass \(M=m_1+m_2\), centre \(\mathbf R=(m_1\mathbf r_1+m_2\mathbf r_2)/M\), relative coordinate \(\mathbf r=\mathbf r_1-\mathbf r_2\), and reduced mass \(\mu=m_1m_2/M\). Substitution into the kinetic energy cancels the cross terms, giving \(M\dot{\mathbf R}^2/2+\mu\dot{\mathbf r}^2/2\). Newtonian gravitational potential is \(-\kappa/r\) with \(\kappa=Gm_1m_2\). The centre moves uniformly when isolated, while the relative motion obeys \(\mu\ddot{\mathbf r}=-\kappa\hat r/r^2\). Dividing by the reduced mass gives the acceleration parameter \(k=\kappa/\mu=GM\); replacing it by \(Gm_1\) is the test-particle approximation.

Use specific energy \(\epsilon=v^2/2-k/r\) and specific angular momentum \(\mathbf h=\mathbf r\times\mathbf v\), both per unit reduced mass. A central force makes \(\dot{\mathbf h}=0\), fixing the plane of nonradial motion. The area of the triangle swept in time \(dt\) is \(r^2d\varphi/2=h\,dt/2\), yielding the constant areal rate. The radial energy is \(\dot r^2/2+h^2/(2r^2)-k/r\). Its turning points determine the accessible radii, but energy and \(h\) alone cannot specify where the major axis lies in the chosen inertial plane.

Construct a vector whose derivative vanishes

The derivative of \(\mathbf v\times\mathbf h\) is \((-k\hat r/r^2)\times\mathbf h\). Use \(\mathbf h=r^2\dot\varphi\,\hat z\) with an oriented plane in which motion is positive. Since \(\hat r\times\hat z=-\hat\varphi\), the derivative becomes

\[k\dot\varphi\hat\varphi=k\,d\hat r/dt.\]

Consequently the eccentricity vector

\[\mathbf e=\frac{\mathbf v\times\mathbf h}{k}-\hat r\]

is constant. The dimensional Runge–Lenz vector is proportional to this choice: for relative momentum \(\mathbf p=\mu\mathbf v\),

\[\mathbf A=\mathbf p\times\mathbf L-\mu\kappa\hat r=\mu\kappa\mathbf e.\]

Every term in the specific expression is dimensionless. The step equating the derivative to \(k\,d\hat r/dt\) uses exactly the inverse-square radial force. General central forces retain areal velocity but lose this cancellation.

Dot \(\mathbf e\) with \(\hat r\). The triple product \((\mathbf v\times\mathbf h)\cdot\hat r=h^2/r\) gives

\[e\cos(\varphi-\varpi)=h^2/(kr)-1,\]

where \(\varpi\) is the fixed direction of \(\mathbf e\). Thus \(r=p/[1+e\cos(\varphi-\varpi)]\), \(p=h^2/k\), the conic in focus-centred polar coordinates. Squaring \(\mathbf e\) and eliminating \(v^2\) with the energy gives

\[e^2=1+2\epsilon h^2/k^2.\]

Bound ellipses have \(\epsilon<0,e<1\), parabolic escape has \(\epsilon=0,e=1\), and positive energy gives hyperbolas for nonzero angular momentum. Radial trajectories require separate care because their orbital plane and apsidal angle are degenerate.

Recover the orbit from a single state vector

In units chosen so \(k=1\), a measured relative position is \((2,0,0)\) and velocity is \((0.2,0.6,0)\). Compute \(h_z=1.2\), \(\epsilon=(0.04+0.36)/2-1/2=-0.30\), and \(p=1.44\). The cross product \(\mathbf v\times\mathbf h=(0.72,-0.24,0)\) gives \(\mathbf e=(-0.28,-0.24,0)\), with \(e=\sqrt{0.136}\approx0.369\). The direction of periapsis is about \(220.6^\circ\) from the positive \(x\) axis. At the measured point \(\dot r>0\), so the body is travelling from periapsis toward apoapsis. Dropping the radial component of velocity would rotate the eccentricity vector and change the inferred shape.

The semimajor axis is

\[a=-k/(2\epsilon)=1.667.\]

Periapsis and apoapsis radii \(a(1-e)\) and \(a(1+e)\) are approximately \(1.05\) and \(2.28\), consistent with the observed radius of two. The orbital period is \(2\pi\sqrt{a^3/k}=13.52\) time units. Derive this last result by dividing ellipse area \(\pi ab\) by the areal speed \(h/2\). Using \(b=a\sqrt{1-e^2}\) and \(h^2=ka(1-e^2)\) cancels eccentricity exactly. This shows why changing the initial radial velocity can change shape and orientation while the period depends only on binding energy.

Determine time along an ellipse and check limiting cases

Introduce the eccentric anomaly \(E\) by \(x=a(\cos E-e)\), \(y=a\sqrt{1-e^2}\sin E\) in axes centred on the focus, with \(x\) pointing toward periapsis. Then \(r=a(1-e\cos E)\). Compute \(x\,dy-y\,dx=ab(1-e\cos E)\,dE\) and equate it to \(h\,dt\). Integrating from periapsis gives

\[n(t-t_p)=E-e\sin E,\]

where \(n=\sqrt{k/a^3}\). This relation turns orbital geometry into an observation clock. The true anomaly is generally different from the linearly advancing mean anomaly because the body sweeps angle faster at small radius.

For \(e=0.6\) and the time \(t-t_p=T/4\), the mean anomaly is \(\pi/2\). Newton iteration for \(F(E)=E-0.6\sin E-\pi/2\) uses denominator \(1-0.6\cos E\), which stays positive. Starting near \(E=2.0\) yields \(E\simeq2.091\) radians and \(r\simeq1.30a\). The orbit has already moved beyond its semimajor radius at a quarter period. At \(e=0\), \(E\) equals the mean and true anomalies up to an arbitrary origin, and \(\mathbf e=0\) has no definable periapsis direction. Estimating a periapsis angle for a nearly circular noisy orbit is therefore poorly conditioned even when the radius and period are well measured.

Perturb the force and measure apsidal precession

For any central acceleration \(f(r)\hat r\), put \(u=1/r\). Since \(\dot\varphi=hu^2\), radial velocity is \(-h\,du/d\varphi\). Substituting into the radial acceleration gives Binet's equation \(u''+u=-f(1/u)/(h^2u^2)\), where primes denote angular derivatives. Choose \(f(r)=-k/r^2-\alpha/r^3\). Then \(u''+(1-\alpha/h^2)u=k/h^2\). If \(0<\alpha<h^2\), a radial oscillation requires angular advance \(2\pi/\sqrt{1-\alpha/h^2}\), greater than one full turn. Successive periapses advance by that value minus \(2\pi\), approximately \(\pi\alpha/h^2\) for a weak perturbation.

With \(\alpha/h^2=0.010\), the exact precession per radial cycle is approximately \(0.03165\,{\rm rad}=1.81^\circ\). Ordinary energy and angular momentum remain constant in this conservative central potential. The Kepler eccentricity vector evaluated from instantaneous position and velocity now changes. Track periapses using zeros of the radial velocity with positive radial acceleration, then compare their inertial angles. A numerical Kepler integrator can precess even for \(\alpha=0\); refine the time step and repeat the unperturbed control before assigning a small precession to the physical perturbation.

Infer an orbit using quantities that remain well conditioned

Suppose positions and velocities are measured with finite precision. A circular orbit has exact eccentricity vector zero, so even tiny perpendicular perturbations can swing the direction of that vector through large angles. Keep its Cartesian components when computing a fit near circularity. For an initially transverse circular state at radius \(r\) and speed \(v_c=\sqrt{k/r}\), perturb the velocity by radial \(\delta v_r\) and tangential \(\delta v_t\). In the local \((\hat r,\hat\varphi)\) basis the eccentricity components are \(rv_t^2/k-1\) and \(-rv_rv_t/k\). At first order they become \(2\delta v_t/v_c\) and \(-\delta v_r/v_c\). This linear response is well behaved even though taking an inverse tangent to quote periapsis longitude can become meaningless.

For a circular speed of \(10.0\,{\rm m/s}\), radial and tangential measurement errors each of \(0.010\,{\rm m/s}\) correspond to component uncertainties about \(0.0010\) and \(0.0020\). A reported eccentricity of \(0.0012\) is not accompanied by a precise preferred direction under those uncertainties. Repeated orbital samples reduce independent noise but do not remove a persistent tangential velocity bias. Measuring the radius and period independently supplies an additional constraint on \(k\) and the orbital energy. A model that regards the primary as fixed also introduces a systematic mass error when the secondary has non-negligible mass.

At the opposite extreme, a near-parabolic orbit can have a well-defined eccentricity direction but a badly conditioned energy estimate. Its energy is the small difference of \(v^2/2\) and \(k/r\). Since \(a=-k/(2\epsilon)\), a small energy uncertainty produces a very large uncertainty in semimajor axis and period. Report the measured state vector, eccentricity vector and energy uncertainty before interpreting an enormous best-fit period. If the energy uncertainty straddles zero, bounded and unbounded conics are both consistent with the measured data. Integrating a single best-fit ellipse for many revolutions does not resolve that ambiguity.

Calculate the velocity impulse to transfer between circular radii

The same two-body invariants reconstruct a transfer between circular trajectories without requiring a time-stepped simulation. Set a lower radius \(r_1\) and an upper radius \(r_2>r_1\). An ellipse tangent to both has periapsis \(r_1\), apoapsis \(r_2\), semimajor axis \(a_t=(r_1+r_2)/2\), and eccentricity \((r_2-r_1)/(r_2+r_1)\). At either apsis its speed follows from energy:

\[v_t(r)=\sqrt{k(2/r-1/a_t)}.\]

A first instantaneous tangential velocity change moves the particle from its lower circular trajectory to the transfer ellipse. A second tangential change at the opposite apsis matches the upper circular speed. These formulas describe impulses idealised as short compared with the orbital period.

Choose normalised \(k=r_1=1\), \(r_2=2\). Initial speed is one, transfer semimajor axis \(1.5\), and periapsis speed \(\sqrt{4/3}=1.1547\). The first change is \(0.1547\). At the upper radius the transfer speed is \(\sqrt{1/3}=0.57735\), while the circular speed there is

\[1/\sqrt2=0.70711.\]

The second change must also be forward, by \(0.12976\). The flight time between the two impulses is \(\pi\sqrt{1.5^3}=5.77\) time units, half the transfer period. Simply increasing the initial speed until the path reaches radius two does not make it circular upon arrival. Matching position and angular momentum at the final boundary requires the second change.

Verify the energy increment from an impulse independently. A change \(\Delta\mathbf v\) at fixed position gives

\[\Delta\epsilon=\mathbf v\cdot\Delta\mathbf v+|\Delta\mathbf v|^2/2.\]

At the lower radius our impulse raises \(\epsilon\) from \(-0.5\) to \(-1/3\), an increase of \(1/6\). Substitution of the rounded \(0.1547\) gives the same value. The same velocity increment generally changes energy more when applied where the initial speed is larger and aligned with the impulse. This is a statement about mechanical work at different initial states, not an exception to conservation between impulses.

A laboratory analogue uses a central potential engineered over a limited radius range. A small finite-duration tangential push changes radius as well as speed during the push. To check the impulse approximation compute the change in angular momentum as \(\int\mathbf r\times\mathbf a_{\rm applied}\,dt\) and energy as \(\int\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf a_{\rm applied}\,dt\). Compare these with the instantaneous predictions and shorten the pulse while holding its integrated velocity change fixed. If the return radius still drifts, map the force against radius to distinguish a finite-pulse error from an actual deviation from inverse-square attraction.

Continue the time law across the escape boundary

A hyperbolic Kepler orbit can be described without calling a divergent ellipse period a clock. Define a positive hyperbolic length \(a_h=k/(2\epsilon)\) for \(\epsilon>0\), and let \(e>1\). With periapsis on the positive \(x\) axis use \(x=a_h(e-\cosh H)\),

\[y=a_h\sqrt{e^2-1}\sinh H.\]

Then \(r=a_h(e\cosh H-1)\), and the swept-area relation gives

\[t-t_p=\sqrt{a_h^3/k}(e\sinh H-H).\]
