It does not by itself prove every trajectory between the resonances is chaotic. The relative phases, other harmonics and the actual time dependence determine the global structure.

For the same inertia \(0.050\,{\rm kg\,m^2}\), choose \(\Omega_1=8.0\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), \(\Omega_2=8.6\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), and forcing energies \(0.0020\) and \(0.00050\,{\rm J}\). The centres differ by \(0.030\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\); the individual half-widths are \(0.020\) and \(0.010\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\). Their nominal intervals just touch. Halving both forcing energies shrinks both widths by \(1/\sqrt2\), leaving a gap. Doubling both makes the estimated intervals overlap. The speed difference corresponds to a beat period \(2\pi/0.6=10.47\,{\rm s}\), another clock that must be resolved when recording nearby motion.

Transforming into the first rotating frame makes the first perturbation stationary and leaves the second with time dependence proportional to \(\cos[\phi-(\Omega_2-\Omega_1)t]\). A stroboscopic map sampled at the beat period is therefore more informative than a map with unsynchronised lab sampling. Prepare nearby initial conditions, compare their separation over multiple beat periods, and repeat with a refined symplectic time step and a modestly varied observation interval. Short-term stretching near an ordinary hyperbolic fixed point is possible even in a fully integrable pendulum. Evidence for widespread chaotic transport requires more than the growth of one separation during one saddle passage.

A student finds an apparent threshold by plotting whether the momentum ever crosses the midpoint between the two resonant centres. What additional evidence distinguishes irregular transport from a single large regular libration?

Work through the solution

A regular libration may cross that midpoint repeatedly inside one island, especially near its separatrix. Plot return pairs at a fixed beat phase, check for closed invariant curves or broader area filling, and track unwrapped relative phase to see whether it slips irregularly between the two patterns. Vary initial conditions and compare the statistical trend with the isolated-width estimate. Confirm that energy balance in the driven system includes explicit work by both patterns; a drift of the autonomous single-pattern energy is expected once the second drive is present.

Use phase reversal to isolate the resonant action transfer

For an initially weak sinusoidal drive, prepare two otherwise identical trajectories with initial relative phases differing by \(\pi\). Their leading changes of action during a short resonant pulse have opposite signs, since \(\dot I\) is proportional to \(\sin\phi_0\). Half the difference isolates that phase-sensitive term, whereas half the sum can reveal phase-independent heating or higher-order effects. With drive coefficient \(A=0.001\,{\rm J}\), duration \(2.0\,{\rm s}\) and initial phases \(\pm\pi/2\), short-pulse first-order predictions differ by \(0.0040\,{\rm J\,s}\). The test requires that detuning and nonlinear phase drift over the pulse remain small.

Perform the comparison at several durations. A linear growth of the early phase-sensitive difference is compatible with coherent transfer. Subsequent saturation, reversal or a slow oscillation can reflect the resonant pendulum dynamics once the action shift changes the phase. Friction can also saturate a response, but it produces mechanical energy loss and typically modifies the two phase preparations in different ways from conservative trapping. Measuring work from the applied torque and the full phase history adds the independent information needed to separate those explanations. A final amplitude alone gives neither the sign of the action transfer nor its phase dependence.

Tutorial on weak forcing

Far from resonance let a one-mode perturbation be \(A\cos(\theta-\Omega t)\) with \(A=0.001\,{\rm J}\) and detuning \(0.50\,{\rm s^{-1}}\). What first-order ripple scale should appear in the old action?

Work through the solution

The generating function has amplitude \(A/|\Delta|=0.002\,{\rm J\,s}\). For harmonic number one its angle derivative has the same amplitude, so the old action oscillates about the averaged action on that scale. At detuning \(0.01\,{\rm s^{-1}}\) the nominal ripple would be fifty times larger. Check its size against the action scale and the change of detuning across the ripple before trusting that continuation.

In the rotor example the forcing energy is multiplied by four. What happens to the resonant action, action width and small-amplitude libration period?

Work through the solution

The central action is set by \(H_0'=\Omega\) and stays \(0.40\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\). The half-width doubles to \(0.040\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\). The libration frequency doubles, so the period halves to \(15.7\,{\rm s}\). These predictions apply to the exact sinusoidal rotor model; other harmonics of a physical drive may also change as its amplitude increases.

A slowly swept drive seems to carry a phase-locked rotor beyond its original resonance band. Is this inconsistent with the static separatrix estimate?

Work through the solution

A varying \(\Omega(t)\) moves the resonance centre \({\cal I}\Omega(t)\). In coordinates measured from that moving centre the equation for detuning momentum has an additional torque-like term \(-{\cal I}\dot\Omega\). Locking can persist if the available restoring torque can track the ramp and the initial motion stays inside the evolving island. The static width describes a fixed-frequency section. Comparing a captured trajectory to its initial centre after the sweep tests the wrong phase portrait.

Compare a resonance driven by a varying stiffness with a rotating additive potential.
